Walk the array element by element, or try every possible answer value and test feasibility. Correct but ignores the monotonic structure that lets you skip half the space each comparison.

Template A · exact / boundary search in a sorted array O(log n)

int lowerBound(int[] a, int target){       // first index with a[i] >= target
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length;              // [lo, hi) half-open
    while (lo < hi){
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo)/2;
        if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
        else hi = mid;
    }
    return lo;                              // insertion point; == length if none
}
int lowerBound(vector<int>& a, int target){       // first index with a[i] >= target
    int lo = 0, hi = a.size();              // [lo, hi) half-open
    while (lo < hi){
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo)/2;
        if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
        else hi = mid;
    }
    return lo;                              // insertion point; == length if none
}
def lower_bound(a, target):       # first index with a[i] >= target
    lo, hi = 0, len(a)            # [lo, hi) half-open
    while lo < hi:
        mid = lo + (hi - lo) // 2
        if a[mid] < target:
            lo = mid + 1
        else:
            hi = mid
    return lo                     # insertion point; == length if none
function lowerBound(a: number[], target: number): number {       // first index with a[i] >= target
    let lo = 0, hi = a.length;              // [lo, hi) half-open
    while (lo < hi) {
        const mid = lo + Math.floor((hi - lo) / 2);
        if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
        else hi = mid;
    }
    return lo;                              // insertion point; == length if none
}

Template B · binary search on the answer (feasibility predicate)

// smallest speed so Koko finishes all piles within h hours
int minEatingSpeed(int[] piles, int h){
    int lo = 1, hi = 0;
    for (int p : piles) hi = Math.max(hi, p);
    while (lo < hi){
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo)/2;
        if (feasible(piles, mid, h)) hi = mid;   // can do it ⇒ try slower
        else lo = mid + 1;                       // too slow ⇒ speed up
    }
    return lo;                                   // smallest feasible speed
}
boolean feasible(int[] piles, int speed, int h){
    long hours = 0;
    for (int p : piles) hours += (p + speed - 1) / speed;  // ceil
    return hours <= h;
}
// smallest speed so Koko finishes all piles within h hours
int minEatingSpeed(vector<int>& piles, int h){
    int lo = 1, hi = 0;
    for (int p : piles) hi = max(hi, p);
    while (lo < hi){
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo)/2;
        if (feasible(piles, mid, h)) hi = mid;   // can do it ⇒ try slower
        else lo = mid + 1;                       // too slow ⇒ speed up
    }
    return lo;                                   // smallest feasible speed
}
bool feasible(vector<int>& piles, int speed, int h){
    long long hours = 0;
    for (int p : piles) hours += (p + speed - 1) / speed;  // ceil
    return hours <= h;
}
def min_eating_speed(piles, h):
    # smallest speed so Koko finishes all piles within h hours
    lo, hi = 1, max(piles)
    while lo < hi:
        mid = lo + (hi - lo) // 2
        if feasible(piles, mid, h):
            hi = mid                              # can do it ⇒ try slower
        else:
            lo = mid + 1                          # too slow ⇒ speed up
    return lo                                     # smallest feasible speed

def feasible(piles, speed, h):
    hours = 0
    for p in piles:
        hours += (p + speed - 1) // speed         # ceil
    return hours <= h
// smallest speed so Koko finishes all piles within h hours
function minEatingSpeed(piles: number[], h: number): number {
    let lo = 1, hi = 0;
    for (const p of piles) hi = Math.max(hi, p);
    while (lo < hi) {
        const mid = lo + Math.floor((hi - lo) / 2);
        if (feasible(piles, mid, h)) hi = mid;   // can do it ⇒ try slower
        else lo = mid + 1;                       // too slow ⇒ speed up
    }
    return lo;                                   // smallest feasible speed
}
function feasible(piles: number[], speed: number, h: number): boolean {
    let hours = 0;
    for (const p of piles) hours += Math.ceil(p / speed);   // ceil
    return hours <= h;
}

Worked example — binary search on the answer, traced

piles=[3,6,7,11], h=8. Feasible speeds form the monotonic pattern F F F T T …; we want the first T. Range [1,11]:

lohimidhours at midfeasible?move
11161+1+2+2=6yeshi=6
1631+2+3+4=10nolo=4
4651+2+2+3=8yeshi=5
4541+2+2+3=8yeshi=4
44lo==hi → answer 4

Answer 4. Note we never enumerate speeds — 4 probes cover the whole range 1..11.

Pitfalls: off-by-one is the whole game. Pin down whether your interval is [lo,hi] (loop while lo<=hi) or [lo,hi) (loop while lo<hi), and use lo+(hi-lo)/2 to dodge integer overflow. For "largest feasible", mirror the moves.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Binary search on the answer requires a monotonic feasible(x) predicate: if speed x works, every larger speed also works (and every smaller speed that fails, all smaller speeds also fail). That monotonicity turns "find the minimum feasible x" into ordinary binary search over the answer space [lo, hi], converging by halving regardless of how expensive feasible(x) is to evaluate.

When to reach for it. "Minimum/maximum X such that condition holds", "smallest capacity/speed/threshold to finish within a limit" — Koko Eating Bananas, Ship Packages Within D Days, split array largest sum. Signal: the array itself isn't sorted for a value search; instead you're searching an answer range against a checkable predicate.

Complexity. O(n log(range)) — log(range) binary-search steps, each paying O(n) to evaluate feasible. Space O(1) beyond input.

Pitfalls & variations. Using mid = (lo+hi)//2 with hi = mid on the feasible branch but forgetting lo = mid + 1 on the infeasible branch causes an infinite loop. Off-by-one on whether hi starts at max(piles) or sum(piles) changes correctness for edge cases like a single giant pile. Variants: search where the predicate is "cost ≤ budget" instead of "time ≤ limit" — the shape is identical, only the checker changes.

Trace input: piles = [3,6,7,11], h = 8 hours — find the minimum eating speed k so sum(ceil(pile/k)) ≤ h.

Steplohimidhours(mid)feasible?Action
0111———search space = candidate speeds 1..max(piles)
111161+1+2+2=6yes (6≤8)hi = 6
21631+2+3+4=10no (10>8)lo = 4
34651+2+2+3=8yes (8≤8)hi = 5
44541+2+2+3=8yes (8≤8)hi = 4 → lo==hi, stop

Result: minimum speed k = 4. The trace shows the window [lo,hi] shrinking from 11 candidates to 1 in four steps (log2(11)≈3.5), and that speed 3 (checked implicitly, would take 10h) fails while 4 exactly meets the 8h budget.

9 · Heap / Priority Queue

Flowchart leaves: kth smallest/largest merge k sorted lists

The idea

A binary heap gives O(log n) insert and O(log n) extract-min/max with O(1) peek. Reach for it whenever you repeatedly need "the current best/worst among a changing set": top-k, kth element, streaming medians, merging sorted sequences, or always expanding the cheapest option (Dijkstra, Prim).

Brute force — sort everything O(n log n)

Sort the whole array and index the kth element. Fine once, but wasteful for streams or when k ≪ n; a size-k heap does it in O(n log k) and handles data that doesn't fit in memory.

Template A · kth largest with a size-k min-heap O(n log k)

int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k){
    PriorityQueue<Integer> min = new PriorityQueue<>();  // keeps k largest
    for (int x : nums){
        min.offer(x);
        if (min.size() > k) min.poll();     // evict the smallest
    }
    return min.peek();                       // kth largest overall
}
int findKthLargest(vector<int>& nums, int k){
    priority_queue<int, vector<int>, greater<int>> minHeap;  // keeps k largest
    for (int x : nums){
        minHeap.push(x);
        if (minHeap.size() > (size_t)k) minHeap.pop();     // evict the smallest
    }
    return minHeap.top();                       // kth largest overall
}
def find_kth_largest(nums, k):
    heap = []                                # keeps k largest (min-heap)
    for x in nums:
        heapq.heappush(heap, x)
        if len(heap) > k:
            heapq.heappop(heap)              # evict the smallest
    return heap[0]                           # kth largest overall
function findKthLargest(nums: number[], k: number): number {
    const min: number[] = [];               // keeps k largest
    for (const x of nums) {
        min.push(x);
        min.sort((a, b) => a - b);
        if (min.length > k) min.shift();     // evict the smallest
    }
    return min[0];                           // kth largest overall
}

Template B · merge k sorted lists O(N log k)

ListNode mergeKLists(ListNode[] lists){
    PriorityQueue<ListNode> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a,b)->a.val-b.val);
    for (ListNode l : lists) if (l!=null) pq.offer(l);
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    while (!pq.isEmpty()){
        ListNode n = pq.poll();
        tail.next = n; tail = n;
        if (n.next != null) pq.offer(n.next);
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
ListNode* mergeKLists(vector<ListNode*>& lists){
    auto cmp = [](ListNode* a, ListNode* b){ return a->val > b->val; };
    priority_queue<ListNode*, vector<ListNode*>, decltype(cmp)> pq(cmp);
    for (ListNode* l : lists) if (l != nullptr) pq.push(l);
    ListNode dummy(0), *tail = &dummy;
    while (!pq.empty()){
        ListNode* n = pq.top(); pq.pop();
        tail->next = n; tail = n;
        if (n->next != nullptr) pq.push(n->next);
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
def merge_k_lists(lists):
    heap = []
    for i, l in enumerate(lists):
        if l is not None:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (l.val, i, l))     # tie-break with index (nodes aren't comparable)
    dummy = ListNode(0)
    tail = dummy
    counter = len(heap)
    while heap:
        val, idx, n = heapq.heappop(heap)
        tail.next = n
        tail = n
        if n.next is not None:
            counter += 1
            heapq.heappush(heap, (n.next.val, counter, n.next))
    return dummy.next
function mergeKLists(lists: (ListNode | null)[]): ListNode | null {
    const pq: ListNode[] = [];
    for (const l of lists) if (l !== null) pq.push(l);
    pq.sort((a, b) => a.val - b.val);
    const dummy = new ListNode(0);
    let tail = dummy;
    while (pq.length) {
        const n = pq.shift()!;
        tail.next = n; tail = n;
        if (n.next !== null) {
            pq.push(n.next);
            pq.sort((a, b) => a.val - b.val);
        }
    }
    return dummy.next;
}

Template C · two heaps for a running median

PriorityQueue<Integer> lo = new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.reverseOrder()); // max-heap, lower half
PriorityQueue<Integer> hi = new PriorityQueue<>();                          // min-heap, upper half
void add(int x){
    lo.offer(x); hi.offer(lo.poll());              // funnel through to keep order
    if (hi.size() > lo.size()) lo.offer(hi.poll()); // rebalance sizes
}
double median(){ return lo.size()>hi.size() ? lo.peek() : (lo.peek()+hi.peek())/2.0; }
priority_queue<int> lo;                              // max-heap, lower half
priority_queue<int, vector<int>, greater<int>> hi;   // min-heap, upper half
void add(int x){
    lo.push(x); hi.push(lo.top()); lo.pop();          // funnel through to keep order
    if (hi.size() > lo.size()){ lo.push(hi.top()); hi.pop(); } // rebalance sizes
}
double median(){ return lo.size()>hi.size() ? lo.top() : (lo.top()+hi.top())/2.0; }
lo = []   # max-heap, lower half (store negated values)
hi = []   # min-heap, upper half

def add(x):
    heapq.heappush(lo, -x)
    heapq.heappush(hi, -heapq.heappop(lo))            # funnel through to keep order
    if len(hi) > len(lo):
        heapq.heappush(lo, -heapq.heappop(hi))        # rebalance sizes

def median():
    if len(lo) > len(hi):
        return -lo[0]
    return (-lo[0] + hi[0]) / 2.0
const lo: number[] = [];   // max-heap, lower half
const hi: number[] = [];   // min-heap, upper half

function add(x: number): void {
    lo.push(x); lo.sort((a, b) => b - a);
    hi.push(lo.shift()!); hi.sort((a, b) => a - b);   // funnel through to keep order
    if (hi.length > lo.length) {
        lo.push(hi.shift()!);
        lo.sort((a, b) => b - a);                      // rebalance sizes
    }
}

function median(): number {
    return lo.length > hi.length ? lo[0] : (lo[0] + hi[0]) / 2.0;
}

Worked example — 2nd largest of [3,2,1,5,6,4], k=2

ReadHeap (min on top)Action
3[3]size 1
2[2,3]size 2
1[2,3]add 1 → [1,2,3], size 3 > 2 → poll 1
5[3,5]add 5 → poll 2
6[5,6]add 6 → poll 3
4[5,6]add 4 → poll 4

Heap holds the two largest {5,6}; its min (top) = 5 is the 2nd largest.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A size-k min-heap maintains the invariant "the heap always holds the k largest elements seen so far, with the smallest of those k sitting at the top". Every new element either grows the heap (while under capacity) or gets compared against the current k-th largest (the heap's min) and only survives if it beats it — so after processing the whole stream, the heap's minimum is exactly the k-th largest overall.

When to reach for it. "Kth largest/smallest", "top-k frequent", "k closest points", or any streaming/online scenario where you can't sort the whole input up front (or want better than O(n log n)). Signal words: "kth", "top k", "k most/least".

Complexity. O(n log k) time — each of n elements does at most one O(log k) push/pop; O(k) space for the heap (vs. O(n log n)/O(n) for full sort).

Pitfalls & variations. Using a max-heap of the full input instead of a min-heap capped at size k throws away the whole point of the technique (back to O(n log n)). Forgetting to pop back down to size k after a push leaves the heap unbounded. Variants: "kth smallest" flips to a max-heap of size k; "k closest points" compares by distance instead of raw value; quickselect is the O(n) average-case alternative when you don't need a running/streaming answer.

Trace input: nums = [7,10,4,3,20,15], k = 2 — find the 2nd largest element using a min-heap capped at size k.

StepElementHeap beforeActionHeap afterSize
17[]push (under capacity)[7]1
210[7]push (now at capacity)[7,10]2
34[7,10]push then pop-min(4)[7,10]2
43[7,10]push then pop-min(3)[7,10]2
520[7,10]push then pop-min(7)[10,20]2
615[10,20]push then pop-min(10)[15,20]2

Result: heap top (min) = 15 = the 2nd largest (full sorted order: 20,15,10,7,4,3). Elements 4 and 3 never survive a single round in the heap because they're immediately the new minimum — the trace shows the heap self-correcting after every push.

10 · Two Pointers

Flowchart leaves: linked-list fast/slow array partitioning two-sequence merge find indices O(1) memory

The idea

Two indices move through the data with a rule, replacing a nested loop with a single linear pass and O(1) memory. Three sub-patterns: opposite ends converging (sorted two-sum, container with most water, palindrome), fast/slow on a linked list (cycle, middle, nth-from-end), and one pointer per sequence (merge, count pairs across two sorted arrays).

Brute force — nested loops O(n²)

Check every pair (i,j). The monotonic structure (usually "the array is sorted") means when a sum is too big you never need a larger j — so one pointer only ever moves inward, collapsing the double loop.

Template A · opposite ends — two-sum on a sorted array O(n)

int[] twoSum(int[] a, int target){        // a sorted ascending
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi){
        int sum = a[lo] + a[hi];
        if (sum == target) return new int[]{lo, hi};
        else if (sum < target) lo++;      // need larger → move left pointer up
        else hi--;                        // need smaller → move right pointer down
    }
    return new int[]{-1,-1};
}
vector<int> twoSum(vector<int>& a, int target){        // a sorted ascending
    int lo = 0, hi = a.size() - 1;
    while (lo < hi){
        int sum = a[lo] + a[hi];
        if (sum == target) return {lo, hi};
        else if (sum < target) lo++;      // need larger → move left pointer up
        else hi--;                        // need smaller → move right pointer down
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}
def two_sum(a, target):        # a sorted ascending
    lo, hi = 0, len(a) - 1
    while lo < hi:
        s = a[lo] + a[hi]
        if s == target:
            return [lo, hi]
        elif s < target:
            lo += 1             # need larger → move left pointer up
        else:
            hi -= 1             # need smaller → move right pointer down
    return [-1, -1]
function twoSum(a: number[], target: number): number[] {        // a sorted ascending
    let lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        const sum = a[lo] + a[hi];
        if (sum === target) return [lo, hi];
        else if (sum < target) lo++;      // need larger → move left pointer up
        else hi--;                        // need smaller → move right pointer down
    }
    return [-1, -1];
}

Template B · fast/slow — cycle detection & middle

boolean hasCycle(ListNode head){
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null){
        slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next;   // 1x vs 2x speed
        if (slow == fast) return true;             // they meet inside a loop
    }
    return false;
}
bool hasCycle(ListNode* head){
    ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head;
    while (fast != nullptr && fast->next != nullptr){
        slow = slow->next; fast = fast->next->next;   // 1x vs 2x speed
        if (slow == fast) return true;                // they meet inside a loop
    }
    return false;
}
def has_cycle(head):
    slow, fast = head, head
    while fast is not None and fast.next is not None:
        slow = slow.next
        fast = fast.next.next                # 1x vs 2x speed
        if slow == fast:
            return True                       # they meet inside a loop
    return False
function hasCycle(head: ListNode | null): boolean {
    let slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast !== null && fast.next !== null) {
        slow = slow!.next; fast = fast.next.next;   // 1x vs 2x speed
        if (slow === fast) return true;              // they meet inside a loop
    }
    return false;
}

Template C · in-place partition — Dutch national flag (sort 0/1/2)

void sortColors(int[] a){
    int low = 0, mid = 0, high = a.length - 1;
    while (mid <= high){
        if (a[mid]==0) swap(a, low++, mid++);
        else if (a[mid]==1) mid++;
        else swap(a, mid, high--);         // 2 → send to the back
    }
}
void sortColors(vector<int>& a){
    int low = 0, mid = 0, high = a.size() - 1;
    while (mid <= high){
        if (a[mid]==0) swap(a[low++], a[mid++]);
        else if (a[mid]==1) mid++;
        else swap(a[mid], a[high--]);         // 2 → send to the back
    }
}
def sort_colors(a):
    low, mid, high = 0, 0, len(a) - 1
    while mid <= high:
        if a[mid] == 0:
            a[low], a[mid] = a[mid], a[low]
            low += 1
            mid += 1
        elif a[mid] == 1:
            mid += 1
        else:
            a[mid], a[high] = a[high], a[mid]        # 2 → send to the back
            high -= 1
function sortColors(a: number[]): void {
    let low = 0, mid = 0, high = a.length - 1;
    while (mid <= high) {
        if (a[mid] === 0) {
            [a[low], a[mid]] = [a[mid], a[low]];
            low++; mid++;
        } else if (a[mid] === 1) {
            mid++;
        } else {
            [a[mid], a[high]] = [a[high], a[mid]];    // 2 → send to the back
            high--;
        }
    }
}

Template D · one pointer per sorted array — count pairs ≤ target

long countPairs(int[] a, int[] b, int target){    // both sorted
    long count = 0; int j = b.length - 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++){
        while (j >= 0 && a[i] + b[j] > target) j--;
        count += (j + 1);                          // b[0..j] all pair with a[i]
    }
    return count;
}
long long countPairs(vector<int>& a, vector<int>& b, int target){    // both sorted
    long long count = 0; int j = b.size() - 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)a.size(); i++){
        while (j >= 0 && a[i] + b[j] > target) j--;
        count += (j + 1);                          // b[0..j] all pair with a[i]
    }
    return count;
}
def count_pairs(a, b, target):    # both sorted
    count = 0
    j = len(b) - 1
    for i in range(len(a)):
        while j >= 0 and a[i] + b[j] > target:
            j -= 1
        count += (j + 1)                          # b[0..j] all pair with a[i]
    return count
function countPairs(a: number[], b: number[], target: number): number {    // both sorted
    let count = 0;
    let j = b.length - 1;
    for (let i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
        while (j >= 0 && a[i] + b[j] > target) j--;
        count += (j + 1);                          // b[0..j] all pair with a[i]
    }
    return count;
}

Worked example — sorted two-sum, target 9 on [2,7,11,15]

lohia[lo]+a[hi]vs 9move
032+15=17>hi→2
022+11=13>hi→1
012+7=9=return [0,1]

Three steps instead of the six pairs a double loop would test. Each pointer only moves one direction, so total work is linear.

Finding the cycle's entry: after fast/slow meet, reset one pointer to the head and advance both at 1x — they meet at the loop start (Floyd's second phase).

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. On a sorted array, two pointers l and r maintain the invariant "every pair already skipped past could not possibly sum to target, given the array's order". If nums[l] + nums[r] > target, decreasing r is the only way to shrink the sum (since all values between the old and new r are ≥ the new nums[r], pairing any of them with l would still overshoot); symmetric logic justifies advancing l when the sum is too small. Each move provably eliminates a whole row/column of the pair matrix, never a false eliminate.

When to reach for it. "Sorted array, find a pair/triplet summing to X", "container with most water", "remove duplicates in place", palindrome checks — anything where the array is sorted (or can be) and you're relating a value at one end to a value at the other.

Complexity. O(n) time — l and r together traverse the array at most once; O(1) extra space, no hash map needed (unlike the unsorted two-sum).

Pitfalls & variations. Applying this technique on an unsorted array without sorting first breaks the elimination argument entirely. Using l < r vs. l ≤ r as the loop bound matters when the array has an odd middle element that could pair with itself only if duplicates are allowed. Variants: 3Sum (fix one element, two-pointer the rest), container-with-most-water (move the pointer at the shorter wall, not the smaller value).

Trace input: nums = [-6,-4,-1,0,2,3,5,7,9,11] (sorted), target = 9 — find a pair of indices whose values sum to target.

Steplrnums[l]nums[r]sumAction
109-61155 < 9 → l++
219-41177 < 9 → l++
329-1111010 > 9 → r--
428-1988 < 9 → l++
5380999 == 9 → found

Result: pair found at indices (3,8), values (0,9). The trace shows l advancing three separate times and r retreating once — every move is justified purely by whether the current sum overshot or undershot 9, never by re-checking a skipped pair.

11 · Sliding Window

Flowchart leaf: maintain a valid window (subarray/substring)

The idea

For "longest/shortest/count of contiguous subarray or substring with property P", keep a window [left, right]. Grow right to include new elements; when the window violates P, shrink from left until valid again. Each index enters and leaves at most once → O(n). Works when extending never fixes a violation (i.e., the property is monotonic in window size).

The universal shape

int left = 0;
for (int right = 0; right < n; right++){
    add(a[right]);                 // extend window
    while (invalid()){ remove(a[left]); left++; }   // shrink to restore validity
    best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);        // window is valid here
}
int left = 0;
for (int right = 0; right < n; right++){
    add(a[right]);                 // extend window
    while (invalid()){ remove(a[left]); left++; }   // shrink to restore validity
    best = max(best, right - left + 1);              // window is valid here
}
left = 0
for right in range(n):
    add(a[right])                                    # extend window
    while invalid():
        remove(a[left])
        left += 1                                    # shrink to restore validity
    best = max(best, right - left + 1)                # window is valid here
let left = 0;
for (let right = 0; right < n; right++) {
    add(a[right]);                 // extend window
    while (invalid()) { remove(a[left]); left++; }   // shrink to restore validity
    best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);          // window is valid here
}

Brute force — every subarray O(n²) or O(n³)

Enumerate all O(n²) start/end pairs and re-scan each to test P. The window reuses work between adjacent ranges instead of recomputing from scratch.

Template A · variable window — longest substring without repeats O(n)

int lengthOfLongestSubstring(String s){
    int[] last = new int[128]; Arrays.fill(last, -1);
    int left = 0, best = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++){
        char c = s.charAt(right);
        if (last[c] >= left) left = last[c] + 1;   // jump past the earlier copy
        last[c] = right;
        best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}
int lengthOfLongestSubstring(string s){
    vector<int> last(128, -1);
    int left = 0, best = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < (int)s.length(); right++){
        char c = s[right];
        if (last[c] >= left) left = last[c] + 1;   // jump past the earlier copy
        last[c] = right;
        best = max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}
def length_of_longest_substring(s):
    last = [-1] * 128
    left = best = 0
    for right, c in enumerate(s):
        if last[ord(c)] >= left:
            left = last[ord(c)] + 1        # jump past the earlier copy
        last[ord(c)] = right
        best = max(best, right - left + 1)
    return best
function lengthOfLongestSubstring(s: string): number {
    const last = new Array(128).fill(-1);
    let left = 0, best = 0;
    for (let right = 0; right < s.length; right++) {
        const c = s.charCodeAt(right);
        if (last[c] >= left) left = last[c] + 1;   // jump past the earlier copy
        last[c] = right;
        best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}

Template B · fixed window — max sum of size k

int maxSum(int[] a, int k){
    int sum = 0, best;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) sum += a[i];
    best = sum;
    for (int i = k; i < a.length; i++){
        sum += a[i] - a[i-k];        // slide: add new, drop oldest
        best = Math.max(best, sum);
    }
    return best;
}
int maxSum(vector<int>& a, int k){
    int sum = 0, best;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) sum += a[i];
    best = sum;
    for (int i = k; i < (int)a.size(); i++){
        sum += a[i] - a[i-k];        // slide: add new, drop oldest
        best = max(best, sum);
    }
    return best;
}
def max_sum(a, k):
    total = sum(a[:k])
    best = total
    for i in range(k, len(a)):
        total += a[i] - a[i - k]        # slide: add new, drop oldest
        best = max(best, total)
    return best
function maxSum(a: number[], k: number): number {
    let sum = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < k; i++) sum += a[i];
    let best = sum;
    for (let i = k; i < a.length; i++) {
        sum += a[i] - a[i - k];        // slide: add new, drop oldest
        best = Math.max(best, sum);
    }
    return best;
}

Template C · counting windows — "at most K" trick for "exactly K"

// # subarrays with exactly K distinct = atMost(K) - atMost(K-1)
int atMost(int[] a, int k){
    Map<Integer,Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
    int left = 0, count = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < a.length; right++){
        freq.merge(a[right], 1, Integer::sum);
        while (freq.size() > k){
            int v = a[left++];
            if (freq.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) freq.remove(v);
        }
        count += right - left + 1;   // # valid windows ending at right
    }
    return count;
}
// # subarrays with exactly K distinct = atMost(K) - atMost(K-1)
int atMost(vector<int>& a, int k){
    unordered_map<int,int> freq;
    int left = 0, count = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < (int)a.size(); right++){
        freq[a[right]]++;
        while ((int)freq.size() > k){
            int v = a[left++];
            if (--freq[v] == 0) freq.erase(v);
        }
        count += right - left + 1;   // # valid windows ending at right
    }
    return count;
}
# # subarrays with exactly K distinct = at_most(K) - at_most(K-1)
def at_most(a, k):
    freq = {}
    left = count = 0
    for right in range(len(a)):
        freq[a[right]] = freq.get(a[right], 0) + 1
        while len(freq) > k:
            v = a[left]
            left += 1
            freq[v] -= 1
            if freq[v] == 0:
                del freq[v]
        count += right - left + 1   # # valid windows ending at right
    return count
// # subarrays with exactly K distinct = atMost(K) - atMost(K-1)
function atMost(a: number[], k: number): number {
    const freq = new Map<number, number>();
    let left = 0, count = 0;
    for (let right = 0; right < a.length; right++) {
        freq.set(a[right], (freq.get(a[right]) ?? 0) + 1);
        while (freq.size > k) {
            const v = a[left++];
            const c = freq.get(v)! - 1;
            if (c === 0) freq.delete(v); else freq.set(v, c);
        }
        count += right - left + 1;   // # valid windows ending at right
    }
    return count;
}

Worked example — longest no-repeat substring of "abcabcbb"

rightcharleft before → afterwindowbest
0a0→0a1
1b0→0ab2
2c0→0abc3
3a0→1bca3
4b1→2cab3
5c2→3abc3
6b3→5cb3
7b5→7b3

Answer 3. left only ever moves right, so both pointers together do ≤ 2n steps.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The window [left, right] maintains the invariant "no duplicate characters inside the window." When extending right introduces a duplicate of some character c, the window's left edge only needs to jump to lastSeen[c] + 1 — everything before that still satisfies the invariant, and skipping character-by-character would revisit states already proven invalid. A hash map of last-seen indices turns each shrink into O(1) instead of a linear scan.

When to reach for it. Look for "contiguous subarray/substring" combined with a constraint that can only get worse as the window grows (distinct chars, sum ≤ target, at most k distinct, etc.) — i.e. the constraint is monotonic in window size, so once it breaks, only shrinking from the left can fix it.

Complexity. O(n) time — left and right each move forward at most n times total; O(min(n, Σ)) space for the last-seen map/set, where Σ is the alphabet size.

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to move left directly to lastSeen[c] + 1 (instead of one step at a time) turns O(n) into O(n²). Confusing "at most k distinct" (shrink while count > k) with "no repeats" (shrink while duplicate exists) is a common mix-up. Variants: fixed-size window (no left recompute, just slide), and "shrink-while-valid" windows that maximize rather than minimize.

Trace input: s = "abcabcbb" — find the length of the longest substring with no repeated characters.

rightcharlastSeen beforeleftwindowlenbest
0a-0[0,0]11
1b-0[0,1]22
2c-0[0,2]33
3a0 (>=left)1[1,3]33
4b1 (>=left)2[2,4]33
5c2 (>=left)3[3,5]33
6b4 (>=left)5[5,6]23
7b6 (>=left)7[7,7]13

Result: Longest length is 3 (e.g. "abc"). The trace shows the window never shrinks more than one step past the offending duplicate, and left is monotonic non-decreasing throughout — the source of the O(n) bound.

12 · Monotonic Stack

Flowchart leaves: nearest greater/smaller (subarray) nearest bounds (max/min)

The idea

To find, for each element, its nearest greater (or smaller) neighbour, keep a stack whose values stay sorted. When a new element breaks the order, pop the violators — the new element is precisely their "next greater/smaller". Each index is pushed and popped once → O(n). Powers next-greater-element, daily temperatures, largest rectangle, trapping rain water, and stock spans.

Brute force — scan forward for each element O(n²)

For every i, walk right until you find a larger value. The stack remembers unresolved candidates so each is settled in O(1) amortized instead of re-scanned.

Template A · next greater element (days until warmer) O(n)

int[] dailyTemperatures(int[] t){
    int n = t.length; int[] res = new int[n];
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();   // indices, temps decreasing down the stack
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++){
        while (!st.isEmpty() && t[i] > t[st.peek()]){
            int j = st.pop();
            res[j] = i - j;                   // i is j's next-warmer day
        }
        st.push(i);
    }
    return res;                               // unresolved stay 0
}
vector<int> dailyTemperatures(vector<int>& t){
    int n = t.size(); vector<int> res(n, 0);
    deque<int> st;   // indices, temps decreasing down the stack
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++){
        while (!st.empty() && t[i] > t[st.back()]){
            int j = st.back(); st.pop_back();
            res[j] = i - j;                   // i is j's next-warmer day
        }
        st.push_back(i);
    }
    return res;                               // unresolved stay 0
}
def daily_temperatures(t):
    n = len(t)
    res = [0] * n
    st = []                                    # indices, temps decreasing down the stack
    for i in range(n):
        while st and t[i] > t[st[-1]]:
            j = st.pop()
            res[j] = i - j                     # i is j's next-warmer day
        st.append(i)
    return res                                 # unresolved stay 0
function dailyTemperatures(t: number[]): number[] {
    const n = t.length;
    const res = new Array(n).fill(0);
    const st: number[] = [];   // indices, temps decreasing down the stack
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (st.length && t[i] > t[st[st.length - 1]]) {
            const j = st.pop()!;
            res[j] = i - j;                   // i is j's next-warmer day
        }
        st.push(i);
    }
    return res;                               // unresolved stay 0
}

Template B · largest rectangle in a histogram O(n)

int largestRectangleArea(int[] h){
    int n = h.length, best = 0;
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++){
        int cur = (i == n) ? 0 : h[i];          // sentinel flushes the stack
        while (!st.isEmpty() && h[st.peek()] >= cur){
            int height = h[st.pop()];
            int width  = st.isEmpty() ? i : i - st.peek() - 1;
            best = Math.max(best, height * width);
        }
        st.push(i);
    }
    return best;
}
int largestRectangleArea(vector<int>& h){
    int n = h.size(), best = 0;
    deque<int> st;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++){
        int cur = (i == n) ? 0 : h[i];          // sentinel flushes the stack
        while (!st.empty() && h[st.back()] >= cur){
            int height = h[st.back()]; st.pop_back();
            int width  = st.empty() ? i : i - st.back() - 1;
            best = max(best, height * width);
        }
        st.push_back(i);
    }
    return best;
}
def largest_rectangle_area(h):
    n = len(h)
    best = 0
    st = []
    for i in range(n + 1):
        cur = 0 if i == n else h[i]             # sentinel flushes the stack
        while st and h[st[-1]] >= cur:
            height = h[st.pop()]
            width = i if not st else i - st[-1] - 1
            best = max(best, height * width)
        st.append(i)
    return best
function largestRectangleArea(h: number[]): number {
    const n = h.length;
    let best = 0;
    const st: number[] = [];
    for (let i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
        const cur = (i === n) ? 0 : h[i];          // sentinel flushes the stack
        while (st.length && h[st[st.length - 1]] >= cur) {
            const height = h[st.pop()!];
            const width = st.length === 0 ? i : i - st[st.length - 1] - 1;
            best = Math.max(best, height * width);
        }
        st.push(i);
    }
    return best;
}

Worked example — next warmer day, [73,74,75,71,69,72,76]

i (temp)pops (resolve)stack (indices) after
0 (73)—[0]
1 (74)pop 0 → res[0]=1[1]
2 (75)pop 1 → res[1]=1[2]
3 (71)—[2,3]
4 (69)—[2,3,4]
5 (72)pop 4 → res[4]=1; pop 3 → res[3]=2[2,5]
6 (76)pop 5 → res[5]=1; pop 2 → res[2]=4[6]

Result [1,1,4,2,1,1,0]. Index 2 (75°) waited 4 days for 76°; index 6 never warms so stays 0.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The stack holds indices whose "answer" is still unresolved, kept in strictly decreasing order of value. When a new element arrives that beats the top of the stack, it simultaneously resolves every stack entry it beats (because the stack is sorted), so each index is popped exactly once — that's what keeps the total work linear despite the nested-looking pop loop.

When to reach for it. Signals: "next greater/smaller element", "distance to the next warmer/taller/larger", or "largest rectangle/area under a skyline." Anything where the answer for position i depends on the nearest future (or past) element satisfying an order relation.

Complexity. O(n) time — amortized, since each index is pushed once and popped at most once; O(n) space for the stack.

Pitfalls & variations. Using >= vs > in the comparison changes whether equal values resolve each other — get this backwards and duplicates silently break. Forgetting leftover stack entries never get a "next" (they default to 0/-1) is a frequent off-by-one. Variants: next-smaller (flip comparison), circular arrays (traverse the array twice), and previous-greater (traverse right-to-left).

Trace input: temps = [73,74,75,71,69,72,76,73] — for each day, find how many days until a strictly warmer day.

itempstack beforepops (idx→ans)stack after
073[]-[0]
174[0]0→1[1]
275[1]1→1[2]
371[2]-[2,3]
469[2,3]-[2,3,4]
572[2,3,4]4→1, 3→2[2,5]
676[2,5]5→1, 2→4[6]
773[6]-[6,7]

Result: ans = [1,1,4,2,1,1,0,0]; indices 6 and 7 stay on the stack forever and default to 0. The trace shows day 6 (76°, the max) triggers a double pop, resolving both day 5 and day 2 in one step.

13 · Prefix Sums & Difference Arrays

Flowchart leaf: sum / additive problem

The idea

Precompute cumulative totals so any range sum is a single subtraction: sum(l..r) = P[r+1] - P[l]. Combine with a hash map of seen prefix sums to count subarrays with a target sum in one pass. The dual, a difference array, applies many range-updates in O(1) each and reconstructs the final array at the end.

Brute force — re-sum each range O(n) per query / O(n²) total

Adding up a[l..r] from scratch for every query. Prefix sums pay O(n) once, then answer each range in O(1).

Template A · count subarrays summing to k (prefix + hashmap) O(n)

int subarraySum(int[] nums, int k){
    Map<Integer,Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    seen.put(0, 1);                       // empty prefix
    int sum = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : nums){
        sum += x;
        count += seen.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0);  // a prior prefix closes a k-sum here
        seen.merge(sum, 1, Integer::sum);
    }
    return count;
}
int subarraySum(vector<int>& nums, int k){
    unordered_map<int,int> seen;
    seen[0] = 1;                       // empty prefix
    int sum = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : nums){
        sum += x;
        count += seen.count(sum - k) ? seen[sum - k] : 0;  // a prior prefix closes a k-sum here
        seen[sum]++;
    }
    return count;
}
def subarray_sum(nums, k):
    seen = {0: 1}                       # empty prefix
    total = count = 0
    for x in nums:
        total += x
        count += seen.get(total - k, 0)  # a prior prefix closes a k-sum here
        seen[total] = seen.get(total, 0) + 1
    return count
function subarraySum(nums: number[], k: number): number {
    const seen = new Map<number, number>();
    seen.set(0, 1);                       // empty prefix
    let sum = 0, count = 0;
    for (const x of nums) {
        sum += x;
        count += seen.get(sum - k) ?? 0;  // a prior prefix closes a k-sum here
        seen.set(sum, (seen.get(sum) ?? 0) + 1);
    }
    return count;
}

Template B · 2D prefix sum (sub-matrix sum in O(1))

// P[i+1][j+1] = sum of the rectangle from (0,0) to (i,j)
int[][] P = new int[m+1][n+1];
for (int i=0;i<m;i++)
    for (int j=0;j<n;j++)
        P[i+1][j+1] = a[i][j] + P[i][j+1] + P[i+1][j] - P[i][j];
// sum of rows [r1,r2], cols [c1,c2]:
int sum = P[r2+1][c2+1] - P[r1][c2+1] - P[r2+1][c1] + P[r1][c1];
// P[i+1][j+1] = sum of the rectangle from (0,0) to (i,j)
vector<vector<int>> P(m+1, vector<int>(n+1, 0));
for (int i=0;i<m;i++)
    for (int j=0;j<n;j++)
        P[i+1][j+1] = a[i][j] + P[i][j+1] + P[i+1][j] - P[i][j];
// sum of rows [r1,r2], cols [c1,c2]:
int sum = P[r2+1][c2+1] - P[r1][c2+1] - P[r2+1][c1] + P[r1][c1];
# P[i+1][j+1] = sum of the rectangle from (0,0) to (i,j)
P = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(m + 1)]
for i in range(m):
    for j in range(n):
        P[i+1][j+1] = a[i][j] + P[i][j+1] + P[i+1][j] - P[i][j]
# sum of rows [r1,r2], cols [c1,c2]:
total = P[r2+1][c2+1] - P[r1][c2+1] - P[r2+1][c1] + P[r1][c1]
// P[i+1][j+1] = sum of the rectangle from (0,0) to (i,j)
const P: number[][] = Array.from({ length: m + 1 }, () => new Array(n + 1).fill(0));
for (let i = 0; i < m; i++)
    for (let j = 0; j < n; j++)
        P[i+1][j+1] = a[i][j] + P[i][j+1] + P[i+1][j] - P[i][j];
// sum of rows [r1,r2], cols [c1,c2]:
const sum = P[r2+1][c2+1] - P[r1][c2+1] - P[r2+1][c1] + P[r1][c1];

Template C · difference array (range add in O(1))

int[] diff = new int[n+1];
void addRange(int l, int r, int val){ diff[l] += val; diff[r+1] -= val; }
int[] build(){ int[] a=new int[n]; int run=0;
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++){ run += diff[i]; a[i]=run; } return a; }
std::vector<int> diff(n+1, 0);
void addRange(int l, int r, int val){ diff[l] += val; diff[r+1] -= val; }
std::vector<int> build(){ std::vector<int> a(n); int run=0;
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++){ run += diff[i]; a[i]=run; } return a; }
diff = [0] * (n + 1)

def add_range(l, r, val):
    diff[l] += val
    diff[r + 1] -= val

def build():
    a = [0] * n
    run = 0
    for i in range(n):
        run += diff[i]
        a[i] = run
    return a
const diff: number[] = new Array(n + 1).fill(0);
function addRange(l: number, r: number, val: number): void { diff[l] += val; diff[r + 1] -= val; }
function build(): number[] {
    const a: number[] = new Array(n).fill(0);
    let run = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) { run += diff[i]; a[i] = run; }
    return a;
}

Worked example — subarrays summing to 2 in [1,1,1]

xrunning sumneed sum−k = sum−2seen count of itcountmap after
—0——0{0:1}
11-100{0:1,1:1}
12011{0:1,1:1,2:1}
13112{…,3:1}

Answer 2 — the subarrays [1,1] at indices (0,1) and (1,2). The map turns "find an earlier prefix" into an O(1) lookup.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Any subarray sum nums[i..j] equals prefix[j] - prefix[i-1]. Fixing j, the count of valid starting points is exactly the number of earlier prefix sums equal to prefix[j] - k. Storing prefix-sum frequencies in a hash map turns "count subarrays ending here with sum k" into an O(1) lookup instead of rescanning from every start.

When to reach for it. "Number of subarrays with sum/XOR equal to k", "subarray sum divisible by k", or anything phrased as a range-sum query repeated over many ranges — especially when negatives are allowed, ruling out sliding window.

Complexity. O(n) time — one pass accumulating the running sum and one map lookup/update per index; O(n) space for the frequency map.

Pitfalls & variations. Must seed the map with {0: 1} to count subarrays starting at index 0. Look up prefix - k before inserting the current prefix, or a subarray can incorrectly "match itself" as a zero-length span. Variants: difference arrays for O(1) range-update/O(n) rebuild, and 2D prefix sums for submatrix queries.

Trace input: nums = [1,1,1,0,1,1], k = 2 — count subarrays summing to exactly 2.

jnumprefixlookup key (prefix-k)count foundansfreq map after
--0--0{0:1}
011-100{0:1,1:1}
112011{0:1,1:1,2:1}
213112{0:1,1:1,2:1,3:1}
303113{0:1,1:1,2:1,3:2}
414214{0:1,1:1,2:1,3:2,4:1}
515326{0:1,1:1,2:1,3:2,4:1,5:1}

Result: 6 subarrays sum to 2: (0,1), (1,2), (1,3), (2,4), (3,5), (4,5). The double count at j=5 (prefix 3 seen twice) shows how one lookup can resolve multiple matches at once.

14 · Dynamic Programming I — 1D, Knapsack, Counting

Flowchart leaves: DP (small constraints) DP (subarray) DP (max/min) DP (counting) DP (parsing)

The idea

DP applies when a problem has overlapping subproblems (the same sub-question recurs) and optimal substructure (the best whole is built from best parts). You define a state, a transition (recurrence), and base cases, then either memoize top-down or fill a table bottom-up. The recipe that never fails:

  1. State: what does dp[i] mean, in one precise sentence?
  2. Transition: how is dp[i] built from smaller states?
  3. Base case: the smallest states you can answer directly.
  4. Order: fill so every state's dependencies are ready first.
  5. Answer: which state (or aggregate) is the result?

Brute force — naive recursion exponential

int coins(int[] c, int amt){                 // recompute same amounts repeatedly
    if (amt == 0) return 0;
    if (amt < 0)  return -1;
    int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int coin : c){
        int sub = coins(c, amt - coin);
        if (sub >= 0) best = Math.min(best, sub + 1);
    }
    return best == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? -1 : best;
}
int coins(std::vector<int>& c, int amt){      // recompute same amounts repeatedly
    if (amt == 0) return 0;
    if (amt < 0)  return -1;
    int best = INT_MAX;
    for (int coin : c){
        int sub = coins(c, amt - coin);
        if (sub >= 0) best = std::min(best, sub + 1);
    }
    return best == INT_MAX ? -1 : best;
}
def coins(c, amt):                            # recompute same amounts repeatedly
    if amt == 0:
        return 0
    if amt < 0:
        return -1
    best = float('inf')
    for coin in c:
        sub = coins(c, amt - coin)
        if sub >= 0:
            best = min(best, sub + 1)
    return -1 if best == float('inf') else best
function coins(c: number[], amt: number): number {   // recompute same amounts repeatedly
    if (amt === 0) return 0;
    if (amt < 0) return -1;
    let best = Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER;
    for (const coin of c) {
        const sub = coins(c, amt - coin);
        if (sub >= 0) best = Math.min(best, sub + 1);
    }
    return best === Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER ? -1 : best;
}

This recomputes coins(amt) for the same amt down countless branches — the overlap DP eliminates.

Template A · 1D min — coin change (fewest coins) O(amount·coins)

int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount){
    int[] dp = new int[amount+1];
    Arrays.fill(dp, amount+1);           // "infinity"
    dp[0] = 0;                           // base: 0 coins make 0
    for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
        for (int c : coins)
            if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a-c] + 1);
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
int coinChange(std::vector<int>& coins, int amount){
    std::vector<int> dp(amount+1, amount+1);   // "infinity"
    dp[0] = 0;                                 // base: 0 coins make 0
    for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
        for (int c : coins)
            if (c <= a) dp[a] = std::min(dp[a], dp[a-c] + 1);
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
def coin_change(coins, amount):
    dp = [amount + 1] * (amount + 1)   # "infinity"
    dp[0] = 0                          # base: 0 coins make 0
    for a in range(1, amount + 1):
        for c in coins:
            if c <= a:
                dp[a] = min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1)
    return -1 if dp[amount] > amount else dp[amount]
function coinChange(coins: number[], amount: number): number {
    const dp: number[] = new Array(amount + 1).fill(amount + 1);   // "infinity"
    dp[0] = 0;                                                     // base: 0 coins make 0
    for (let a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
        for (const c of coins)
            if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}

Template B · 1D counting — number of ways (combinations)

int change(int amount, int[] coins){
    int[] dp = new int[amount+1]; dp[0] = 1;
    for (int c : coins)                   // coin outer ⇒ order-independent combos
        for (int a = c; a <= amount; a++)
            dp[a] += dp[a-c];
    return dp[amount];
}
int change(int amount, std::vector<int>& coins){
    std::vector<int> dp(amount+1, 0); dp[0] = 1;
    for (int c : coins)                   // coin outer ⇒ order-independent combos
        for (int a = c; a <= amount; a++)
            dp[a] += dp[a-c];
    return dp[amount];
}
def change(amount, coins):
    dp = [0] * (amount + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    for c in coins:                       # coin outer ⇒ order-independent combos
        for a in range(c, amount + 1):
            dp[a] += dp[a - c]
    return dp[amount]
function change(amount: number, coins: number[]): number {
    const dp: number[] = new Array(amount + 1).fill(0);
    dp[0] = 1;
    for (const c of coins)                // coin outer ⇒ order-independent combos
        for (let a = c; a <= amount; a++)
            dp[a] += dp[a - c];
    return dp[amount];
}

Template C · 0/1 knapsack (each item once — iterate capacity downward)

int knapsack(int[] w, int[] v, int W){
    int[] dp = new int[W+1];
    for (int i = 0; i < w.length; i++)
        for (int c = W; c >= w[i]; c--)          // DOWNWARD ⇒ item used at most once
            dp[c] = Math.max(dp[c], dp[c-w[i]] + v[i]);
    return dp[W];
}
int knapsack(std::vector<int>& w, std::vector<int>& v, int W){
    std::vector<int> dp(W+1, 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < w.size(); i++)
        for (int c = W; c >= w[i]; c--)          // DOWNWARD ⇒ item used at most once
            dp[c] = std::max(dp[c], dp[c-w[i]] + v[i]);
    return dp[W];
}
def knapsack(w, v, W):
    dp = [0] * (W + 1)
    for i in range(len(w)):
        for c in range(W, w[i] - 1, -1):    # DOWNWARD ⇒ item used at most once
            dp[c] = max(dp[c], dp[c - w[i]] + v[i])
    return dp[W]
function knapsack(w: number[], v: number[], W: number): number {
    const dp: number[] = new Array(W + 1).fill(0);
    for (let i = 0; i < w.length; i++)
        for (let c = W; c >= w[i]; c--)          // DOWNWARD ⇒ item used at most once
            dp[c] = Math.max(dp[c], dp[c - w[i]] + v[i]);
    return dp[W];
}

Template D · Kadane — max subarray (running-best DP)

int maxSubArray(int[] a){
    int best = a[0], cur = a[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++){
        cur = Math.max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend previous or restart at a[i]
        best = Math.max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}
int maxSubArray(std::vector<int>& a){
    int best = a[0], cur = a[0];
    for (size_t i = 1; i < a.size(); i++){
        cur = std::max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend previous or restart at a[i]
        best = std::max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}
def max_sub_array(a):
    best = cur = a[0]
    for i in range(1, len(a)):
        cur = max(a[i], cur + a[i])     # extend previous or restart at a[i]
        best = max(best, cur)
    return best
function maxSubArray(a: number[]): number {
    let best = a[0], cur = a[0];
    for (let i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
        cur = Math.max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend previous or restart at a[i]
        best = Math.max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}

Template E · parsing DP — decode ways (rolling O(1) memory)

int numDecodings(String s){
    if (s.charAt(0)=='0') return 0;
    int prev2 = 1, prev1 = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i < s.length(); i++){
        int cur = 0;
        if (s.charAt(i) != '0') cur += prev1;                     // take 1 digit
        int two = (s.charAt(i-1)-'0')*10 + (s.charAt(i)-'0');
        if (two >= 10 && two <= 26) cur += prev2;                  // take 2 digits
        prev2 = prev1; prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}
int numDecodings(std::string s){
    if (s[0]=='0') return 0;
    int prev2 = 1, prev1 = 1;
    for (size_t i = 1; i < s.size(); i++){
        int cur = 0;
        if (s[i] != '0') cur += prev1;                            // take 1 digit
        int two = (s[i-1]-'0')*10 + (s[i]-'0');
        if (two >= 10 && two <= 26) cur += prev2;                 // take 2 digits
        prev2 = prev1; prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}
def num_decodings(s):
    if s[0] == '0':
        return 0
    prev2, prev1 = 1, 1
    for i in range(1, len(s)):
        cur = 0
        if s[i] != '0':
            cur += prev1                                   # take 1 digit
        two = int(s[i-1]) * 10 + int(s[i])
        if 10 <= two <= 26:
            cur += prev2                                   # take 2 digits
        prev2, prev1 = prev1, cur
    return prev1
function numDecodings(s: string): number {
    if (s[0] === '0') return 0;
    let prev2 = 1, prev1 = 1;
    for (let i = 1; i < s.length; i++) {
        let cur = 0;
        if (s[i] !== '0') cur += prev1;                          // take 1 digit
        const two = (s.charCodeAt(i - 1) - 48) * 10 + (s.charCodeAt(i) - 48);
        if (two >= 10 && two <= 26) cur += prev2;                // take 2 digits
        prev2 = prev1; prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}

Worked example — coin change min, coins {1,2,5}, amount 11

acandidates dp[a-c]+1dp[a]
0base0
1dp[0]+11
2dp[1]+1=2, dp[0]+1=11
3dp[2]+1=2, dp[1]+1=22
5dp[4]+1=3, dp[3]+1=3, dp[0]+1=11
6dp[5]+1=2, dp[4]+1=3, dp[1]+1=22
10dp[5]+1=2 …2
11dp[10]+1=3, dp[9]+1=4, dp[6]+1=33

Answer 3 coins (5+5+1). Each dp[a] is computed once and reused — that reuse is the entire speedup over the recursion above.

Combinations vs permutations: in counting problems, coin-outer/amount-inner counts unordered combos; swapping the loops counts ordered sequences. Getting this backwards is the most common DP counting bug.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. dp[i] = fewest coins to make amount i, and it obeys the recurrence dp[i] = 1 + min(dp[i-c]) over every coin c ≤ i. This is valid because an optimal solution for i must end in some last coin c, and removing that coin leaves an optimal solution for the smaller amount i-c — the classic optimal-substructure argument that lets us build answers for larger amounts strictly from already-finalized smaller ones.

When to reach for it. "Fewest/most ways to reach a target using unlimited/limited copies of given items" — coin change, combination/permutation counting, unbounded knapsack. The target decomposes into a smaller instance of the same problem via one item choice.

Complexity. O(amount × coins) time — one pass over coins for each amount from 1 to target; O(amount) space for the 1D dp array.

Pitfalls & variations. Initializing dp with 0 instead of infinity/sentinel for unreachable amounts silently produces wrong minimums. Iterating amount in the outer loop vs. coin in the outer loop changes counting problems from combinations to permutations (order matters) — mixing this up is the most common bug. Variants: 0/1 knapsack (loop items outer, amount inner, descending) vs. unbounded knapsack (amount inner, ascending, as here).

Trace input: coins = [1,3,4], amount = 6 — fewest coins that sum exactly to 6.

icandidates (dp[i-c]+1)dp[i]
0base case0
1dp[0]+1=11
2dp[1]+1=22
3dp[2]+1=3, dp[0]+1=11
4dp[3]+1=2, dp[1]+1=2, dp[0]+1=11
5dp[4]+1=2, dp[2]+1=3, dp[1]+1=22
6dp[5]+1=3, dp[3]+1=2, dp[2]+1=32

Result: dp[6] = 2 (e.g. 3+3). The table shows dp[3] and dp[4] hitting 1 coin directly, which is what lets dp[6] and dp[5] beat the naive all-ones count.

15 · Dynamic Programming II — Grids & Two Sequences

Flowchart leaves: DP (two sequences) string segmentation DP LIS (max/min)

The idea

When two strings/arrays interact, the state is a pair of prefix lengths dp[i][j] = answer for a[0..i) vs b[0..j). Grid-path problems use dp[i][j] over cell coordinates. The transition compares the current pair of characters/cells and pulls from the neighbours (diagonal / up / left). Most 2D DPs roll down to a 1D array over one dimension.

Brute force — recurse on both indices O(2^(m+n))

Branch on "match / skip in a / skip in b" without a table; the same (i,j) pair is revisited exponentially. The 2D table stores each pair once → O(m·n).

Template A · Longest Common Subsequence O(m·n)

int lcs(String a, String b){
    int m = a.length(), n = b.length();
    int[][] dp = new int[m+1][n+1];
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a.charAt(i-1)==b.charAt(j-1))
                     ? dp[i-1][j-1] + 1                       // chars match
                     : Math.max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]);      // drop one side
    return dp[m][n];
}
int lcs(const std::string& a, const std::string& b){
    int m = a.size(), n = b.size();
    std::vector<std::vector<int>> dp(m+1, std::vector<int>(n+1, 0));
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a[i-1]==b[j-1])
                     ? dp[i-1][j-1] + 1                       // chars match
                     : std::max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]);      // drop one side
    return dp[m][n];
}
def lcs(a, b):
    m, n = len(a), len(b)
    dp = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(m + 1)]
    for i in range(1, m + 1):
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            dp[i][j] = (dp[i-1][j-1] + 1 if a[i-1] == b[j-1]      # chars match
                        else max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]))         # drop one side
    return dp[m][n]
function lcs(a: string, b: string): number {
    const m = a.length, n = b.length;
    const dp: number[][] = Array.from({ length: m + 1 }, () => new Array(n + 1).fill(0));
    for (let i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (let j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a[i - 1] === b[j - 1])
                     ? dp[i - 1][j - 1] + 1                        // chars match
                     : Math.max(dp[i - 1][j], dp[i][j - 1]);       // drop one side
    return dp[m][n];
}

Template B · Edit distance (Levenshtein)

int editDistance(String a, String b){
    int m=a.length(), n=b.length();
    int[][] dp = new int[m+1][n+1];
    for (int i=0;i<=m;i++) dp[i][0]=i;      // delete all
    for (int j=0;j<=n;j++) dp[0][j]=j;      // insert all
    for (int i=1;i<=m;i++)
        for (int j=1;j<=n;j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a.charAt(i-1)==b.charAt(j-1))
                     ? dp[i-1][j-1]
                     : 1 + Math.min(dp[i-1][j-1],             // replace
                              Math.min(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]));// delete / insert
    return dp[m][n];
}
int editDistance(const std::string& a, const std::string& b){
    int m=a.size(), n=b.size();
    std::vector<std::vector<int>> dp(m+1, std::vector<int>(n+1, 0));
    for (int i=0;i<=m;i++) dp[i][0]=i;      // delete all
    for (int j=0;j<=n;j++) dp[0][j]=j;      // insert all
    for (int i=1;i<=m;i++)
        for (int j=1;j<=n;j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a[i-1]==b[j-1])
                     ? dp[i-1][j-1]
                     : 1 + std::min(dp[i-1][j-1],              // replace
                              std::min(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]));// delete / insert
    return dp[m][n];
}
def edit_distance(a, b):
    m, n = len(a), len(b)
    dp = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(m + 1)]
    for i in range(m + 1):
        dp[i][0] = i                       # delete all
    for j in range(n + 1):
        dp[0][j] = j                       # insert all
    for i in range(1, m + 1):
        for j in range(1, n + 1):
            dp[i][j] = (dp[i-1][j-1] if a[i-1] == b[j-1]
                        else 1 + min(dp[i-1][j-1],             # replace
                                     dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]))   # delete / insert
    return dp[m][n]
function editDistance(a: string, b: string): number {
    const m = a.length, n = b.length;
    const dp: number[][] = Array.from({ length: m + 1 }, () => new Array(n + 1).fill(0));
    for (let i = 0; i <= m; i++) dp[i][0] = i;      // delete all
    for (let j = 0; j <= n; j++) dp[0][j] = j;      // insert all
    for (let i = 1; i <= m; i++)
        for (let j = 1; j <= n; j++)
            dp[i][j] = (a[i - 1] === b[j - 1])
                     ? dp[i - 1][j - 1]
                     : 1 + Math.min(dp[i - 1][j - 1],               // replace
                              Math.min(dp[i - 1][j], dp[i][j - 1])); // delete / insert
    return dp[m][n];
}

Template C · count distinct subsequences (rolling 1D)

int numDistinct(String s, String t){
    int n = t.length();
    long[] dp = new long[n+1]; dp[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= s.length(); i++)
        for (int j = n; j >= 1; j--)                  // reverse ⇒ 1D safe
            if (s.charAt(i-1) == t.charAt(j-1)) dp[j] += dp[j-1];
    return (int) dp[n];
}
int numDistinct(const std::string& s, const std::string& t){
    int n = t.size();
    std::vector<long long> dp(n+1, 0); dp[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= (int)s.size(); i++)
        for (int j = n; j >= 1; j--)                  // reverse ⇒ 1D safe
            if (s[i-1] == t[j-1]) dp[j] += dp[j-1];
    return (int) dp[n];
}
def num_distinct(s, t):
    n = len(t)
    dp = [0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    for i in range(1, len(s) + 1):
        for j in range(n, 0, -1):                      # reverse ⇒ 1D safe
            if s[i-1] == t[j-1]:
                dp[j] += dp[j-1]
    return dp[n]
function numDistinct(s: string, t: string): number {
    const n = t.length;
    const dp: number[] = new Array(n + 1).fill(0);
    dp[0] = 1;
    for (let i = 1; i <= s.length; i++)
        for (let j = n; j >= 1; j--)                  // reverse ⇒ 1D safe
            if (s[i - 1] === t[j - 1]) dp[j] += dp[j - 1];
    return dp[n];
}

Template D · word break (segmentation DP)

boolean wordBreak(String s, List<String> dict){
    Set<String> words = new HashSet<>(dict);
    boolean[] dp = new boolean[s.length()+1]; dp[0] = true;
    for (int i = 1; i <= s.length(); i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (dp[j] && words.contains(s.substring(j, i))){ dp[i] = true; break; }
    return dp[s.length()];
}
bool wordBreak(const std::string& s, std::vector<std::string>& dict){
    std::unordered_set<std::string> words(dict.begin(), dict.end());
    std::vector<bool> dp(s.size()+1, false); dp[0] = true;
    for (size_t i = 1; i <= s.size(); i++)
        for (size_t j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (dp[j] && words.count(s.substr(j, i-j))){ dp[i] = true; break; }
    return dp[s.size()];
}
def word_break(s, dict_):
    words = set(dict_)
    dp = [False] * (len(s) + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    for i in range(1, len(s) + 1):
        for j in range(i):
            if dp[j] and s[j:i] in words:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    return dp[len(s)]
function wordBreak(s: string, dict: string[]): boolean {
    const words = new Set(dict);
    const dp: boolean[] = new Array(s.length + 1).fill(false);
    dp[0] = true;
    for (let i = 1; i <= s.length; i++)
        for (let j = 0; j < i; j++)
            if (dp[j] && words.has(s.substring(j, i))) { dp[i] = true; break; }
    return dp[s.length];
}

Template E · Longest Increasing Subsequence O(n log n)

int lengthOfLIS(int[] nums){
    int[] tails = new int[nums.length]; int size = 0;
    for (int x : nums){
        int lo = 0, hi = size;
        while (lo < hi){ int mid=(lo+hi)/2; if (tails[mid]<x) lo=mid+1; else hi=mid; }
        tails[lo] = x;                          // patience sorting
        if (lo == size) size++;
    }
    return size;
}
int lengthOfLIS(std::vector<int>& nums){
    std::vector<int> tails(nums.size()); int size = 0;
    for (int x : nums){
        int lo = 0, hi = size;
        while (lo < hi){ int mid=(lo+hi)/2; if (tails[mid]<x) lo=mid+1; else hi=mid; }
        tails[lo] = x;                          // patience sorting
        if (lo == size) size++;
    }
    return size;
}
def length_of_lis(nums):
    tails = [0] * len(nums)
    size = 0
    for x in nums:
        lo, hi = 0, size
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if tails[mid] < x:
                lo = mid + 1
            else:
                hi = mid
        tails[lo] = x                          # patience sorting
        if lo == size:
            size += 1
    return size
function lengthOfLIS(nums: number[]): number {
    const tails: number[] = new Array(nums.length).fill(0);
    let size = 0;
    for (const x of nums) {
        let lo = 0, hi = size;
        while (lo < hi) { const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1; if (tails[mid] < x) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid; }
        tails[lo] = x;                          // patience sorting
        if (lo === size) size++;
    }
    return size;
}

Worked example — LCS of "ABCBDAB" and "BDCAB"

Filling dp[i][j] (rows = A's prefix, cols = B's prefix); on a match take the diagonal +1, else the max of up/left:

       ""  B  D  C  A  B
   ""   0  0  0  0  0  0
    A    0  0  0  0  1  1
    B    0  1  1  1  1  2
    C    0  1  1  2  2  2
    B    0  1  1  2  2  3
    D    0  1  2  2  2  3
    A    0  1  2  2  3  3
    B    0  1  2  2  3  4
       ""  B  D  C  A  B
   ""   0  0  0  0  0  0
    A    0  0  0  0  1  1
    B    0  1  1  1  1  2
    C    0  1  1  2  2  2
    B    0  1  1  2  2  3
    D    0  1  2  2  2  3
    A    0  1  2  2  3  3
    B    0  1  2  2  3  4
       ""  B  D  C  A  B
   ""   0  0  0  0  0  0
    A    0  0  0  0  1  1
    B    0  1  1  1  1  2
    C    0  1  1  2  2  2
    B    0  1  1  2  2  3
    D    0  1  2  2  2  3
    A    0  1  2  2  3  3
    B    0  1  2  2  3  4
       ""  B  D  C  A  B
   ""   0  0  0  0  0  0
    A    0  0  0  0  1  1
    B    0  1  1  1  1  2
    C    0  1  1  2  2  2
    B    0  1  1  2  2  3
    D    0  1  2  2  2  3
    A    0  1  2  2  3  3
    B    0  1  2  2  3  4

Bottom-right = 4 — e.g. the common subsequence "BCAB". Every cell reads only its three neighbours, so the whole grid is O(m·n).

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. dp[i][j] = LCS length of the first i chars of s1 and first j chars of s2. If the current characters match, they must both belong to the LCS, so dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j-1] + 1; if they don't match, the LCS of the prefixes can't use both characters, so it's the better of dropping one: max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1]). Every cell only needs the row above and the current row, which is why row-by-row filling works.

When to reach for it. Two sequences, asking for the longest common/similar structure preserving relative order (not contiguity) — LCS, edit distance, shortest common supersequence, longest palindromic subsequence (LCS of s with reverse(s)).

Complexity. O(m·n) time and O(m·n) space for the full table; space reducible to O(min(m,n)) by keeping only two rows if reconstruction isn't needed.

Pitfalls & variations. Off-by-one on the 1-indexed dp table vs. 0-indexed strings is the single most common bug. Confusing LCS (subsequence, order preserved, gaps allowed) with longest common substring (contiguous) leads to the wrong recurrence entirely (substring resets to 0 on mismatch). Variants: edit distance replaces the match branch with insert/delete/replace costs.

Trace input: s1 = "ABCBDAB", s2 = "BDCABA" — length of the longest common subsequence.

is1[i]dp row (j=1..6, vs B,D,C,A,B,A)
0-0,0,0,0,0,0
1A0,0,0,1,1,1
2B1,1,1,1,2,2
3C1,1,2,2,2,2
4B1,1,2,2,3,3
5D1,2,2,2,3,3
6A1,2,2,3,3,4
7B1,2,2,3,4,4

Result: dp[7][6] = 4 (one valid LCS is "BCBA"). Watch row 6→7: the match at s1[7]=B against s2[5]=B lifts dp[7][5] from 3 to 4 by pulling from dp[6][4]=3, showing the diagonal-plus-one rule in action.

16 · Bitmask DP

Flowchart leaf: subset state (small n)

The idea

When the state is "which subset of ≤ ~20 items have I used", encode that subset as the bits of an integer. dp[mask][i] then means "best having visited exactly the set mask, currently at item i." This turns factorial search (n! orderings) into O(2ⁿ · n²) — the standard weapon for TSP, assignment, and "visit all" problems.

Bit operations you need

mask & (1 << i)        // is item i in the set?
mask | (1 << i)        // add item i
mask & ~(1 << i)       // remove item i
Integer.bitCount(mask) // how many items used
mask & -mask          // lowest set bit
mask & (1 << i)              // is item i in the set?
mask | (1 << i)              // add item i
mask & ~(1 << i)              // remove item i
__builtin_popcount(mask)     // how many items used
mask & -mask                 // lowest set bit
mask & (1 << i)              # is item i in the set?
mask | (1 << i)              # add item i
mask & ~(1 << i)              # remove item i
bin(mask).count('1')         # how many items used
mask & -mask                  # lowest set bit
mask & (1 << i)                                   // is item i in the set?
mask | (1 << i)                                   // add item i
mask & ~(1 << i)                                   // remove item i
mask.toString(2).replace(/0/g, '').length         // how many items used
mask & -mask                                       // lowest set bit

Brute force — try all orderings O(n!)

Permute all cities/tasks and evaluate each route. 12! ≈ 479 million already; bitmask DP does the same 12-node TSP in ~600k operations.

Template · shortest Hamiltonian path (TSP core) O(2ⁿ·n²)

int tsp(int[][] dist){
    int n = dist.length, FULL = (1 << n) - 1;
    int[][] dp = new int[1 << n][n];
    for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dp[1 << i][i] = 0;      // start at each node
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= FULL; mask++)
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++){
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) == 0) continue;         // i must be in mask
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++){
                if ((mask & (1 << j)) != 0) continue;     // j not yet visited
                int nm = mask | (1 << j);
                dp[nm][j] = Math.min(dp[nm][j], dp[mask][i] + dist[i][j]);
            }
        }
    int ans = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ans = Math.min(ans, dp[FULL][i]);
    return ans;
}
int tsp(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& dist){
    int n = dist.size(), FULL = (1 << n) - 1;
    std::vector<std::vector<int>> dp(1 << n, std::vector<int>(n, INT_MAX / 2));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dp[1 << i][i] = 0;      // start at each node
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= FULL; mask++)
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++){
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) == 0) continue;         // i must be in mask
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++){
                if ((mask & (1 << j)) != 0) continue;     // j not yet visited
                int nm = mask | (1 << j);
                dp[nm][j] = std::min(dp[nm][j], dp[mask][i] + dist[i][j]);
            }
        }
    int ans = INT_MAX;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ans = std::min(ans, dp[FULL][i]);
    return ans;
}
def tsp(dist):
    n = len(dist)
    FULL = (1 << n) - 1
    INF = float('inf')
    dp = [[INF] * n for _ in range(1 << n)]
    for i in range(n):
        dp[1 << i][i] = 0                              # start at each node
    for mask in range(1, FULL + 1):
        for i in range(n):
            if not (mask & (1 << i)):
                continue                                # i must be in mask
            for j in range(n):
                if mask & (1 << j):
                    continue                            # j not yet visited
                nm = mask | (1 << j)
                dp[nm][j] = min(dp[nm][j], dp[mask][i] + dist[i][j])
    return min(dp[FULL][i] for i in range(n))
function tsp(dist: number[][]): number {
    const n = dist.length, FULL = (1 << n) - 1;
    const dp: number[][] = Array.from({ length: 1 << n }, () => new Array(n).fill(Infinity));
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) dp[1 << i][i] = 0;      // start at each node
    for (let mask = 1; mask <= FULL; mask++)
        for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) === 0) continue;         // i must be in mask
            for (let j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                if ((mask & (1 << j)) !== 0) continue;     // j not yet visited
                const nm = mask | (1 << j);
                dp[nm][j] = Math.min(dp[nm][j], dp[mask][i] + dist[i][j]);
            }
        }
    let ans = Infinity;
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) ans = Math.min(ans, dp[FULL][i]);
    return ans;
}

Worked example — 3 nodes, how the masks fill

Distances symmetric: 0-1=1, 1-2=2, 0-2=4. Masks are 3-bit sets of visited nodes.

mask (set)meaningkey values
001,010,100single start nodedp=0 at that node
011 = {0,1}visited 0&1dp[011][1]=1 (0→1)
111 = {0,1,2}all visiteddp[111][2]=dp[011][1]+dist(1,2)=1+2=3

Best path visiting all three ends at node 2 with cost 3 (0→1→2). Each subset is solved once and feeds larger subsets — the exponential factor is 2ⁿ, not n!.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A bitmask compresses "which subset of nodes has been visited" into a single integer, so dp[mask][j] = min cost of a path that has visited exactly the nodes in mask and currently sits at j. The recurrence extends smaller masks by one bit: dp[mask][j] = min over prev in mask (prev≠j) of dp[mask without j][prev] + dist[prev][j]. Because a mask with k bits set only depends on masks with k-1 bits, processing masks in increasing numeric order (or by popcount) guarantees dependencies are ready.

When to reach for it. n is small (≤ ~20) and the state is "a subset of items/nodes visited/used" — TSP, set-cover-style counting, assigning n tasks to n slots, or DP over subsets of a small graph.

Complexity. O(2^n · n²) time (each of 2^n masks, times n choices of current node, times n choices of previous node); O(2^n · n) space for the dp table.

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to add the return-to-start edge when the problem wants a cycle (Hamiltonian tour) vs. an open path is the classic omission. Bit-index confusion — using node id directly as the bit position only works if nodes are 0-indexed and the start node is excluded from the mask. Variants: profile/broken-profile DP for grid tiling uses the same bitmask-over-a-frontier idea.

Trace input: 4 cities {0=start,1,2,3}, dist(0,1)=10, dist(0,2)=15, dist(0,3)=20, dist(1,2)=35, dist(1,3)=25, dist(2,3)=30 — min-cost tour visiting all cities once and returning to 0 (TSP over the 3 non-start nodes).

mask (bits=1,2,4)dp[mask][end]via
{1}dp[1][1]=100→1
{2}dp[2][2]=150→2
{4}dp[4][3]=200→3
{1,2}dp[3][1]=50, dp[3][2]=452→1, 1→2
{1,4}dp[5][1]=45, dp[5][3]=353→1, 1→3
{2,4}dp[6][2]=50, dp[6][3]=453→2, 2→3
{1,2,4}dp[7][1]=70, dp[7][2]=65, dp[7][3]=75best of predecessor masks + edge
close tourmin(70+10, 65+15, 75+20) = 80best: 0→1→3→2→0

Result: Optimal tour cost is 80 (0→1→3→2→0 or its reverse). dp[7][2]=65 is built from dp[5][3]=35 + dist(3,2)=30, which itself came from dp[1][1]=10 + dist(1,3)=25 — a chain showing each mask strictly reuses the previous popcount's answers.

17 · Greedy

Flowchart leaf: greedy solution (max/min)

The idea

Make the locally-best choice at each step and never reconsider. Greedy is only correct when a local optimum provably leads to a global optimum — proven via an exchange argument (any optimal solution can be transformed into the greedy one without getting worse) or a matroid structure. When greedy fails, you usually need DP.

Brute force — DP / try all splits O(n²) or worse

Interval scheduling, jump game, and Huffman all have DP formulations, but when the greedy choice is provably safe you skip the table entirely for O(n log n) or O(n).

Template A · interval scheduling (max non-overlapping) — sort by end O(n log n)

int maxMeetings(int[][] iv){
    Arrays.sort(iv, (a,b) -> a[1] - b[1]);      // earliest finish first
    int count = 0, end = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int[] m : iv)
        if (m[0] >= end){ count++; end = m[1]; } // take it if it starts after last end
    return count;
}
int maxMeetings(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& iv){
    std::sort(iv.begin(), iv.end(), [](const std::vector<int>& a, const std::vector<int>& b){
        return a[1] < b[1];                     // earliest finish first
    });
    int count = 0, end = INT_MIN;
    for (auto& m : iv)
        if (m[0] >= end){ count++; end = m[1]; } // take it if it starts after last end
    return count;
}
def max_meetings(iv):
    iv.sort(key=lambda m: m[1])            # earliest finish first
    count, end = 0, float('-inf')
    for m in iv:
        if m[0] >= end:                    # take it if it starts after last end
            count += 1
            end = m[1]
    return count
function maxMeetings(iv: number[][]): number {
    iv.sort((a, b) => a[1] - b[1]);        // earliest finish first
    let count = 0, end = -Infinity;
    for (const m of iv)
        if (m[0] >= end) { count++; end = m[1]; }  // take it if it starts after last end
    return count;
}

Template B · jump game II (fewest jumps) — BFS-like frontier

int jump(int[] a){
    int jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length - 1; i++){
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + a[i]);   // best reach from this layer
        if (i == curEnd){ jumps++; curEnd = farthest; }  // must jump; extend layer
    }
    return jumps;
}
int jump(std::vector<int>& a){
    int jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)a.size() - 1; i++){
        farthest = std::max(farthest, i + a[i]);   // best reach from this layer
        if (i == curEnd){ jumps++; curEnd = farthest; }  // must jump; extend layer
    }
    return jumps;
}
def jump(a):
    jumps = cur_end = farthest = 0
    for i in range(len(a) - 1):
        farthest = max(farthest, i + a[i])     # best reach from this layer
        if i == cur_end:                        # must jump; extend layer
            jumps += 1
            cur_end = farthest
    return jumps
function jump(a: number[]): number {
    let jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (let i = 0; i < a.length - 1; i++) {
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + a[i]);   // best reach from this layer
        if (i === curEnd) { jumps++; curEnd = farthest; }  // must jump; extend layer
    }
    return jumps;
}

Worked example — interval scheduling

Meetings [1,3],[2,4],[3,5],[0,6],[5,7]. Sort by end → [1,3],[2,4],[3,5],[0,6],[5,7]:

Meetingstart ≥ end?take?end aftercount
[1,3]1 ≥ −∞yes31
[2,4]2 ≥ 3?no31
[3,5]3 ≥ 3?yes52
[0,6]0 ≥ 5?no52
[5,7]5 ≥ 5?yes73

3 meetings. Why earliest-end is safe: the meeting that frees the room soonest leaves the most room for the rest — swapping in any other first choice can't beat it (exchange argument).

Pitfall: greedy on the wrong key silently gives near-right answers. For "max non-overlapping" sort by end; sorting by start or by length is a classic wrong turn.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Sorting by finish time and always keeping the interval that frees up the earliest is optimal because of an exchange argument: for any optimal solution, swapping its first-finishing interval for the earliest-finishing candidate never makes things worse and can only leave more room for later picks. The greedy choice never needs to be reconsidered — that's what makes it greedy rather than requiring backtracking.

When to reach for it. "Maximum number of non-overlapping intervals", "minimum number of rooms/platforms", or any problem where a locally-best choice (by some sort key) provably can't be beaten by saving the resource for later — provable via exchange argument or matroid structure.

Complexity. O(n log n) time dominated by the sort; O(1) extra space beyond the sort (O(n) if the sort isn't in-place).
