Pitfalls & variations. Sorting by start time instead of finish time is the most common wrong greedy — it isn't optimal for maximizing count. Using > instead of >= when comparing start to lastEnd changes whether touching endpoints count as overlapping. Variants: weighted interval scheduling needs DP instead of greedy (greedy fails once profit varies), and minimum platforms/rooms sorts start and end events together instead.

Trace input: intervals sorted by end: A(1,3), B(2,5), C(4,6), D(6,7), E(5,8), F(7,9), G(8,10) — max count of non-overlapping intervals.

intervallastEnd beforestart >= lastEnd?decisionlastEnd after
A(1,3)-∞yestake3
B(2,5)3no (2<3)skip3
C(4,6)3yestake6
D(6,7)6yestake7
E(5,8)7no (5<7)skip7
F(7,9)7yestake9
G(8,10)9no (8<9)skip9

Result: 4 intervals selected: A, C, D, F. Note B and E are skipped even though each individually looks fine — they only conflict with the greedy pick, which is exactly the trade the exchange argument guarantees won't cost us the optimum.

18 · Trie & String Matching

Flowchart leaves: Trie / string matching / rolling hash Trie / DP (segmentation)

The idea

A trie (prefix tree) stores strings by shared prefixes: each node is one character, each root-to-node path is a prefix. Insert/search/startsWith are O(word length), independent of how many words are stored — ideal for autocomplete, prefix queries, and matching many patterns at once. For single-pattern search inside text, KMP (O(n+m)) and rolling hash (Rabin-Karp) avoid re-scanning.

Brute force — compare against every stored word O(words · len)

Checking a prefix by looping over all stored strings. The trie shares common prefixes so a lookup only walks the query's own characters.

Template A · Trie O(L) per op

class Trie {
    private final Trie[] next = new Trie[26];
    private boolean end;
    void insert(String w){
        Trie node = this;
        for (char c : w.toCharArray()){
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node.next[i] == null) node.next[i] = new Trie();
            node = node.next[i];
        }
        node.end = true;
    }
    boolean search(String w){ Trie n = walk(w); return n != null && n.end; }
    boolean startsWith(String p){ return walk(p) != null; }
    private Trie walk(String s){
        Trie node = this;
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()){
            node = node.next[c - 'a'];
            if (node == null) return null;
        }
        return node;
    }
}
class Trie {
private:
    Trie* next[26] = {nullptr};
    bool end = false;

    Trie* walk(const std::string& s){
        Trie* node = this;
        for (char c : s){
            node = node->next[c - 'a'];
            if (node == nullptr) return nullptr;
        }
        return node;
    }

public:
    void insert(const std::string& w){
        Trie* node = this;
        for (char c : w){
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node->next[i] == nullptr) node->next[i] = new Trie();
            node = node->next[i];
        }
        node->end = true;
    }
    bool search(const std::string& w){ Trie* n = walk(w); return n != nullptr && n->end; }
    bool startsWith(const std::string& p){ return walk(p) != nullptr; }
};
class Trie:
    def __init__(self):
        self.next = [None] * 26
        self.end = False

    def insert(self, w):
        node = self
        for c in w:
            i = ord(c) - ord('a')
            if node.next[i] is None:
                node.next[i] = Trie()
            node = node.next[i]
        node.end = True

    def search(self, w):
        n = self._walk(w)
        return n is not None and n.end

    def starts_with(self, p):
        return self._walk(p) is not None

    def _walk(self, s):
        node = self
        for c in s:
            node = node.next[ord(c) - ord('a')]
            if node is None:
                return None
        return node
class Trie {
    private next: (Trie | null)[] = new Array(26).fill(null);
    private end = false;

    insert(w: string): void {
        let node: Trie = this;
        for (const c of w) {
            const i = c.charCodeAt(0) - 'a'.charCodeAt(0);
            if (node.next[i] === null) node.next[i] = new Trie();
            node = node.next[i]!;
        }
        node.end = true;
    }
    search(w: string): boolean { const n = this.walk(w); return n !== null && n.end; }
    startsWith(p: string): boolean { return this.walk(p) !== null; }
    private walk(s: string): Trie | null {
        let node: Trie | null = this;
        for (const c of s) {
            node = node.next[c.charCodeAt(0) - 'a'.charCodeAt(0)];
            if (node === null) return null;
        }
        return node;
    }
}

Template B · KMP substring search O(n+m)

int strStr(String text, String pat){
    int m = pat.length(); int[] lps = new int[m];  // longest proper prefix=suffix
    for (int i=1,len=0; i<m; ){
        if (pat.charAt(i)==pat.charAt(len)) lps[i++] = ++len;
        else if (len>0) len = lps[len-1];
        else lps[i++] = 0;
    }
    for (int i=0,j=0; i<text.length(); ){
        if (text.charAt(i)==pat.charAt(j)){ i++; j++; if (j==m) return i-m; }
        else if (j>0) j = lps[j-1];                 // reuse matched prefix, no re-scan
        else i++;
    }
    return -1;
}
int strStr(const std::string& text, const std::string& pat){
    int m = pat.size(); std::vector<int> lps(m, 0);  // longest proper prefix=suffix
    for (int i=1,len=0; i<m; ){
        if (pat[i]==pat[len]) lps[i++] = ++len;
        else if (len>0) len = lps[len-1];
        else lps[i++] = 0;
    }
    for (int i=0,j=0; i<(int)text.size(); ){
        if (text[i]==pat[j]){ i++; j++; if (j==m) return i-m; }
        else if (j>0) j = lps[j-1];                  // reuse matched prefix, no re-scan
        else i++;
    }
    return -1;
}
def str_str(text, pat):
    m = len(pat)
    lps = [0] * m                          # longest proper prefix=suffix
    i, length = 1, 0
    while i < m:
        if pat[i] == pat[length]:
            length += 1
            lps[i] = length
            i += 1
        elif length > 0:
            length = lps[length - 1]
        else:
            lps[i] = 0
            i += 1
    i = j = 0
    while i < len(text):
        if text[i] == pat[j]:
            i += 1
            j += 1
            if j == m:
                return i - m
        elif j > 0:
            j = lps[j - 1]                 # reuse matched prefix, no re-scan
        else:
            i += 1
    return -1
function strStr(text: string, pat: string): number {
    const m = pat.length; const lps: number[] = new Array(m).fill(0);  // longest proper prefix=suffix
    for (let i = 1, len = 0; i < m; ) {
        if (pat[i] === pat[len]) lps[i++] = ++len;
        else if (len > 0) len = lps[len - 1];
        else lps[i++] = 0;
    }
    for (let i = 0, j = 0; i < text.length; ) {
        if (text[i] === pat[j]) { i++; j++; if (j === m) return i - m; }
        else if (j > 0) j = lps[j - 1];                 // reuse matched prefix, no re-scan
        else i++;
    }
    return -1;
}

Worked example — trie holding {"app","apple"}

root ─a─ p ─p*─ l ─e*        (* = word end)
root ─a─ p ─p*─ l ─e*        (* = word end)
root ─a─ p ─p*─ l ─e*        (* = word end)
root ─a─ p ─p*─ l ─e*        (* = word end)
Querywalkresult
search("app")reach node after 2nd p, end=truetrue
search("ap")reach node, end=falsefalse
startsWith("appl")path exists to ltrue
search("apples")no child 's' after efalse

"app" and "apple" share the "app" path — stored once, so 5 nodes hold both words.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Each trie node represents a shared prefix; children branch on the next character, so any two words sharing a prefix share the same path from the root and only diverge where the strings actually differ. An isEnd flag per node distinguishes "this prefix exists in the tree" from "this prefix is itself a complete stored word" — the same node can be both a mid-path prefix for one word and a terminator for another.

When to reach for it. "Search / autocomplete / prefix match over a dictionary of strings", counting words with a given prefix, or word search on a grid with a dictionary — anywhere repeated prefix comparisons against a fixed word set would otherwise be redone from scratch.

Complexity. O(L) time per insert/search where L is the word length, independent of dictionary size; O(total characters × alphabet branching) space in the worst case (shared prefixes reduce this in practice).

Pitfalls & variations. Confusing "search" (must end exactly on a node with isEnd=true) with "startsWith" (just needs the path to exist) is the classic bug. Not reusing existing nodes on insert (always creating fresh nodes) breaks the whole point of prefix sharing. Variants: compressed/radix tries collapse single-child chains; suffix tries/Aho-Corasick extend this to multi-pattern matching.

Trace input: insert "app", insert "apple", then search("app") and search("appl") — build a trie over {"app","apple"} and test membership.

opcharnode pathactionisEnd after
insert "app"aroot→acreatefalse
insert "app"pa→pcreatefalse
insert "app"pap→pcreatetrue
insert "apple"aroot→areusefalse
insert "apple"pa→preusefalse
insert "apple"pap→preuse (already isEnd)true
insert "apple"lapp→lcreatefalse
insert "apple"eappl→ecreatetrue
search "app"a,p,proot→a→p→ppath exists, check isEndtrue → found
search "appl"a,p,p,lroot→a→p→p→lpath exists, check isEndfalse → not found

Result: "app" is found (isEnd=true at that node); "appl" exists only as a prefix (isEnd=false), so search reports false even though the path is walkable — the distinction the trie exists to make cheap.

19 · Interval Sweep

Flowchart leaf: merge / insert / scan intervals

The idea

Most interval problems collapse once you sort by start (or end) and sweep once, maintaining the "current" interval: merge overlaps, flush and restart on a gap, or insert in order. For "max concurrent" problems, a sweep line over sorted +1/−1 events counts overlaps in O(n log n).

Brute force — compare all pairs O(n²)

Testing every pair for overlap. Sorting imposes an order so a single left-to-right pass sees all overlaps in sequence.

Template A · merge overlapping intervals O(n log n)

int[][] merge(int[][] iv){
    Arrays.sort(iv, (a,b) -> a[0] - b[0]);
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    int[] cur = iv[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < iv.length; i++){
        if (iv[i][0] <= cur[1]) cur[1] = Math.max(cur[1], iv[i][1]);  // overlap → extend
        else { out.add(cur); cur = iv[i]; }                          // gap → flush
    }
    out.add(cur);
    return out.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
std::vector<std::vector<int>> merge(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& iv){
    std::sort(iv.begin(), iv.end(), [](const std::vector<int>& a, const std::vector<int>& b){
        return a[0] < b[0];
    });
    std::vector<std::vector<int>> out;
    std::vector<int> cur = iv[0];
    for (size_t i = 1; i < iv.size(); i++){
        if (iv[i][0] <= cur[1]) cur[1] = std::max(cur[1], iv[i][1]);  // overlap → extend
        else { out.push_back(cur); cur = iv[i]; }                     // gap → flush
    }
    out.push_back(cur);
    return out;
}
def merge(iv):
    iv.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
    out = []
    cur = iv[0]
    for i in range(1, len(iv)):
        if iv[i][0] <= cur[1]:
            cur[1] = max(cur[1], iv[i][1])     # overlap → extend
        else:
            out.append(cur)                    # gap → flush
            cur = iv[i]
    out.append(cur)
    return out
function merge(iv: number[][]): number[][] {
    iv.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0]);
    const out: number[][] = [];
    let cur = iv[0];
    for (let i = 1; i < iv.length; i++) {
        if (iv[i][0] <= cur[1]) cur[1] = Math.max(cur[1], iv[i][1]);  // overlap → extend
        else { out.push(cur); cur = iv[i]; }                          // gap → flush
    }
    out.push(cur);
    return out;
}

Template B · sweep line — max concurrent intervals

int maxOverlap(int[][] iv){
    int n = iv.length; int[] starts = new int[n], ends = new int[n];
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++){ starts[i]=iv[i][0]; ends[i]=iv[i][1]; }
    Arrays.sort(starts); Arrays.sort(ends);
    int i=0,j=0,cur=0,best=0;
    while (i<n){
        if (starts[i] < ends[j]){ cur++; best=Math.max(best,cur); i++; }
        else { cur--; j++; }
    }
    return best;
}
int maxOverlap(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& iv){
    int n = iv.size(); std::vector<int> starts(n), ends(n);
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++){ starts[i]=iv[i][0]; ends[i]=iv[i][1]; }
    std::sort(starts.begin(), starts.end()); std::sort(ends.begin(), ends.end());
    int i=0,j=0,cur=0,best=0;
    while (i<n){
        if (starts[i] < ends[j]){ cur++; best=std::max(best,cur); i++; }
        else { cur--; j++; }
    }
    return best;
}
def max_overlap(iv):
    n = len(iv)
    starts = sorted(x[0] for x in iv)
    ends = sorted(x[1] for x in iv)
    i = j = cur = best = 0
    while i < n:
        if starts[i] < ends[j]:
            cur += 1
            best = max(best, cur)
            i += 1
        else:
            cur -= 1
            j += 1
    return best
function maxOverlap(iv: number[][]): number {
    const n = iv.length;
    const starts = iv.map(x => x[0]).sort((a, b) => a - b);
    const ends = iv.map(x => x[1]).sort((a, b) => a - b);
    let i = 0, j = 0, cur = 0, best = 0;
    while (i < n) {
        if (starts[i] < ends[j]) { cur++; best = Math.max(best, cur); i++; }
        else { cur--; j++; }
    }
    return best;
}

Worked example — merge [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]]

Intervalstarts ≤ cur.end?actioncur / output
[1,3]seed—cur=[1,3]
[2,6]2 ≤ 3 yesextend end to 6cur=[1,6]
[8,10]8 ≤ 6 noflush [1,6], startcur=[8,10]
[15,18]15 ≤ 10 noflush [8,10], startcur=[15,18]

Output [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]. Sorting guarantees any interval that overlaps cur comes right after it.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. After sorting by start time, any interval that overlaps the currently-open merged interval must have a start ≤ the current merged end (since it's sorted, later intervals can only start later or equal). So a single left-to-right sweep with one "current merged interval" suffices — the moment a new interval's start exceeds the current end, every future interval (sorted) is also guaranteed disjoint from it, so it can be closed off for good.

When to reach for it. "Merge overlapping intervals", "insert an interval into a sorted list", meeting-room/free-time problems, or any question about intervals where the answer only depends on order along one axis — sort, then sweep once.

Complexity. O(n log n) time for the sort (the sweep itself is O(n)); O(n) space for the output (O(log n) extra if sorting in place).

Pitfalls & variations. Comparing with < instead of <= at the boundary decides whether touching intervals like [1,3] and [3,5] merge — get the problem's convention right. Forgetting to sort first (or sorting by end instead of start) breaks the single-pass guarantee. Variants: sweep-line with separate start/end events (for counting overlaps at any point) instead of merging whole intervals.

Trace input: intervals = [[1,3],[2,6],[5,7],[8,10],[9,12],[15,18]] (already sorted by start) — merge all overlapping intervals.

currentlast mergedoverlap? (start <= last.end)merged list after
[1,3]none-[[1,3]]
[2,6][1,3]yes (2<=3)[[1,6]]
[5,7][1,6]yes (5<=6)[[1,7]]
[8,10][1,7]no (8>7)[[1,7],[8,10]]
[9,12][8,10]yes (9<=10)[[1,7],[8,12]]
[15,18][8,12]no (15>12)[[1,7],[8,12],[15,18]]

Result: Merged output is [[1,7],[8,12],[15,18]]. The merged end keeps extending (3→6→7) across two consecutive overlaps before the sweep hits a genuine gap at [8,10], showing merges can chain across more than one input interval.

20 · Linked List Manipulation

Flowchart leaf: linked list manipulation

The idea

Pointer surgery: reverse, reorder, merge, remove. Three habits prevent 90% of bugs — save next before you overwrite a link, use a dummy head when the head might change, and draw the pointers before coding. Fast/slow pointers (see §10) find the middle and detect cycles.

Brute force — copy to an array O(n) extra space

Dumping nodes into a list, manipulating, and rebuilding works but wastes O(n) memory; in-place pointer rewiring is O(1) space and is what interviewers want.

Template A · reverse a list O(n), O(1) space

ListNode reverse(ListNode head){
    ListNode prev = null, cur = head;
    while (cur != null){
        ListNode next = cur.next;   // save
        cur.next = prev;            // flip
        prev = cur; cur = next;     // advance
    }
    return prev;
}
ListNode* reverse(ListNode* head){
    ListNode* prev = nullptr, *cur = head;
    while (cur != nullptr){
        ListNode* next = cur->next;   // save
        cur->next = prev;             // flip
        prev = cur; cur = next;       // advance
    }
    return prev;
}
def reverse(head):
    prev, cur = None, head
    while cur is not None:
        nxt = cur.next   # save
        cur.next = prev  # flip
        prev, cur = cur, nxt   # advance
    return prev
function reverse(head: ListNode | null): ListNode | null {
    let prev: ListNode | null = null, cur = head;
    while (cur !== null) {
        const next = cur.next;   // save
        cur.next = prev;         // flip
        prev = cur; cur = next;  // advance
    }
    return prev;
}

Template B · reverse first k nodes (building block for reverse-in-groups)

ListNode reverseK(ListNode head, int k){
    ListNode prev = null, cur = head;
    while (k-- > 0 && cur != null){
        ListNode next = cur.next;
        cur.next = prev; prev = cur; cur = next;
    }
    head.next = cur;      // old head now points to the remainder
    return prev;          // new head of this segment
}
ListNode* reverseK(ListNode* head, int k){
    ListNode* prev = nullptr, *cur = head;
    while (k-- > 0 && cur != nullptr){
        ListNode* next = cur->next;
        cur->next = prev; prev = cur; cur = next;
    }
    head->next = cur;      // old head now points to the remainder
    return prev;           // new head of this segment
}
def reverse_k(head, k):
    prev, cur = None, head
    while k > 0 and cur is not None:
        nxt = cur.next
        cur.next = prev
        prev = cur
        cur = nxt
        k -= 1
    head.next = cur      # old head now points to the remainder
    return prev           # new head of this segment
function reverseK(head: ListNode, k: number): ListNode | null {
    let prev: ListNode | null = null, cur: ListNode | null = head;
    while (k-- > 0 && cur !== null) {
        const next = cur.next;
        cur.next = prev; prev = cur; cur = next;
    }
    head.next = cur;      // old head now points to the remainder
    return prev;          // new head of this segment
}

Template C · remove nth from end (dummy + gap pointers)

ListNode removeNthFromEnd(ListNode head, int n){
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0); dummy.next = head;
    ListNode fast = dummy, slow = dummy;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) fast = fast.next;   // open a gap of n+1
    while (fast != null){ fast = fast.next; slow = slow.next; }
    slow.next = slow.next.next;                       // skip target
    return dummy.next;
}
ListNode* removeNthFromEnd(ListNode* head, int n){
    ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0); dummy->next = head;
    ListNode* fast = dummy, *slow = dummy;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) fast = fast->next;   // open a gap of n+1
    while (fast != nullptr){ fast = fast->next; slow = slow->next; }
    slow->next = slow->next->next;                     // skip target
    return dummy->next;
}
def remove_nth_from_end(head, n):
    dummy = ListNode(0)
    dummy.next = head
    fast = slow = dummy
    for i in range(n + 1):
        fast = fast.next   # open a gap of n+1
    while fast is not None:
        fast = fast.next
        slow = slow.next
    slow.next = slow.next.next    # skip target
    return dummy.next
function removeNthFromEnd(head: ListNode | null, n: number): ListNode | null {
    const dummy = new ListNode(0); dummy.next = head;
    let fast: ListNode | null = dummy, slow: ListNode | null = dummy;
    for (let i = 0; i <= n; i++) fast = fast!.next;   // open a gap of n+1
    while (fast !== null) { fast = fast.next; slow = slow!.next; }
    slow!.next = slow!.next!.next;                     // skip target
    return dummy.next;
}

Worked example — reverse 1→2→3

stepprevcurlist so far
initnull11→2→3
1121→null
2232→1→null
33null3→2→1→null

Return prev = 3. Saving next first is what keeps the tail reachable after each flip.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The invariant held before each step is "everything from the original head up to (but not including) curr has already been reversed and points backward, prev is the new head of that reversed segment, and curr still points forward into the untouched remainder." Each iteration peels one node off the untouched remainder, flips its pointer to point at prev, then advances both prev and curr by one — so the reversed-prefix invariant is preserved and grows by exactly one node per step.

When to reach for it. Any "reverse/rearrange a linked list in place" task — full reversal, reverse in groups of k, reverse a sublist [left,right], or detect/remove a cycle (Floyd's) — the shared tool is a small fixed set of pointers (prev/curr/next, or slow/fast) advanced with no extra data structure.

Complexity. O(n) time — one pass, one pointer reassignment per node; O(1) space — no recursion stack, no auxiliary list (recursive reversal trades this for O(n) call-stack space).

Pitfalls & variations. Saving next after (instead of before) reassigning curr.next loses the rest of the list irrecoverably. Returning curr instead of prev as the new head after the loop ends (curr is null at that point) is the classic off-by-one. Variants: reversing only a sublist requires stitching the reversed segment back between two saved boundary pointers; reversing in groups of k requires recursion or an explicit stack of segment boundaries.

Trace input: 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5 → 6 — reverse the linked list in place, iteratively.

stepprevcurrnext (saved)action
0 (init)null1-start
11221.next = null
22332.next = 1
33443.next = 2
44554.next = 3
55665.next = 4
66nullnull6.next = 5; loop ends (curr null)

Result: New head is prev = 6, giving 6 → 5 → 4 → 3 → 2 → 1. Each row's "action" shows the reversed prefix growing by exactly one link, and the loop terminates precisely when curr falls off the end.

21 · Hashing / Counting

Flowchart leaf: fast lookup, counting, grouping

The idea

A hash map gives O(1) average membership, frequency, and "have I seen the complement?" — turning many O(n²) scans into O(n). Reach for it for two-sum-style complements, frequency counts, grouping (anagrams), and de-duplication.

Brute force — nested search O(n²)

For each element, scan the rest to find a match. The map remembers what you've seen so the match is an O(1) lookup.

Template A · complement lookup — two sum O(n)

int[] twoSum(int[] a, int target){
    Map<Integer,Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++){
        int need = target - a[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(need)) return new int[]{seen.get(need), i};
        seen.put(a[i], i);
    }
    return new int[0];
}
std::vector<int> twoSum(std::vector<int>& a, int target){
    std::unordered_map<int,int> seen;
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)a.size(); i++){
        int need = target - a[i];
        auto it = seen.find(need);
        if (it != seen.end()) return {it->second, i};
        seen[a[i]] = i;
    }
    return {};
}
def two_sum(a, target):
    seen = {}
    for i, x in enumerate(a):
        need = target - x
        if need in seen:
            return [seen[need], i]
        seen[x] = i
    return []
function twoSum(a: number[], target: number): number[] {
    const seen = new Map<number, number>();
    for (let i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
        const need = target - a[i];
        if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need)!, i];
        seen.set(a[i], i);
    }
    return [];
}

Template B · grouping — anagrams by sorted-key

List<List<String>> groupAnagrams(String[] strs){
    Map<String,List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
    for (String s : strs){
        char[] c = s.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(c);
        groups.computeIfAbsent(new String(c), k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(s);
    }
    return new ArrayList<>(groups.values());
}
std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> groupAnagrams(std::vector<std::string>& strs){
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<std::string>> groups;
    for (const std::string& s : strs){
        std::string c = s;
        std::sort(c.begin(), c.end());
        groups[c].push_back(s);
    }
    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> res;
    for (auto& [k, v] : groups) res.push_back(v);
    return res;
}
def group_anagrams(strs):
    groups = {}
    for s in strs:
        c = "".join(sorted(s))
        groups.setdefault(c, []).append(s)
    return list(groups.values())
function groupAnagrams(strs: string[]): string[][] {
    const groups = new Map<string, string[]>();
    for (const s of strs) {
        const c = s.split("").sort().join("");
        if (!groups.has(c)) groups.set(c, []);
        groups.get(c)!.push(s);
    }
    return Array.from(groups.values());
}

Template C · frequency count

Map<Integer,Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
for (int x : a) freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);   // idiomatic counter
std::unordered_map<int,int> freq;
for (int x : a) freq[x]++;   // idiomatic counter
freq = Counter(a)   # idiomatic counter
const freq = new Map<number, number>();
for (const x of a) freq.set(x, (freq.get(x) ?? 0) + 1);   // idiomatic counter

Worked example — two sum, target 9, [2,7,11,15]

ia[i]needneed in map?map after
027no{2:0}
172yes → [0,1]—

Answer [0,1] in one pass; the map converts "does a partner exist earlier?" into an O(1) check.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A hashmap turns "have I seen a value that completes this condition?" into an O(1) average lookup, letting one pass replace nested loops. The invariant is: at index i, the map contains exactly the elements from indices 0..i-1, so a hit reflects a valid, earlier partner — never the current element itself.

When to reach for it. Phrases like "find two numbers that sum to", "count subarrays with sum k", "first repeating element", "longest run with at most k distinct" or "group items by a derived key" — anything trading O(n²) pairwise comparison for O(n) with O(1) membership/frequency checks.

Complexity. Time O(n): one pass, O(1) average hashmap ops. Space O(n) worst case: every element gets its own map entry.

Pitfalls & variations. Checking the map before inserting the current element (otherwise an element can pair with itself); storing only presence when the answer needs the index/count; when the key domain is small and bounded (26 letters, digits 0–9), a fixed-size array is faster than a hashmap and avoids collision overhead.

Trace input: nums = [2, 11, 3, 15, 7, 4], target = 9 — find the indices of two numbers that sum to the target.

Stepinumtarget−numseen (before)Hit?Action
0027{}noinsert 2→0
1111-2{2:0}noinsert 11→1
2236{2:0, 11:1}noinsert 3→2
3315-6{2:0, 11:1, 3:2}noinsert 15→3
4472{2:0, 11:1, 3:2, 15:3}yes (2 at idx 0)return [0, 4]

Result: indices [0, 4] (values 2 and 7) sum to 9. The trace shows the lookup always happens before the insert, so an element can never be matched with itself.

22 · Stack (Parsing)

Flowchart leaf: parse symbols → stack

The idea

A stack mirrors nesting: push on an opener, pop on the matching closer. It handles balanced brackets, expression evaluation (RPN / shunting-yard), and any "most recent unmatched thing" logic. Postfix (RPN) evaluation is a stack of operands; each operator pops two and pushes the result.

Brute force — repeatedly remove matched pairs O(n²)

Scanning the string over and over deleting "()" pairs until stable. The stack resolves each closer in O(1) against the top, one pass.

Template A · balanced brackets O(n)

boolean isValid(String s){
    Deque<Character> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    Map<Character,Character> match = Map.of(')','(', ']','[', '}','{');
    for (char c : s.toCharArray()){
        if (c=='('||c=='['||c=='{') st.push(c);
        else if (st.isEmpty() || st.pop() != match.get(c)) return false;
    }
    return st.isEmpty();
}
bool isValid(const std::string& s){
    std::vector<char> st;
    std::unordered_map<char,char> match = {{')','('}, {']','['}, {'}','{'}};
    for (char c : s){
        if (c=='('||c=='['||c=='{') st.push_back(c);
        else {
            if (st.empty() || st.back() != match[c]) return false;
            st.pop_back();
        }
    }
    return st.empty();
}
def is_valid(s):
    st = []
    match = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
    for c in s:
        if c in '([{':
            st.append(c)
        elif not st or st.pop() != match[c]:
            return False
    return not st
function isValid(s: string): boolean {
    const st: string[] = [];
    const match: Record<string, string> = { ')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{' };
    for (const c of s) {
        if (c === '(' || c === '[' || c === '{') st.push(c);
        else if (st.length === 0 || st.pop() !== match[c]) return false;
    }
    return st.length === 0;
}

Template B · evaluate reverse Polish notation

int evalRPN(String[] tokens){
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (String t : tokens){
        switch (t){
            case "+": st.push(st.pop() + st.pop()); break;
            case "*": st.push(st.pop() * st.pop()); break;
            case "-": { int b=st.pop(), a=st.pop(); st.push(a-b); } break;
            case "/": { int b=st.pop(), a=st.pop(); st.push(a/b); } break;
            default:  st.push(Integer.parseInt(t));
        }
    }
    return st.pop();
}
int evalRPN(std::vector<std::string>& tokens){
    std::vector<int> st;
    for (const std::string& t : tokens){
        if (t == "+") { int b=st.back(); st.pop_back(); int a=st.back(); st.pop_back(); st.push_back(a+b); }
        else if (t == "*") { int b=st.back(); st.pop_back(); int a=st.back(); st.pop_back(); st.push_back(a*b); }
        else if (t == "-") { int b=st.back(); st.pop_back(); int a=st.back(); st.pop_back(); st.push_back(a-b); }
        else if (t == "/") { int b=st.back(); st.pop_back(); int a=st.back(); st.pop_back(); st.push_back(a/b); }
        else st.push_back(std::stoi(t));
    }
    return st.back();
}
def eval_rpn(tokens):
    st = []
    for t in tokens:
        if t == "+":
            st.append(st.pop() + st.pop())
        elif t == "*":
            st.append(st.pop() * st.pop())
        elif t == "-":
            b, a = st.pop(), st.pop()
            st.append(a - b)
        elif t == "/":
            b, a = st.pop(), st.pop()
            st.append(int(a / b))
        else:
            st.append(int(t))
    return st.pop()
function evalRPN(tokens: string[]): number {
    const st: number[] = [];
    for (const t of tokens) {
        switch (t) {
            case "+": st.push(st.pop()! + st.pop()!); break;
            case "*": st.push(st.pop()! * st.pop()!); break;
            case "-": { const b = st.pop()!, a = st.pop()!; st.push(a - b); break; }
            case "/": { const b = st.pop()!, a = st.pop()!; st.push(Math.trunc(a / b)); break; }
            default: st.push(parseInt(t, 10));
        }
    }
    return st.pop()!;
}

Worked example — "([{}])"

charactionstack
(push(
[push( [
{push( [ {
}pop { == match( [
]pop [ == match(
)pop ( == match(empty)

Ends empty → valid. "([)]" would pop ( to match ] — mismatch → invalid.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A stack enforces "last opened, first closed" — the invariant is that the top of the stack is always the most recently opened, unmatched item, which is exactly what nested structures (brackets, tags, function calls, expressions) require for a match.

When to reach for it. Validating/matching nested delimiters, evaluating expressions with precedence, "undo the most recent unresolved thing" (basic calculator, decode string, remove adjacent duplicates), or finding the next greater/smaller element via a monotonic stack.

Complexity. Time O(n): one pass, O(1) push/pop. Space O(n) worst case: an all-openers input like "((((".

Pitfalls & variations. Popping without first checking the stack isn't empty (unmatched closer crashes or false-matches); forgetting to check the stack is empty at the very end (unmatched opener passes silently); mixing bracket types without a closer→opener lookup map. Variant: a monotonic stack keeps elements sorted instead of matching pairs, used for next-greater-element and histogram problems.

Trace input: s = "{[()]}" — determine whether the brackets are validly nested.

StepcharActionStack after (bottom→top)
1{opener → push{
2[opener → push{ [
3(opener → push{ [ (
4)closer → top is ( → match → pop{ [
5]closer → top is [ → match → pop{
6}closer → top is { → match → pop(empty)
7end of stringstack is empty → valid(empty)

Result: valid (true). Every closer resolved against the current top of stack, and the stack was empty at the end — the trace shows both conditions are necessary, not just "no crash mid-scan".

23 · Design + Supporting Structures

Flowchart leaf: object with operation guarantees

The idea

"Design a structure where every operation is O(1)/O(log n)." The trick is composing primitives: a hash map for lookup plus a doubly linked list for order (LRU), an array plus a map for insert-delete-getRandom, a heap plus lazy deletion for a max-stream. Pick the data structure whose strengths match each required operation.

Brute force — single structure O(n) somewhere

An LRU built on just an array or just a map makes one operation O(n) (finding the least-recent, or reordering). Combining two structures gives O(1) on all operations.

Template A · LRU cache (map + access-ordered linked map) O(1)

class LRUCache {
    private final int cap;
    private final LinkedHashMap<Integer,Integer> map;
    LRUCache(int cap){
        this.cap = cap;
        map = new LinkedHashMap<>(cap, 0.75f, true){        // access-order
            protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer,Integer> e){
                return size() > LRUCache.this.cap;           // auto-evict LRU
            }
        };
    }
    int get(int k){ return map.getOrDefault(k, -1); }
    void put(int k, int v){ map.put(k, v); }
}
class LRUCache {
private:
    int cap;
    std::list<std::pair<int,int>> items;                              // front = most recent
    std::unordered_map<int, std::list<std::pair<int,int>>::iterator> map;
public:
    LRUCache(int cap) : cap(cap) {}
    int get(int k){
        auto it = map.find(k);
        if (it == map.end()) return -1;
        items.splice(items.begin(), items, it->second);   // move to front (access-order)
        return it->second->second;
    }
    void put(int k, int v){
        auto it = map.find(k);
        if (it != map.end()){
            it->second->second = v;
            items.splice(items.begin(), items, it->second);
        } else {
            items.emplace_front(k, v);
            map[k] = items.begin();
            if ((int)items.size() > cap){                  // auto-evict LRU
                auto last = items.end(); --last;
                map.erase(last->first);
                items.pop_back();
            }
        }
    }
};
class LRUCache:
    def __init__(self, cap):
        self.cap = cap
        self.map = OrderedDict()   # access-order

    def get(self, k):
        if k not in self.map:
            return -1
        self.map.move_to_end(k)
        return self.map[k]

    def put(self, k, v):
        self.map[k] = v
        self.map.move_to_end(k)
        if len(self.map) > self.cap:      # auto-evict LRU
            self.map.popitem(last=False)
class LRUCache {
    private cap: number;
    private map: Map<number, number>;   // insertion order == access order (re-inserted on touch)

    constructor(cap: number) {
        this.cap = cap;
        this.map = new Map();
    }
    get(k: number): number {
        if (!this.map.has(k)) return -1;
        const v = this.map.get(k)!;
        this.map.delete(k);
        this.map.set(k, v);              // move to most-recent
        return v;
    }
    put(k: number, v: number): void {
        this.map.delete(k);
        this.map.set(k, v);
        if (this.map.size > this.cap) {                    // auto-evict LRU
            const oldest = this.map.keys().next().value!;
            this.map.delete(oldest);
        }
    }
}

Template B · min-stack (pair each value with the min-so-far)

class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> st = new ArrayDeque<>();  // {value, minAtOrBelow}
    void push(int x){ int m = st.isEmpty()? x : Math.min(x, st.peek()[1]); st.push(new int[]{x,m}); }
    void pop(){ st.pop(); }
    int top(){ return st.peek()[0]; }
    int getMin(){ return st.peek()[1]; }
}
class MinStack {
private:
    std::vector<std::pair<int,int>> st;  // {value, minAtOrBelow}
public:
    void push(int x){ int m = st.empty() ? x : std::min(x, st.back().second); st.push_back({x, m}); }
    void pop(){ st.pop_back(); }
    int top(){ return st.back().first; }
    int getMin(){ return st.back().second; }
};
class MinStack:
    def __init__(self):
        self.st = []   # (value, minAtOrBelow)

    def push(self, x):
        m = x if not self.st else min(x, self.st[-1][1])
        self.st.append((x, m))

    def pop(self):
        self.st.pop()

    def top(self):
        return self.st[-1][0]

    def get_min(self):
        return self.st[-1][1]
class MinStack {
    private st: [number, number][] = [];  // [value, minAtOrBelow]

    push(x: number): void {
        const m = this.st.length === 0 ? x : Math.min(x, this.st[this.st.length - 1][1]);
        this.st.push([x, m]);
    }
    pop(): void { this.st.pop(); }
    top(): number { return this.st[this.st.length - 1][0]; }
    getMin(): number { return this.st[this.st.length - 1][1]; }
}

Worked example — LRU, capacity 2

opstate (LRU→MRU)returns
put(1,1)1—
put(2,2)1,2—
get(1)2,11 (1 becomes most-recent)
put(3,3)1,3evicts key 2 (least recent)
get(2)1,3−1 (gone)

Every op is O(1) because the map finds entries and the ordering list tracks recency simultaneously.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Pairing a hashmap (O(1) key→node lookup) with a doubly linked list (O(1) unlink/move-to-front) maintains two invariants at once: the map always points to a live node, and the list order always reflects recency. Eviction becomes "drop the tail" with no scanning.

When to reach for it. The problem demands O(1) (or amortized O(1)) for both a keyed lookup and an order-sensitive operation: LRU/LFU cache, min-stack, browser history, "insert/delete/getRandom in O(1)".

Complexity. Time O(1) per get/put. Space O(capacity): one map entry + one list node per cached key.

Pitfalls & variations. A hashmap alone loses order (can't find the LRU key in O(1)); a list alone loses O(1) lookup; forgetting that get must also move the accessed node to the front (not just put) breaks the recency invariant; eviction must happen at capacity, and must happen for a new key, not an update to an existing one.

Trace input: LRUCache(capacity=2); put(1,1); put(2,2); get(1); put(3,3); get(2); put(4,4); get(1); get(3); get(4) — the canonical LC146 walkthrough.

StepOperationCache (MRU→LRU)EvictedReturned
1put(1,1)[1]——
2put(2,2)[2, 1]——
3get(1)[1, 2]—1
4put(3,3)[3, 1]2—
5get(2)[3, 1]—-1
6put(4,4)[4, 3]1—
7get(1)[4, 3]—-1
8get(3)[3, 4]—3
9get(4)[4, 3]—4

Result: returns 1, -1, -1, 3, 4, matching the standard LC146 example exactly. The trace shows a plain get still reorders the list even though it never inserts — the detail most implementations get wrong.

24 · Simulation

Flowchart leaf: direct transformation / simulation

The idea

The problem literally describes a process — do exactly what it says, carefully. No clever algorithm, just correct bookkeeping and boundaries: spiral traversal, matrix rotation, Conway's life, robot movement. The skill is managing indices and directions without off-by-one errors.

Template A · spiral matrix (shrinking borders) O(m·n)

List<Integer> spiralOrder(int[][] m){
    List<Integer> res = new ArrayList<>();
    int top=0, bot=m.length-1, left=0, right=m[0].length-1;
    while (top<=bot && left<=right){
        for (int c=left;c<=right;c++) res.add(m[top][c]);  top++;
        for (int r=top;r<=bot;r++)    res.add(m[r][right]); right--;
        if (top<=bot){ for (int c=right;c>=left;c--) res.add(m[bot][c]); bot--; }
        if (left<=right){ for (int r=bot;r>=top;r--) res.add(m[r][left]); left++; }
    }
    return res;
}
std::vector<int> spiralOrder(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& m){
    std::vector<int> res;
    int top=0, bot=m.size()-1, left=0, right=m[0].size()-1;
    while (top<=bot && left<=right){
        for (int c=left;c<=right;c++) res.push_back(m[top][c]);  top++;
        for (int r=top;r<=bot;r++)    res.push_back(m[r][right]); right--;
        if (top<=bot){ for (int c=right;c>=left;c--) res.push_back(m[bot][c]); bot--; }
        if (left<=right){ for (int r=bot;r>=top;r--) res.push_back(m[r][left]); left++; }
    }
    return res;
}
def spiral_order(m):
    res = []
    top, bot, left, right = 0, len(m) - 1, 0, len(m[0]) - 1
    while top <= bot and left <= right:
        for c in range(left, right + 1): res.append(m[top][c])
        top += 1
        for r in range(top, bot + 1):    res.append(m[r][right])
        right -= 1
        if top <= bot:
            for c in range(right, left - 1, -1): res.append(m[bot][c])
            bot -= 1
        if left <= right:
            for r in range(bot, top - 1, -1): res.append(m[r][left])
            left += 1
    return res
function spiralOrder(m: number[][]): number[] {
    const res: number[] = [];
    let top = 0, bot = m.length - 1, left = 0, right = m[0].length - 1;
    while (top <= bot && left <= right) {
        for (let c = left; c <= right; c++) res.push(m[top][c]);  top++;
        for (let r = top; r <= bot; r++)    res.push(m[r][right]); right--;
        if (top <= bot) { for (let c = right; c >= left; c--) res.push(m[bot][c]); bot--; }
        if (left <= right) { for (let r = bot; r >= top; r--) res.push(m[r][left]); left++; }
    }
    return res;
}

Template B · rotate image 90° in place (transpose then reverse rows)

void rotate(int[][] a){
    int n = a.length;
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++) for (int j=i+1;j<n;j++){ int t=a[i][j]; a[i][j]=a[j][i]; a[j][i]=t; }
    for (int[] row : a) for (int l=0,r=n-1;l<r;l++,r--){ int t=row[l]; row[l]=row[r]; row[r]=t; }
}
void rotate(std::vector<std::vector<int>>& a){
    int n = a.size();
    for (int i=0;i<n;i++) for (int j=i+1;j<n;j++){ int t=a[i][j]; a[i][j]=a[j][i]; a[j][i]=t; }
    for (auto& row : a) for (int l=0,r=n-1;l<r;l++,r--){ int t=row[l]; row[l]=row[r]; row[r]=t; }
}
def rotate(a):
    n = len(a)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            a[i][j], a[j][i] = a[j][i], a[i][j]
    for row in a:
        l, r = 0, n - 1
        while l < r:
            row[l], row[r] = row[r], row[l]
            l += 1
            r -= 1
function rotate(a: number[][]): void {
    const n = a.length;
    for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) for (let j = i + 1; j < n; j++) { const t = a[i][j]; a[i][j] = a[j][i]; a[j][i] = t; }
    for (const row of a) for (let l = 0, r = n - 1; l < r; l++, r--) { const t = row[l]; row[l] = row[r]; row[r] = t; }
}

Worked example — spiral of a 3×3

1 2 3
4 5 6      → 1 2 3 · 6 9 · 8 7 · 4 · 5
7 8 9
// 1 2 3
// 4 5 6      → 1 2 3 · 6 9 · 8 7 · 4 · 5
// 7 8 9
# 1 2 3
# 4 5 6      → 1 2 3 · 6 9 · 8 7 · 4 · 5
# 7 8 9
// 1 2 3
// 4 5 6      → 1 2 3 · 6 9 · 8 7 · 4 · 5
// 7 8 9

Top row (1,2,3), right col (6,9), bottom row reversed (8,7), left col (4), center (5) → [1,2,3,6,9,8,7,4,5]. Each border shrinks after it's consumed, and the two if guards stop a single leftover row/column from being walked twice.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Simulation problems don't hinge on a clever formula — they hinge on precisely maintained state (position, direction, shrinking boundaries) updated exactly per the rules given. Correctness comes from getting each state transition and boundary check exactly right.

When to reach for it. The problem literally describes a step-by-step process — walk a grid, deal cards, run a simulated game/robot — and asks for the state after N steps or the full output order. No shortcut exists; you model exactly what's described.

Complexity. Time O(cells) or O(steps): every cell/step is touched once. Space O(1) extra beyond the output: a handful of boundary/position variables.

Pitfalls & variations. Off-by-one when shrinking an inclusive boundary; forgetting to re-check top ≤ bottom / left ≤ right between the horizontal and vertical sweeps of the same lap (causes double-visiting the middle row/column on odd-sized inputs); direction-cycling logic drifting on non-square matrices.

Trace input: matrix = [[1,2,3],[4,5,6],[7,8,9]] — read all elements in spiral order.

StepLegBoundaries before (T,B,L,R)Cells visitedResult so far
1top row, left→rightT0 B2 L0 R21, 2, 3[1,2,3]
2right col, top→bottomT1 B2 L0 R26, 9[1,2,3,6,9]
3bottom row, right→leftT1 B2 L0 R18, 7[1,2,3,6,9,8,7]
4left col, bottom→topT1 B1 L0 R14[1,2,3,6,9,8,7,4]
5top row, left→rightT1 B1 L1 R15[1,2,3,6,9,8,7,4,5]
6loop check: T2 > B1T2 B1 L1 R1(none — loop exits)[1,2,3,6,9,8,7,4,5]

Result: [1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 8, 7, 4, 5]. Each leg shrinks exactly one boundary and re-tests the loop condition, which is what stops the sweep from re-visiting the center cell.

25 · Math & Bit Manipulation

Flowchart leaf: math identities, powers, bit tricks

The idea

Exploit algebraic or bitwise identities to replace loops with O(1)/O(log) tricks. XOR cancels equal pairs (x^x=0); n & (n-1) clears the lowest set bit; fast exponentiation squares in O(log e).

Template A · single number via XOR O(n), O(1) space

int singleNumber(int[] a){ int x=0; for (int v:a) x^=v; return x; }   // pairs vanish
int singleNumber(std::vector<int>& a){ int x=0; for (int v : a) x^=v; return x; }   // pairs vanish
def single_number(a):
    x = 0
    for v in a:
        x ^= v   # pairs vanish
    return x
function singleNumber(a: number[]): number {
    let x = 0;
    for (const v of a) x ^= v;   // pairs vanish
    return x;
}

Template B · fast modular exponentiation O(log e)

long power(long base, long e, long mod){
    long r = 1; base %= mod;
    while (e > 0){
        if ((e & 1) == 1) r = r * base % mod;   // odd bit ⇒ multiply
        base = base * base % mod;               // square
        e >>= 1;
    }
    return r;
}
long long power(long long base, long long e, long long mod){
    long long r = 1; base %= mod;
    while (e > 0){
        if ((e & 1) == 1) r = r * base % mod;   // odd bit ⇒ multiply
        base = base * base % mod;               // square
        e >>= 1;
    }
    return r;
}
def power(base, e, mod):
    r = 1
    base %= mod
    while e > 0:
        if e & 1 == 1:
            r = r * base % mod   # odd bit ⇒ multiply
        base = base * base % mod   # square
        e >>= 1
    return r
function power(base: bigint, e: bigint, mod: bigint): bigint {
    let r = 1n;
    base %= mod;
    while (e > 0n) {
        if ((e & 1n) === 1n) r = r * base % mod;   // odd bit ⇒ multiply
        base = base * base % mod;                   // square
        e >>= 1n;
    }
    return r;
}

Common bit tricks

n & (n-1)              // drop lowest set bit  → power-of-two test: n>0 && (n&(n-1))==0
n & -n                 // isolate lowest set bit
Integer.bitCount(n)    // popcount
x ^= (1 << i)          // toggle bit i
n & (n-1)                  // drop lowest set bit  → power-of-two test: n>0 && (n&(n-1))==0
n & -n                     // isolate lowest set bit
__builtin_popcount(n)      // popcount
x ^= (1 << i)               // toggle bit i
n & (n - 1)                # drop lowest set bit  → power-of-two test: n>0 and (n&(n-1))==0
n & -n                     # isolate lowest set bit
bin(n).count('1')          # popcount
x ^= (1 << i)               # toggle bit i
n & (n - 1);                 // drop lowest set bit  → power-of-two test: n>0 && (n&(n-1))==0
n & -n;                      // isolate lowest set bit
n.toString(2).split('1').length - 1;   // popcount
x ^= (1 << i);                // toggle bit i

Worked example — single number in [4,1,2,1,2]

XOR is associative/commutative, so order doesn't matter: 4^1^2^1^2 = 4^(1^1)^(2^2) = 4^0^0 = 4. The two 1s and two 2s annihilate, leaving the loner.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. XOR is its own inverse and commutative/associative, so XOR-ing every element cancels any value that appears an even number of times (a^a=0). The running invariant is "accumulator = XOR of all values not yet cancelled," so after a full pass only the odd-count value(s) survive.

When to reach for it. "Every element appears twice except one," parity/toggle questions, swapping without a temp variable, or representing a set as a bitmask (subset enumeration, "missing number") — anywhere equality-under-XOR determines the answer.

Complexity. Time O(n): one pass. Space O(1): a single accumulator.

Pitfalls & variations. Plain XOR only isolates a single odd-count value cleanly when everything else appears in pairs — "Single Number II" (others appear 3×) and "Single Number III" (two singles) need bit-counting or a partitioning bit, not plain XOR alone; reaching for addition/subtraction instead of XOR risks overflow and needs sorting.

Trace input: nums = [4, 1, 2, 1, 2] — find the element that appears exactly once.

Stepnumnum (binary)result beforeresult after (binary)
0 (init)———000 (0)
14100000100 (4)
21001100101 (5)
32010101111 (7)
41001111110 (6)
52010110100 (4)

Result: 4. Every value that appears twice (1 and 2) cancels itself out in pairs, so the running XOR settles on the one value without a partner.

26 · Number Theory

Flowchart leaf: number properties

The idea

Primes, divisors, GCD/LCM, modular arithmetic. Two workhorses: the Sieve of Eratosthenes finds all primes below n in O(n log log n), and Euclid's GCD runs in O(log min).

Brute force — trial division per number O(n√n)

Testing each number for primality independently. The sieve instead crosses off multiples once, sharing work across all numbers.

Template A · Sieve of Eratosthenes O(n log log n)

int countPrimes(int n){
    if (n < 3) return 0;
    boolean[] composite = new boolean[n];
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 2; i < n; i++){
        if (composite[i]) continue;
        count++;
        for (long j = (long)i*i; j < n; j += i) composite[(int)j] = true; // mark multiples
    }
    return count;
}
int countPrimes(int n){
    if (n < 3) return 0;
    std::vector<bool> composite(n, false);
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 2; i < n; i++){
        if (composite[i]) continue;
        count++;
        for (long long j = (long long)i*i; j < n; j += i) composite[j] = true; // mark multiples
    }
    return count;
}
def count_primes(n):
    if n < 3:
        return 0
    composite = [False] * n
    count = 0
    for i in range(2, n):
        if composite[i]:
            continue
        count += 1
        for j in range(i * i, n, i):
            composite[j] = True   # mark multiples
    return count
function countPrimes(n: number): number {
    if (n < 3) return 0;
    const composite = new Array<boolean>(n).fill(false);
    let count = 0;
    for (let i = 2; i < n; i++) {
        if (composite[i]) continue;
        count++;
        for (let j = i * i; j < n; j += i) composite[j] = true; // mark multiples
    }
    return count;
}

Template B · GCD / LCM

long gcd(long a, long b){ return b == 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b); }
long lcm(long a, long b){ return a / gcd(a, b) * b; }        // divide first to avoid overflow
long long gcd(long long a, long long b){ return b == 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b); }
long long lcm(long long a, long long b){ return a / gcd(a, b) * b; }        // divide first to avoid overflow
def gcd(a, b):
    return a if b == 0 else gcd(b, a % b)

def lcm(a, b):
    return a // gcd(a, b) * b        # divide first to avoid overflow
function gcd(a: number, b: number): number { return b === 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b); }
function lcm(a: number, b: number): number { return a / gcd(a, b) * b; }        // divide first to avoid overflow

Worked example — primes below 10

icomposite?markscount
2no4,6,81
3no92
4yesskip2
5no(25≥10)3
6..8yesskip3
9yesskip3

Wait — 7 is also prime: at i=7 composite is false, count→4 (2,3,5,7). Starting the inner loop at i*i (not 2i) skips redundant marking.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The Sieve of Eratosthenes maintains the invariant "every composite ≤ the current limit has already been marked by the time we reach its smallest prime factor" — each prime p eliminates its own multiples the moment it's confirmed prime, and every composite's smallest prime factor is ≤ √n, so the sieve never has to "discover" a composite the hard way.

When to reach for it. Primality is needed for many numbers up to n, not just one — "count primes below n," precomputing a smallest-prime-factor table for fast factorization, GCD/LCM-heavy batch problems.

Complexity. Time O(n log log n) (sum of n/p over all primes p ≤ n). Space O(n) for the boolean array.

Pitfalls & variations. Off-by-one on inclusive vs. exclusive n; forgetting 0 and 1 aren't prime; starting the inner marking loop at 2p instead of p*p is still correct, just slower (multiples below p² are already marked by smaller primes); not capping the outer loop at √n is a performance miss, not a correctness bug. Variants: segmented sieve for huge n; linear sieve to build a smallest-prime-factor table in O(n).

Trace input: n = 10 — sieve to find all primes below 10 (check indices 2..9).

Steppp prime?ActionRemaining candidates after
12yesmark multiples 4, 6, 8 composite2, 3, 5, 7, 9
23yesmark multiples 6 (already), 92, 3, 5, 7
34no (composite)skip2, 3, 5, 7
45yesno multiple of 5 below 10 left to mark2, 3, 5, 7
56no (composite)skip2, 3, 5, 7
67yesno multiple of 7 below 10 left to mark2, 3, 5, 7
78no (composite)skip2, 3, 5, 7
89no (composite, marked at p=3)skip2, 3, 5, 7

Result: primes below 10 = {2, 3, 5, 7}, count 4. Past p=3 (⌈√10⌉) no new numbers ever get marked — the trace shows the tail of the outer loop is pure overhead once p exceeds √n.

27 · Fenwick / Segment Tree

Flowchart leaf: dynamic range / order queries

The idea

When values change and you keep asking range aggregates (sum / min / kth), a plain prefix-sum array can't keep up (O(n) per update). A Fenwick tree (BIT) gives O(log n) point-update + prefix-query using the lowest-set-bit trick; a segment tree generalizes to any associative aggregate and range updates.

Brute force — prefix array rebuild O(n) per update

Recomputing prefix sums after every change. The BIT localizes each update to O(log n) cells.

Template A · Fenwick tree (BIT) O(log n) update & query

class Fenwick {                    // 1-indexed
    int[] t; int n;
    Fenwick(int n){ this.n=n; t=new int[n+1]; }
    void update(int i, int delta){ for (; i<=n; i += i&-i) t[i]+=delta; }
    int prefix(int i){ int s=0; for (; i>0; i-=i&-i) s+=t[i]; return s; }
    int range(int l, int r){ return prefix(r) - prefix(l-1); }
}
class Fenwick {                    // 1-indexed
public:
    std::vector<int> t; int n;
    Fenwick(int n){ this->n=n; t.assign(n+1, 0); }
    void update(int i, int delta){ for (; i<=n; i += i&-i) t[i]+=delta; }
    int prefix(int i){ int s=0; for (; i>0; i-=i&-i) s+=t[i]; return s; }
    int range(int l, int r){ return prefix(r) - prefix(l-1); }
};
class Fenwick:                    # 1-indexed
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.t = [0] * (n + 1)

    def update(self, i, delta):
        while i <= self.n:
            self.t[i] += delta
            i += i & -i

    def prefix(self, i):
        s = 0
        while i > 0:
            s += self.t[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    def range(self, l, r):
        return self.prefix(r) - self.prefix(l - 1)
class Fenwick {                    // 1-indexed
    t: number[]; n: number;
    constructor(n: number) { this.n = n; this.t = new Array(n + 1).fill(0); }
    update(i: number, delta: number): void { for (; i <= this.n; i += i & -i) this.t[i] += delta; }
    prefix(i: number): number { let s = 0; for (; i > 0; i -= i & -i) s += this.t[i]; return s; }
    range(l: number, r: number): number { return this.prefix(r) - this.prefix(l - 1); }
}

Template B · segment tree (range sum, point update)

class SegTree {
    int[] t; int n;
    SegTree(int[] a){ n=a.length; t=new int[2*n];
        for (int i=0;i<n;i++) t[n+i]=a[i];
        for (int i=n-1;i>0;i--) t[i]=t[2*i]+t[2*i+1];
    }
    void update(int i, int val){ for (t[i+=n]=val; i>1; i>>=1) t[i>>1]=t[i]+t[i^1]; }
    int query(int l, int r){ int s=0;                    // [l, r)
        for (l+=n, r+=n; l<r; l>>=1, r>>=1){
            if ((l&1)==1) s+=t[l++];
            if ((r&1)==1) s+=t[--r];
        } return s;
    }
}
class SegTree {
public:
    std::vector<int> t; int n;
    SegTree(std::vector<int>& a){ n=a.size(); t.assign(2*n, 0);
        for (int i=0;i<n;i++) t[n+i]=a[i];
        for (int i=n-1;i>0;i--) t[i]=t[2*i]+t[2*i+1];
    }
    void update(int i, int val){ for (t[i+=n]=val; i>1; i>>=1) t[i>>1]=t[i]+t[i^1]; }
    int query(int l, int r){ int s=0;                    // [l, r)
        for (l+=n, r+=n; l<r; l>>=1, r>>=1){
            if ((l&1)==1) s+=t[l++];
            if ((r&1)==1) s+=t[--r];
        } return s;
    }
};
class SegTree:
    def __init__(self, a):
        self.n = len(a)
        self.t = [0] * (2 * self.n)
        for i in range(self.n):
            self.t[self.n + i] = a[i]
        for i in range(self.n - 1, 0, -1):
            self.t[i] = self.t[2 * i] + self.t[2 * i + 1]

    def update(self, i, val):
        i += self.n
        self.t[i] = val
        while i > 1:
            i >>= 1
            self.t[i] = self.t[2 * i] + self.t[2 * i + 1]

    def query(self, l, r):                        # [l, r)
        s = 0
        l += self.n
        r += self.n
        while l < r:
            if l & 1 == 1:
                s += self.t[l]
                l += 1
            if r & 1 == 1:
                r -= 1
                s += self.t[r]
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        return s
class SegTree {
    t: number[]; n: number;
    constructor(a: number[]) {
        this.n = a.length;
        this.t = new Array(2 * this.n).fill(0);
        for (let i = 0; i < this.n; i++) this.t[this.n + i] = a[i];
        for (let i = this.n - 1; i > 0; i--) this.t[i] = this.t[2 * i] + this.t[2 * i + 1];
    }
    update(i: number, val: number): void {
        i += this.n;
        this.t[i] = val;
        for (; i > 1; i >>= 1) this.t[i >> 1] = this.t[i] + this.t[i ^ 1];
    }
    query(l: number, r: number): number {                    // [l, r)
        let s = 0;
        for (l += this.n, r += this.n; l < r; l >>= 1, r >>= 1) {
            if ((l & 1) === 1) s += this.t[l++];
            if ((r & 1) === 1) s += this.t[--r];
        }
        return s;
    }
}

Worked example — Fenwick on 6 slots

opeffectresult
update(3,+5)touches t[3],t[4]index 3 holds 5
update(5,+2)touches t[5],t[6]index 5 holds 2
range(3,5)prefix(5)−prefix(2)=7−07
update(5,−2)index 5 back to 0—
range(3,5)prefix(5)−prefix(2)=55

Each query/update walks ≤ log₂6 ≈ 3 cells via the i & -i jumps.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A Fenwick (Binary Indexed) tree stores at index i the sum of a power-of-two-sized block ending at i, where the block size is i's lowest set bit. That invariant lets update climb via i += i & (-i) and query climb via i -= i & (-i), each touching only O(log n) nodes instead of rewriting or scanning O(n) of them.

When to reach for it. Point updates and prefix/range-sum queries are interleaved and need to beat O(n) recompute per query — "range sum query — mutable," inversion counting, frequency-rank queries (order statistics on a stream).

Complexity. Time O(log n) per update or prefix-sum query; O(n log n) to build via n sequential updates. Space O(n).

Pitfalls & variations. Fenwick trees are 1-indexed — calling update/query with index 0 breaks the i & (-i) climb; a range sum over [l, r] needs query(r) − query(l-1), not a single call; Fenwick relies on subtraction being valid, so it's a poor fit for range-min/max (use a segment tree there instead).

Trace input: a[1..6] = [3, 2, -1, 6, 5, 4] — build a Fenwick tree over 6 slots, then query the prefix sum(6).

StepOperationIndices touchedtree[1..6] after
1update(1, +3)1 → 2 → 4[3, 3, 0, 3, 0, 0]
2update(2, +2)2 → 4[3, 5, 0, 5, 0, 0]
3update(3, -1)3 → 4[3, 5, -1, 4, 0, 0]
4update(4, +6)4 (4+4=8>6, stop)[3, 5, -1, 10, 0, 0]
5update(5, +5)5 → 6[3, 5, -1, 10, 5, 5]
6update(6, +4)6 (6+2=8>6, stop)[3, 5, -1, 10, 5, 9]
7query(6)6 → 4 → 0 (stop)sum = tree[6]+tree[4] = 9+10 = 19

Result: prefix sum(6) = 19, matching 3+2-1+6+5+4 = 19 directly. The trace shows update climbing by adding the lowest set bit and query climbing by subtracting it — mirror-image walks over the same tree.

28 · Specialized (Quickselect, Reservoir Sampling)

Flowchart leaf: specialized / advanced pattern

The idea

The catch-all for techniques that don't fit a common bucket. Two you should actually know: quickselect (kth element in average O(n), beating heap/sort) and reservoir sampling (uniform random pick from a stream of unknown length in O(1) memory).

Template A · quickselect — kth smallest avg O(n)

int quickselect(int[] a, int k){          // k is 0-based rank
    int lo=0, hi=a.length-1;
    while (lo < hi){
        int p = partition(a, lo, hi);
        if (p == k) return a[p];
        else if (p < k) lo = p + 1;
        else hi = p - 1;
    }
    return a[lo];
}
int partition(int[] a, int lo, int hi){
    int pivot = a[hi], i = lo;
    for (int j = lo; j < hi; j++) if (a[j] < pivot) swap(a, i++, j);
    swap(a, i, hi); return i;
}
int quickselect(vector<int>& a, int k){          // k is 0-based rank
    int lo = 0, hi = (int)a.size() - 1;
    while (lo < hi){
        int p = partition(a, lo, hi);
        if (p == k) return a[p];
        else if (p < k) lo = p + 1;
        else hi = p - 1;
    }
    return a[lo];
}
int partition(vector<int>& a, int lo, int hi){
    int pivot = a[hi], i = lo;
    for (int j = lo; j < hi; j++) if (a[j] < pivot) std::swap(a[i++], a[j]);
    std::swap(a[i], a[hi]); return i;
}
def quickselect(a, k):          # k is 0-based rank
    lo, hi = 0, len(a) - 1
    while lo < hi:
        p = partition(a, lo, hi)
        if p == k:
            return a[p]
        elif p < k:
            lo = p + 1
        else:
            hi = p - 1
    return a[lo]

def partition(a, lo, hi):
    pivot, i = a[hi], lo
    for j in range(lo, hi):
        if a[j] < pivot:
            a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]
            i += 1
    a[i], a[hi] = a[hi], a[i]
    return i
function quickselect(a: number[], k: number): number {          // k is 0-based rank
    let lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        const p = partition(a, lo, hi);
        if (p === k) return a[p];
        else if (p < k) lo = p + 1;
        else hi = p - 1;
    }
    return a[lo];
}
function partition(a: number[], lo: number, hi: number): number {
    const pivot = a[hi];
    let i = lo;
    for (let j = lo; j < hi; j++) {
        if (a[j] < pivot) {
            [a[i], a[j]] = [a[j], a[i]];
            i++;
        }
    }
    [a[i], a[hi]] = [a[hi], a[i]];
    return i;
}

Template B · reservoir sampling — one uniform pick from a stream

int pick(int[] stream){
    Random rand = new Random();
    int result = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : stream){
        count++;
        if (rand.nextInt(count) == 0) result = x;   // keep x with prob 1/count
    }
    return result;
}
int pick(vector<int>& stream){
    std::random_device rd;
    std::mt19937 gen(rd());
    int result = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : stream){
        count++;
        std::uniform_int_distribution<int> dist(0, count - 1);
        if (dist(gen) == 0) result = x;   // keep x with prob 1/count
    }
    return result;
}
def pick(stream):
    result, count = 0, 0
    for x in stream:
        count += 1
        if random.randrange(count) == 0:
            result = x   # keep x with prob 1/count
    return result
function pick(stream: number[]): number {
    let result = 0, count = 0;
    for (const x of stream) {
        count++;
        if (Math.floor(Math.random() * count) === 0) result = x;   // keep x with prob 1/count
    }
    return result;
}

Worked example — reservoir uniformity, stream of 5

Element #3 is chosen when seen (prob 1/3), then must survive #4 (kept-out prob 3/4) and #5 (4/5):

P(keep #3) = 1/3 × 3/4 × 4/5 = 1/5
// P(keep #3) = 1/3 × 3/4 × 4/5 = 1/5
# P(keep #3) = 1/3 × 3/4 × 4/5 = 1/5
// P(keep #3) = 1/3 × 3/4 × 4/5 = 1/5

Every element ends with probability exactly 1/5 — uniform, using a single variable and one pass. Quickselect's average O(n) comes from discarding one partition side each round (n + n/2 + n/4 + … ≈ 2n).

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Reservoir sampling maintains the invariant that after processing i elements, every element seen so far has probability exactly k/i of being in the reservoir. When element i+1 arrives, it's admitted with probability k/(i+1), and any existing occupant is displaced with that same probability — so the invariant renews itself inductively for every new element.

When to reach for it. Sampling uniformly from a stream of unknown or huge length using O(k) space — "pick a random node from a linked list of unknown length," "sample k lines from a massive file/stream you can't hold in memory."

Complexity. Time O(n): one pass, one random draw per element. Space O(k): the reservoir itself.

Pitfalls & variations. Drawing the random index over the wrong range — it must be [0, i] inclusive of i, not [0, k); replacing with the wrong probability (must be k/(i+1), not 1/(i+1)); assuming later elements are less likely to survive — they aren't, that's the entire point of the invariant. Quickselect uses a related "maintain an invariant partition" idea, but for order statistics via random pivots rather than uniform sampling.

Trace input: k = 1, stream = [A, B, C, D, E] — pick one element with uniform 1/5 probability each, using O(1) extra space.

Stepi (0-indexed)IncomingDraw j in [0,i]Replace? (j<k)Reservoir after
00A— (seed)—A
11Bj=0yes (0<1)B
22Cj=2no (2≥1)B
33Dj=0yes (0<1)D
44Ej=4no (4≥1)D

Result: the reservoir holds D. D's survival probability telescopes to exactly 1/5: it wins its own draw at i=3 with probability 1/4, then survives not being replaced at i=4 with probability 4/5 — 1/4 × 4/5 = 1/5, matching every other element's chance.

1 · Arrays & Hashing

Tests whether you reach for O(1) lookup structures (hash maps/sets) to trade space for time whenever you see "duplicate", "pair that sums to", "grouped by", or "frequency of" in a problem statement.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern

Worked example — Group Anagrams (LC 49, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Uber, Bloomberg

Problem. Given an array of strings, group the strings that are anagrams of one another, returning the groups in any order.

Idea. Two strings are anagrams iff they share the same multiset of characters, so sorting each string's characters yields a canonical key. Bucket every string into a hash map under that key; the map's values are the answer groups.

class Solution {
    public List<List<String>> groupAnagrams(String[] strs) {
        Map<String, List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
        for (String s : strs) {
            char[] chars = s.toCharArray();
            Arrays.sort(chars);
            String key = new String(chars);
            groups.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(s);
        }
        return new ArrayList<>(groups.values());
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<string>> groupAnagrams(vector<string>& strs) {
        unordered_map<string, vector<string>> groups;
        for (const string& s : strs) {
            string key = s;
            sort(key.begin(), key.end());
            groups[key].push_back(s);
        }
        vector<vector<string>> result;
        for (auto& [key, group] : groups) {
            result.push_back(group);
        }
        return result;
    }
};
class Solution:
    def groupAnagrams(self, strs: List[str]) -> List[List[str]]:
        groups = {}
        for s in strs:
            key = "".join(sorted(s))
            groups.setdefault(key, []).append(s)
        return list(groups.values())
class Solution {
    groupAnagrams(strs: string[]): string[][] {
        const groups = new Map<string, string[]>();
        for (const s of strs) {
            const key = s.split("").sort().join("");
            if (!groups.has(key)) groups.set(key, []);
            groups.get(key)!.push(s);
        }
        return Array.from(groups.values());
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n * k log k) Space O(n * k) — n strings each sorted in O(k log k) for length k, plus map storage for every character.

Walkthrough. Input ["eat","tea","tan","ate","nat","bat"]:

stringsorted keybucket after insert
eataetaet -> [eat]
teaaetaet -> [eat, tea]
tanantant -> [tan]
ateaetaet -> [eat, tea, ate]
natantant -> [tan, nat]
batabtabt -> [bat]

Final groups: [eat, tea, ate], [tan, nat], [bat].

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Anagrams share a canonical form — their letters sorted, or a fixed-length letter-count signature — so hashing every string by that canonical key groups every permutation into the same bucket. The invariant enforced is "two strings land in the same bucket iff they're anagrams," which sorting (or counting) guarantees exactly.

When to reach for it. "Group items by a derived signature" problems — anagrams, isomorphic strings, bucketing by digit sum or frequency profile — whenever equality after some transformation defines the equivalence classes you need.

Complexity. Time O(n·k log k) with sort-based keys (n strings, average length k), or O(n·k) with a fixed-alphabet count-tuple key. Space O(n·k) to store all strings across the map's buckets.

Pitfalls & variations. Using the raw string as the key misses anagrams entirely; sorting each string is simple but O(k log k) — a 26-length count array as the key is O(k) and faster for pure-lowercase inputs; building a string-encoded count key (e.g. "1a2b0c…") works but is easy to get delimiter bugs on vs. just using a tuple/array directly as the key.

Trace input: strs = ["eat","tea","tan","ate","nat","bat"] — group all anagrams together (the classic LC49 example).

Stepwordsorted keymap state after insert
1eataet{aet:[eat]}
2teaaet{aet:[eat, tea]}
3tanant{aet:[eat, tea], ant:[tan]}
4ateaet{aet:[eat, tea, ate], ant:[tan]}
5natant{aet:[eat, tea, ate], ant:[tan, nat]}
6batabt{aet:[eat, tea, ate], ant:[tan, nat], abt:[bat]}

Result: [["eat","tea","ate"], ["tan","nat"], ["bat"]] — matches LeetCode's own example. The sorted key is computed once per word and is all the hashmap ever sees; the original word is just the payload riding along in the bucket.

2 · Two Pointers

Tests whether you can collapse an O(n²) nested-loop search into O(n) by walking two indices toward (or away from) each other over a sorted or partitionable array — the tell is "sorted array" plus "pair/triplet that satisfies a condition".

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Valid Palindrome Easy AmazonMicrosoftMeta two pointers skip non-alphanumeric
2 Two Sum II - Input Array Is Sorted ★ Medium AmazonAdobeMicrosoft converge pointers on sorted array
3 3Sum ★ Medium AmazonMetaMicrosoft sort + fixed anchor + two pointers
4 Container With Most Water ★ Medium AmazonGoogleBloomberg move shorter wall inward greedily
5 Trapping Rain Water ★ Hard AmazonGoogleApple two pointers with running left/right max
6 Remove Duplicates from Sorted Array Easy AmazonMicrosoftMeta in-place write pointer
7 Sort Colors Medium AmazonMetaGoogle Dutch national flag three pointers
