Worked example — 3Sum (LC 15, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, Bloomberg

Problem. Given an integer array, return all unique triplets [a, b, c] such that a + b + c == 0.

Idea. Sort the array first. Fix each element nums[i] as the smallest of the triplet, then run the classic two-pointer sweep on the remaining sorted suffix to find pairs summing to -nums[i], skipping duplicate values at every level to keep results unique.

class Solution {
    public List<List<Integer>> threeSum(int[] nums) {
        Arrays.sort(nums);
        List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < nums.length - 2; i++) {
            if (i > 0 && nums[i] == nums[i - 1]) continue;
            if (nums[i] > 0) break;
            int lo = i + 1, hi = nums.length - 1;
            while (lo < hi) {
                int sum = nums[i] + nums[lo] + nums[hi];
                if (sum < 0) {
                    lo++;
                } else if (sum > 0) {
                    hi--;
                } else {
                    res.add(Arrays.asList(nums[i], nums[lo], nums[hi]));
                    lo++;
                    hi--;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[lo] == nums[lo - 1]) lo++;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[hi] == nums[hi + 1]) hi--;
                }
            }
        }
        return res;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<int>> threeSum(vector<int>& nums) {
        sort(nums.begin(), nums.end());
        vector<vector<int>> res;
        for (int i = 0; i < (int)nums.size() - 2; i++) {
            if (i > 0 && nums[i] == nums[i - 1]) continue;
            if (nums[i] > 0) break;
            int lo = i + 1, hi = nums.size() - 1;
            while (lo < hi) {
                int sum = nums[i] + nums[lo] + nums[hi];
                if (sum < 0) {
                    lo++;
                } else if (sum > 0) {
                    hi--;
                } else {
                    res.push_back({nums[i], nums[lo], nums[hi]});
                    lo++;
                    hi--;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[lo] == nums[lo - 1]) lo++;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[hi] == nums[hi + 1]) hi--;
                }
            }
        }
        return res;
    }
};
class Solution:
    def threeSum(self, nums: List[int]) -> List[List[int]]:
        nums.sort()
        res = []
        for i in range(len(nums) - 2):
            if i > 0 and nums[i] == nums[i - 1]:
                continue
            if nums[i] > 0:
                break
            lo, hi = i + 1, len(nums) - 1
            while lo < hi:
                total = nums[i] + nums[lo] + nums[hi]
                if total < 0:
                    lo += 1
                elif total > 0:
                    hi -= 1
                else:
                    res.append([nums[i], nums[lo], nums[hi]])
                    lo += 1
                    hi -= 1
                    while lo < hi and nums[lo] == nums[lo - 1]:
                        lo += 1
                    while lo < hi and nums[hi] == nums[hi + 1]:
                        hi -= 1
        return res
class Solution {
    threeSum(nums: number[]): number[][] {
        nums.sort((a, b) => a - b);
        const res: number[][] = [];
        for (let i = 0; i < nums.length - 2; i++) {
            if (i > 0 && nums[i] === nums[i - 1]) continue;
            if (nums[i] > 0) break;
            let lo = i + 1, hi = nums.length - 1;
            while (lo < hi) {
                const sum = nums[i] + nums[lo] + nums[hi];
                if (sum < 0) {
                    lo++;
                } else if (sum > 0) {
                    hi--;
                } else {
                    res.push([nums[i], nums[lo], nums[hi]]);
                    lo++;
                    hi--;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[lo] === nums[lo - 1]) lo++;
                    while (lo < hi && nums[hi] === nums[hi + 1]) hi--;
                }
            }
        }
        return res;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n²) Space O(1) extra — O(n log n) sort plus an O(n) two-pointer sweep for each of n anchors.

Walkthrough. Input [-1,0,1,2,-1,-4] sorts to [-4,-1,-1,0,1,2]:

i (anchor)lo, hisumaction
i=0 (-4)lo=1(-1), hi=5(2)-3sum<0, lo++ ... no triplet found for -4
i=1 (-1)lo=2(-1), hi=5(2)0record [-1,-1,2], then lo++, hi--
i=1 (-1)lo=3(0), hi=4(1)0record [-1,0,1], lo/hi cross, stop
i=2 (-1)skipped-duplicate of nums[1], continue

Result: [[-1,-1,2], [-1,0,1]].

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. After sorting, fixing the smallest element at index i and sweeping l/r inward keeps the triplet sum monotonic in each pointer — moving l right only increases the sum, moving r left only decreases it. That monotonicity means no combination is ever re-checked, turning an O(n²) inner search into O(n) per fixed i.

When to reach for it. "Find pairs/triples with a target sum or relationship" on a sorted (or sortable) array, especially when brute force is O(n²) or O(n³) and duplicate results must be deduped — 3Sum, 3Sum Closest, Container With Most Water, pair sum in a sorted array.

Complexity. Time O(n²): O(n log n) sort + O(n) outer loop × O(n) inner two-pointer sweep. Space O(1) extra (or O(log n) for the sort's recursion, depending on implementation).

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to skip duplicate values for i, l, and r — three separate dedup checks, easy to miss one and emit duplicate triplets; not sorting first (two pointers require sortedness to guarantee monotonic sum movement); letting i run past n-3, leaving fewer than two elements for l/r.

Trace input: nums = [-1, 0, 1, 2, -1, -4] → sorted [-4, -1, -1, 0, 1, 2] — find all unique triplets summing to 0 (classic LC15 example).

StepilrsumAction
0sort———sorted = [-4,-1,-1,0,1,2]
10 (-4)15-4-1+2=-3-3<0 → l++
20 (-4)25-4-1+2=-3-3<0 → l++
30 (-4)35-4+0+2=-2-2<0 → l++
40 (-4)45-4+1+2=-1-1<0 → l++ → l==r, done with i=0
51 (-1)25-1-1+2=0match [-1,-1,2] → l++, r--
61 (-1)34-1+0+1=0match [-1,0,1] → l++, r-- → l>r, done with i=1
72 (-1)———duplicate of nums[1] → skip i
83 (0)450+1+2=33>0 → r-- → l==r, loop ends (i at n-3)

Result: [[-1,-1,2], [-1,0,1]] — matches LeetCode's own example exactly. The trace shows the dedup skip at i=2 and the strictly monotonic pointer moves that guarantee every valid triplet is found exactly once.

3 · Sliding Window

Tests whether you can maintain a contiguous subarray/substring incrementally — expanding the right edge and shrinking the left edge in response to a constraint — instead of re-scanning from scratch; the tell is "longest/shortest/max substring or subarray satisfying X".

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Best Time to Buy and Sell Stock ★ Easy AmazonMetaGoogle track running min price / max profit
2 Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters ★ Medium AmazonMetaBloomberg expand/shrink window with a set
3 Longest Repeating Character Replacement Medium AmazonGoogleMicrosoft window valid while size - maxFreq <= k
4 Permutation in String Medium AmazonMicrosoftUber fixed-size window char-count match
5 Minimum Window Substring ★ Hard AmazonMetaGoogle variable window with need/have counters
6 Sliding Window Maximum ★ Hard AmazonGoogleTikTok monotonic deque of candidate maxima
7 Max Consecutive Ones III Medium GoogleAmazon window with a flip/zero budget
8 Fruit Into Baskets Medium AmazonGoogle window with at-most-2-distinct types

Worked example — Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters (LC 3, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Bloomberg, Adobe

Problem. Given a string s, find the length of the longest substring without repeating characters.

Idea. Keep a window [left, right] and a set of characters currently inside it. Advance right one step at a time; whenever the new character is already in the window, shrink from left until the duplicate is evicted. The window is always duplicate-free, so its length at every step is a candidate answer.

class Solution {
    public int lengthOfLongestSubstring(String s) {
        Set<Character> window = new HashSet<>();
        int left = 0, best = 0;
        for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
            char c = s.charAt(right);
            while (window.contains(c)) {
                window.remove(s.charAt(left));
                left++;
            }
            window.add(c);
            best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
        }
        return best;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int lengthOfLongestSubstring(string s) {
        unordered_set<char> window;
        int left = 0, best = 0;
        for (int right = 0; right < (int)s.length(); right++) {
            char c = s[right];
            while (window.count(c)) {
                window.erase(s[left]);
                left++;
            }
            window.insert(c);
            best = max(best, right - left + 1);
        }
        return best;
    }
};
class Solution:
    def lengthOfLongestSubstring(self, s: str) -> int:
        window = set()
        left = best = 0
        for right in range(len(s)):
            c = s[right]
            while c in window:
                window.remove(s[left])
                left += 1
            window.add(c)
            best = max(best, right - left + 1)
        return best
class Solution {
    lengthOfLongestSubstring(s: string): number {
        const window = new Set<string>();
        let left = 0, best = 0;
        for (let right = 0; right < s.length; right++) {
            const c = s[right];
            while (window.has(c)) {
                window.delete(s[left]);
                left++;
            }
            window.add(c);
            best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
        }
        return best;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n) Space O(min(n, alphabet)) — left and right each advance at most n times total; the set holds at most one entry per distinct character in the window.

Walkthrough. Input "abcabcbb":

rightcharwindow beforeactionbest
0a{}add a1
1b{a}add b2
2c{a,b}add c3
3a{a,b,c}duplicate: evict a (left=1), then add a3
4b{b,c,a}duplicate: evict b (left=2), then add b3

Answer: 3 (substring "abc").

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The window [left, right] is maintained as an invariant: it never contains a repeated character. When character at right was last seen at an index >= left, that occurrence is inside the current window, so left jumps to one past it — a single O(1) jump rather than shrinking one step at a time. Because left only ever moves forward, the whole scan is O(n).

When to reach for it. Contiguous-subarray/substring problems where the validity of a window can be checked and updated incrementally as one pointer advances (no repeats, sum ≤ k, at most k distinct characters) and the answer is a min/max length or a count of valid windows.

Complexity. O(n) time — both pointers only move rightward over n characters combined; O(min(n, Σ)) space for the last-seen-index map, bounded by the alphabet size Σ.

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting the lastSeen[c] >= left guard lets left jump backward on a stale index. Off-by-one when computing right - left + 1. The "at most k distinct characters" variant needs a frequency-count shrink loop instead of a single jump — a common source of confusion with this exact problem.

Trace input: s = "abcabcbb" — find the length of the longest substring with no repeated characters.

StepicharlastSeen[char]left beforeactionleft afterwindowmaxLen
0————init0""0
10anone0record a@00"a"1
21bnone0record b@10"ab"2
32cnone0record c@20"abc"3
43a000 >= left(0) → left=1; record a@31"bca"3

65c222 >= left(2) → left=3; record c@53"abc"3

87b656 >= left(5) → left=7; record b@77"b"3

Result: maxLen = 3 (substring "abc"). The trace shows left only ever jumping forward past a stale duplicate, never re-scanning already-cleared ground.

4 · Stack

Tests whether you recognize "most recent unmatched thing" or "next element that breaks a trend" problems — nested/matching structures and monotonic scans both collapse to O(n) with a stack instead of repeated backward scans.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Valid Parentheses ★ Easy AmazonMetaMicrosoftBloomberg stack of expected closing brackets
2 Min Stack ★ Medium AmazonGoogleApple stack paired with running min
3 Evaluate Reverse Polish Notation ★ Medium AmazonMetaGoogle operand stack, pop two on operator
4 Generate Parentheses ★ Medium AmazonMetaGoogle backtracking with open/close budget
5 Daily Temperatures ★ Medium AmazonMetaBloomberg monotonic decreasing stack of indices
6 Car Fleet Medium GoogleAmazon stack of arrival times by position

Worked example — Daily Temperatures (LC 739, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Bloomberg, Google

Problem. Given daily temperatures, return for each day the number of days until a warmer temperature, or 0 if none exists.

Idea. Walk left to right keeping a stack of indices whose answer is still unknown, with temperatures in decreasing order down the stack. When the current temperature beats the temperature at the top index, that index's wait is resolved (current index minus stored index); pop and repeat, then push the current index.

class Solution {
    public int[] dailyTemperatures(int[] temperatures) {
        int n = temperatures.length;
        int[] res = new int[n];
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(); // indices, temps decreasing
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            while (!stack.isEmpty() && temperatures[i] > temperatures[stack.peek()]) {
                int idx = stack.pop();
                res[idx] = i - idx;
            }
            stack.push(i);
        }
        return res;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    vector<int> dailyTemperatures(vector<int>& temperatures) {
        int n = temperatures.size();
        vector<int> res(n, 0);
        vector<int> stack; // indices, temps decreasing
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            while (!stack.empty() && temperatures[i] > temperatures[stack.back()]) {
                int idx = stack.back();
                stack.pop_back();
                res[idx] = i - idx;
            }
            stack.push_back(i);
        }
        return res;
    }
};
class Solution:
    def dailyTemperatures(self, temperatures: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(temperatures)
        res = [0] * n
        stack = []  # indices, temps decreasing
        for i in range(n):
            while stack and temperatures[i] > temperatures[stack[-1]]:
                idx = stack.pop()
                res[idx] = i - idx
            stack.append(i)
        return res
class Solution {
    dailyTemperatures(temperatures: number[]): number[] {
        const n = temperatures.length;
        const res = new Array(n).fill(0);
        const stack: number[] = []; // indices, temps decreasing
        for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            while (stack.length > 0 && temperatures[i] > temperatures[stack[stack.length - 1]]) {
                const idx = stack.pop()!;
                res[idx] = i - idx;
            }
            stack.push(i);
        }
        return res;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n) Space O(n) — each index is pushed once and popped at most once.

Walkthrough. Input [73,74,75,71,69,72,76,73]:

itemppops (resolved)stack after (indices)
073-[0]
174pop 0 -> res[0]=1[1]
275pop 1 -> res[1]=1[2]
371-[2,3]
469-[2,3,4]
572pop 4 -> res[4]=1, pop 3 -> res[3]=2[2,5]
676pop 5 -> res[5]=1, pop 2 -> res[2]=4[6]
773-[6,7]

Result: [1,1,4,2,1,1,0,0].

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. The stack holds indices of days whose "next warmer day" is still unknown, kept in decreasing order of temperature. When a new, warmer day arrives it resolves every cooler pending day still on the stack in one pass — each index is pushed once and popped at most once, so the total work across all pops is O(n) even though a single day can trigger many pops.

When to reach for it. "Next greater/smaller element to the right" problems, and anything phrased as a running span or histogram comparison (stock span, largest rectangle, next warmer/colder day) — the signal is needing, for every element, the nearest qualifying element ahead of it.

Complexity. O(n) time — each index enters and leaves the stack exactly once; O(n) space for the stack and the answer array.

Pitfalls & variations. Storing values instead of indices on the stack loses the distance needed for the answer. Using if instead of while when popping misses multiple resolutions triggered by one new day. Swapping the comparison direction turns this into "next colder day" instead.

Trace input: temperatures = [73,74,75,71,69,72,76,73] — for each day, how many days until a strictly warmer day (0 if never).

Stepitempstack before (idx:temp)pops & ans updatesstack after
0———init ans=[0,0,0,0,0,0,0,0][]
1073[]no pops; push 0[0:73]
2174[0:73]74 > 73 → pop 0, ans[0]=1-0=1; push 1[1:74]
3275[1:74]75 > 74 → pop 1, ans[1]=2-1=1; push 2[2:75]
4371[2:75]71 < 75, no pop; push 3[2:75, 3:71]
5469[2:75, 3:71]69 < 71, no pop; push 4[2:75, 3:71, 4:69]

7676[2:75, 5:72]76>72 pop5 ans[5]=1; 76>75 pop2 ans[2]=4; stack empty; push 6[6:76]
8773[6:76]73 < 76, no pop; push 7[6:76, 7:73]

Result: ans = [1,1,4,2,1,1,0,0]. Indices 6 and 7 keep their default 0 — nothing warmer ever comes, so they are never popped, which is exactly what the leftover stack contents mean.

5 · Binary Search

Tests whether you can spot a monotonic "yes/no" boundary — not just in a sorted array but in the space of possible answers — and drive it out in O(log n); the tell is "sorted", "rotated sorted", or "minimize/maximize the smallest value that still works".

Worked example — Search in Rotated Sorted Array (LC 33, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, Bloomberg

Problem. Given a sorted array rotated at an unknown pivot and a target value, return the index of the target in O(log n) time, or -1 if it is not present.

Idea. At every midpoint, one of the two halves [lo, mid] or [mid, hi] must still be internally sorted even though the whole array isn't. Determine which half is sorted by comparing nums[lo] to nums[mid], then check whether the target falls inside that sorted half's value range to decide which side to keep.

class Solution {
    public int search(int[] nums, int target) {
        int lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
        while (lo <= hi) {
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (nums[mid] == target) return mid;
            if (nums[lo] <= nums[mid]) {
                if (nums[lo] <= target && target < nums[mid]) {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                } else {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                }
            } else {
                if (nums[mid] < target && target <= nums[hi]) {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                } else {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                }
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int search(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
        int lo = 0, hi = nums.size() - 1;
        while (lo <= hi) {
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (nums[mid] == target) return mid;
            if (nums[lo] <= nums[mid]) {
                if (nums[lo] <= target && target < nums[mid]) {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                } else {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                }
            } else {
                if (nums[mid] < target && target <= nums[hi]) {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                } else {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                }
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }
};
class Solution:
    def search(self, nums: List[int], target: int) -> int:
        lo, hi = 0, len(nums) - 1
        while lo <= hi:
            mid = lo + (hi - lo) // 2
            if nums[mid] == target:
                return mid
            if nums[lo] <= nums[mid]:
                if nums[lo] <= target < nums[mid]:
                    hi = mid - 1
                else:
                    lo = mid + 1
            else:
                if nums[mid] < target <= nums[hi]:
                    lo = mid + 1
                else:
                    hi = mid - 1
        return -1
class Solution {
    search(nums: number[], target: number): number {
        let lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
        while (lo <= hi) {
            const mid = lo + Math.floor((hi - lo) / 2);
            if (nums[mid] === target) return mid;
            if (nums[lo] <= nums[mid]) {
                if (nums[lo] <= target && target < nums[mid]) {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                } else {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                }
            } else {
                if (nums[mid] < target && target <= nums[hi]) {
                    lo = mid + 1;
                } else {
                    hi = mid - 1;
                }
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(log n) Space O(1) — every iteration discards half of the remaining search space.

Walkthrough. Input nums = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], target = 0:

lo, mid, hinums[lo..hi] viewsorted halfdecision
0, 3, 6[4,5,6,7,0,1,2]left [4,5,6,7] since nums[0]<=nums[3]target 0 not in [4,7) -> go right, lo=4
4, 5, 6[0,1,2]left [0,1] since nums[4]<=nums[5]target 0 in [0,1) -> go left, hi=4
4, 4, 4[0]nums[mid]==targetreturn mid = 4

Answer: index 4.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. At any midpoint of a rotated sorted array, at least one half (relative to mid) is guaranteed to be internally sorted. Comparing nums[lo] to nums[mid] identifies which half is sorted; a plain range check then tells you whether the target could be in that sorted half. Either way you can discard one half every iteration, so the search stays O(log n) despite the rotation.

When to reach for it. Arrays described as "sorted but rotated" or, more generally, any search space with a monotonic predicate ("too small / candidate / too big") you can evaluate at a single index without scanning — that predicate is what you're actually binary searching on.

Complexity. O(log n) time — the search space halves every iteration; O(1) space.

Pitfalls & variations. Using strict < instead of <= when comparing nums[lo] and nums[mid] breaks on 2-element windows. Duplicate values (LC81) can make both halves look "equal," defeating the sortedness test and forcing a linear fallback (lo++/hi--). Miscomputing mid±1 causes infinite loops.

Trace input: nums = [5,6,7,8,9,10,1,2,3], target = 3 — find the index of target in a rotated sorted array.

Steplohimidnums[mid]which half sortedtarget in range?action
008————start
10849nums[0]=5 <= 9 → left sorted3 in [5,9]? nolo = mid+1 = 5
25861nums[5]=10 <= 1? no → right sorted3 in [1,3]? yeslo = mid+1 = 7
37872nums[7]=2 <= 2 → left sorted3 in [2,2]? nolo = mid+1 = 8
48883—nums[mid] == targetreturn 8

Result: return 8 (nums[8] = 3). The trace visits both branches of the algorithm — "left half sorted" (steps 1, 3) and "right half sorted" (step 2) — converging in ⌈log₂9⌉ ≈ 4 iterations.

Worked example — Reorder List (LC 143, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Microsoft.

Problem. Given the head of a singly linked list L0 → L1 → ... → Ln-1 → Ln, reorder it in place to L0 → Ln → L1 → Ln-1 → L2 → Ln-2 → ... You may not modify node values, only rewire next pointers, and you must use O(1) extra space.

Idea. Three classic sub-routines chained together: (1) locate the midpoint with slow/fast pointers, (2) reverse the second half in place, (3) splice the two halves together by alternating one node from each side. Each step is a well-known O(n) primitive, so the composition stays O(n) time, O(1) space.

class ListNode {
    int val;
    ListNode next;
    ListNode(int val) { this.val = val; }
}

class Solution {
    public void reorderList(ListNode head) {
        if (head == null || head.next == null) return;

        // 1. find the middle (slow ends on the last node of the first half)
        ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
        while (fast.next != null && fast.next.next != null) {
            slow = slow.next;
            fast = fast.next.next;
        }

        // 2. split and reverse the second half
        ListNode second = slow.next;
        slow.next = null;
        ListNode prev = null;
        while (second != null) {
            ListNode next = second.next;
            second.next = prev;
            prev = second;
            second = next;
        }

        // 3. merge the two halves alternately
        ListNode first = head, secondHalf = prev;
        while (secondHalf != null) {
            ListNode tmp1 = first.next;
            ListNode tmp2 = secondHalf.next;
            first.next = secondHalf;
            secondHalf.next = tmp1;
            first = tmp1;
            secondHalf = tmp2;
        }
    }
}

struct ListNode {
    int val;
    ListNode *next;
    ListNode(int val) : val(val), next(nullptr) {}
};

class Solution {
public:
    void reorderList(ListNode* head) {
        if (head == nullptr || head->next == nullptr) return;

        // 1. find the middle (slow ends on the last node of the first half)
        ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head;
        while (fast->next != nullptr && fast->next->next != nullptr) {
            slow = slow->next;
            fast = fast->next->next;
        }

        // 2. split and reverse the second half
        ListNode* second = slow->next;
        slow->next = nullptr;
        ListNode* prev = nullptr;
        while (second != nullptr) {
            ListNode* next = second->next;
            second->next = prev;
            prev = second;
            second = next;
        }

        // 3. merge the two halves alternately
        ListNode *first = head, *secondHalf = prev;
        while (secondHalf != nullptr) {
            ListNode* tmp1 = first->next;
            ListNode* tmp2 = secondHalf->next;
            first->next = secondHalf;
            secondHalf->next = tmp1;
            first = tmp1;
            secondHalf = tmp2;
        }
    }
};
class ListNode:
    def __init__(self, val=0):
        self.val = val
        self.next = None

class Solution:
    def reorderList(self, head: ListNode) -> None:
        if head is None or head.next is None:
            return

        # 1. find the middle (slow ends on the last node of the first half)
        slow, fast = head, head
        while fast.next is not None and fast.next.next is not None:
            slow = slow.next
            fast = fast.next.next

        # 2. split and reverse the second half
        second = slow.next
        slow.next = None
        prev = None
        while second is not None:
            next = second.next
            second.next = prev
            prev = second
            second = next

        # 3. merge the two halves alternately
        first, secondHalf = head, prev
        while secondHalf is not None:
            tmp1 = first.next
            tmp2 = secondHalf.next
            first.next = secondHalf
            secondHalf.next = tmp1
            first = tmp1
            secondHalf = tmp2
class ListNode {
    val: number;
    next: ListNode | null;
    constructor(val: number) {
        this.val = val;
        this.next = null;
    }
}

class Solution {
    reorderList(head: ListNode | null): void {
        if (head === null || head.next === null) return;

        // 1. find the middle (slow ends on the last node of the first half)
        let slow: ListNode | null = head, fast: ListNode | null = head;
        while (fast!.next !== null && fast!.next!.next !== null) {
            slow = slow!.next;
            fast = fast!.next!.next;
        }

        // 2. split and reverse the second half
        let second: ListNode | null = slow!.next;
        slow!.next = null;
        let prev: ListNode | null = null;
        while (second !== null) {
            const next: ListNode | null = second.next;
            second.next = prev;
            prev = second;
            second = next;
        }

        // 3. merge the two halves alternately
        let first: ListNode | null = head, secondHalf: ListNode | null = prev;
        while (secondHalf !== null) {
            const tmp1: ListNode | null = first!.next;
            const tmp2: ListNode | null = secondHalf.next;
            first!.next = secondHalf;
            secondHalf.next = tmp1;
            first = tmp1;
            secondHalf = tmp2;
        }
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n) Space O(1) — a constant number of single passes over the list, all pointer rewiring with no auxiliary structures.

Walkthrough. Input: 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5.

StepState
Find middleslow lands on 3, so first half = 1→2→3, second = 4→5
Reverse second half4→5 becomes 5→4
Merge iteration 1first=1, secondHalf=5 → 1→5, first advances to 2
Merge iteration 2first=2, secondHalf=4 → 2→4, first advances to 3
secondHalf exhaustedloop stops; 3's original next (null) already terminates the list
Result1 → 5 → 2 → 4 → 3

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Reordering L0→Ln→L1→Ln-1→… decomposes into three well-known O(n) linked-list primitives — find the middle with slow/fast pointers, reverse the second half with iterative prev/cur rewiring, then merge the two halves by alternating one node from each — all done via pointer surgery with no auxiliary array.

When to reach for it. Any problem manipulating a singly linked list's structure based on position or symmetry (reorder, palindrome check, k-group reversal): fast/slow pointers locate the middle, prev/cur/next reverses a segment, and a two-pointer interleave merges two lists.

Complexity. O(n) time across the three linear passes; O(1) extra space — everything is in-place pointer rewiring, no recursion stack or array copy.

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to cut slow.next = None before reversing the second half creates a cycle. Odd vs. even length changes which node slow lands on and thus which half is longer. Overwriting .next during reversal before saving the next pointer loses the rest of the list.

Trace input: 1→2→3→4→5 — reorder in place to L0,Ln,L1,Ln-1,…

StepPhasePointers / stateList snapshotNote
0startslow=1, fast=11-2-3-4-5n=5 (odd)
1find-middleslow=2, fast=31-2-3-4-5advance slow ×1, fast ×2
2find-middleslow=3, fast=51-2-3-4-5fast.next.next is null → stop; middle = 3
3splitfirst=1-2-3, second=4-5(cut)set slow.next = None
4reverse 2nd halfprev=None→4, cur=4→5second: 4-5 → 5,4 (partial)reversed so far: 4
5reverse 2nd halfprev=4→5, cur=5→Nonesecond fully reversed: 5-4reversal done
6mergep1=1, p2=51-5attach 1.next=5; advance both
7mergep1=2, p2=41-5-2-4attach 2.next=4; advance both
8mergep1=3, p2=None1-5-2-4-3p2 exhausted; 3 becomes the tail

Result: Final list 1→5→2→4→3. Because n is odd, the second half (4-5) is one node shorter than the first, so after merging, the leftover node 3 naturally becomes the tail with no special-casing needed.

Worked example — Validate Binary Search Tree (LC 98, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Microsoft, Apple, Bloomberg.

Problem. Given the root of a binary tree, determine whether it is a valid binary search tree — for every node, all values in its left subtree must be strictly less than the node's value and all values in its right subtree must be strictly greater.

Idea. Checking only left.val < node.val < right.val at each node is insufficient — a node deep in the left subtree could still violate the ancestor's bound. Instead thread a valid (low, high) range down through the recursion: each node must fall strictly inside the range inherited from its ancestors, and it narrows that range for its own children.

class TreeNode {
    int val;
    TreeNode left, right;
    TreeNode(int val) { this.val = val; }
}

class Solution {
    public boolean isValidBST(TreeNode root) {
        return valid(root, null, null);
    }

    private boolean valid(TreeNode node, Integer low, Integer high) {
        if (node == null) return true;
        if ((low != null && node.val <= low) || (high != null && node.val >= high)) {
            return false;
        }
        return valid(node.left, low, node.val) && valid(node.right, node.val, high);
    }
}

struct TreeNode {
    int val;
    TreeNode *left, *right;
    TreeNode(int val) : val(val), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
};

class Solution {
public:
    bool isValidBST(TreeNode* root) {
        return valid(root, std::nullopt, std::nullopt);
    }

private:
    bool valid(TreeNode* node, std::optional<int> low, std::optional<int> high) {
        if (node == nullptr) return true;
        if ((low.has_value() && node->val <= low.value()) || (high.has_value() && node->val >= high.value())) {
            return false;
        }
        return valid(node->left, low, node->val) && valid(node->right, node->val, high);
    }
};
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, val=0):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None

class Solution:
    def isValidBST(self, root: Optional[TreeNode]) -> bool:
        return self._valid(root, None, None)

    def _valid(self, node, low, high):
        if node is None:
            return True
        if (low is not None and node.val <= low) or (high is not None and node.val >= high):
            return False
        return self._valid(node.left, low, node.val) and self._valid(node.right, node.val, high)
class TreeNode {
    val: number;
    left: TreeNode | null;
    right: TreeNode | null;
    constructor(val: number) {
        this.val = val;
        this.left = null;
        this.right = null;
    }
}

class Solution {
    isValidBST(root: TreeNode | null): boolean {
        return this.valid(root, null, null);
    }

    private valid(node: TreeNode | null, low: number | null, high: number | null): boolean {
        if (node === null) return true;
        if ((low !== null && node.val <= low) || (high !== null && node.val >= high)) {
            return false;
        }
        return this.valid(node.left, low, node.val) && this.valid(node.right, node.val, high);
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n) Space O(h) — every node is visited once; space is the recursion stack, proportional to tree height h.

Walkthrough. Input tree: root 5, left child 1, right child 4 (whose children are 3 and 6).

CallBounds (low, high)Result
valid(5, null, null)(-∞, +∞)5 in range, recurse both sides
valid(1, null, 5)(-∞, 5)1 < 5, valid leaf
valid(4, 5, null)(5, +∞)4 ≤ 5 → false
Overall—returns false — the right subtree of 5 must contain only values > 5, but 4 breaks that

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A tree is a valid BST iff every node's value lies strictly inside the (low, high) bound inherited from all its ancestors, not just its immediate parent. A local check like node.val > node.left.val is insufficient because a node several levels down can violate a constraint set far above it; passing tightened bounds down the recursion captures that global constraint in O(1) work per node.

When to reach for it. Tree problems where a tempting local sibling/parent comparison is actually wrong because the real constraint spans multiple ancestor levels — BST validation is the classic example; similar bound-propagation shows up in range/coloring problems on trees.

Complexity. O(n) time — every node is visited once; O(h) space for the recursion or explicit stack, where h is the tree height.

Pitfalls & variations. Checking only node.val vs. its direct children's values misses violations from grandparents. Using <= instead of < silently allows duplicate values a strict BST forbids. Not short-circuiting on the first violation wastes work scanning subtrees that can no longer change the answer.

Trace input: root = 5, left = 3 (left = 1, right = 6), right = 8 — is this tree a valid binary search tree?

StepPopped call (node, low, high)CheckResultPushed children
0init——stack = [(5, -∞, +∞)]
1(5, -∞, +∞)-∞ < 5 < +∞truepush (8, 5, +∞), push (3, -∞, 5)
2(3, -∞, 5)-∞ < 3 < 5truepush (6, 3, 5), push (1, -∞, 3)
3(1, -∞, 3)-∞ < 1 < 3true (leaf)nothing to push
4(6, 3, 5)3 < 6 < 5 → 6 < 5 is falseINVALID — stop(8, 5, +∞) never popped

Result: Returns false. Node 6 only violates the tree once it inherits the tight upper bound 5 handed down from the root two levels above — a purely local check against its immediate parent (6 > 3) would have wrongly passed it.

8 · Tries

Tests prefix-shared storage and retrieval over strings — the tell is repeated work on "starts with", autocomplete, dictionary lookups, or word-search over a fixed word list; reach for a 26-ary (or map-based) trie node with an end-of-word flag.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Implement Trie (Prefix Tree) ★ Medium AmazonGoogleMicrosoft array-of-children trie node
2 Design Add and Search Words Data Structure ★ Medium AmazonGoogleUber trie DFS with wildcard '.' branching
3 Word Search II ★ Hard AmazonGoogleMicrosoft trie + board backtracking with pruning
4 Longest Word in Dictionary Medium AmazonGoogle trie build + DFS on complete-prefix chains

Worked example — Implement Trie (Prefix Tree) (LC 208, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Google, Microsoft.

Problem. Implement a trie with insert(word), search(word) (exact match), and startsWith(prefix) (prefix match), all operating on lowercase English letters.

Idea. Each trie node holds a fixed-size array of 26 child pointers (one per letter) plus a boolean marking "a word ends here". Insert and search both just walk character by character, either creating or following child links; startsWith is identical to search minus the end-of-word check.

class Trie {
    private Trie[] children;
    private boolean isEnd;

    public Trie() {
        children = new Trie[26];
        isEnd = false;
    }

    public void insert(String word) {
        Trie node = this;
        for (char c : word.toCharArray()) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node.children[i] == null) node.children[i] = new Trie();
            node = node.children[i];
        }
        node.isEnd = true;
    }

    public boolean search(String word) {
        Trie node = find(word);
        return node != null && node.isEnd;
    }

    public boolean startsWith(String prefix) {
        return find(prefix) != null;
    }

    private Trie find(String s) {
        Trie node = this;
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node.children[i] == null) return null;
            node = node.children[i];
        }
        return node;
    }
}

class Trie {
private:
    Trie* children[26];
    bool isEnd;

public:
    Trie() {
        for (int i = 0; i < 26; i++) children[i] = nullptr;
        isEnd = false;
    }

    void insert(string word) {
        Trie* node = this;
        for (char c : word) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node->children[i] == nullptr) node->children[i] = new Trie();
            node = node->children[i];
        }
        node->isEnd = true;
    }

    bool search(string word) {
        Trie* node = find(word);
        return node != nullptr && node->isEnd;
    }

    bool startsWith(string prefix) {
        return find(prefix) != nullptr;
    }

private:
    Trie* find(string s) {
        Trie* node = this;
        for (char c : s) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node->children[i] == nullptr) return nullptr;
            node = node->children[i];
        }
        return node;
    }
};
class Trie:
    def __init__(self):
        self.children = [None] * 26
        self.isEnd = False

    def insert(self, word: str) -> None:
        node = self
        for c in word:
            i = ord(c) - ord('a')
            if node.children[i] is None:
                node.children[i] = Trie()
            node = node.children[i]
        node.isEnd = True

    def search(self, word: str) -> bool:
        node = self._find(word)
        return node is not None and node.isEnd

    def startsWith(self, prefix: str) -> bool:
        return self._find(prefix) is not None

    def _find(self, s: str):
        node = self
        for c in s:
            i = ord(c) - ord('a')
            if node.children[i] is None:
                return None
            node = node.children[i]
        return node
class Trie {
    private children: (Trie | null)[];
    private isEnd: boolean;

    constructor() {
        this.children = new Array(26).fill(null);
        this.isEnd = false;
    }

    insert(word: string): void {
        let node: Trie = this;
        for (const c of word) {
            const i = c.charCodeAt(0) - 'a'.charCodeAt(0);
            if (node.children[i] === null) node.children[i] = new Trie();
            node = node.children[i]!;
        }
        node.isEnd = true;
    }

    search(word: string): boolean {
        const node = this.find(word);
        return node !== null && node.isEnd;
    }

    startsWith(prefix: string): boolean {
        return this.find(prefix) !== null;
    }

    private find(s: string): Trie | null {
        let node: Trie = this;
        for (const c of s) {
            const i = c.charCodeAt(0) - 'a'.charCodeAt(0);
            if (node.children[i] === null) return null;
            node = node.children[i]!;
        }
        return node;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(L) Space O(N·L) — each call costs the length L of the word/prefix; total space is proportional to the number of inserted words N times their average length (bounded by shared prefixes).

Walkthrough. Operations: insert("apple"), search("app"), startsWith("app"), insert("app"), search("app").

CallWhat happensReturns
insert("apple")creates chain a-p-p-l-e, marks 'e' node isEnd=true—
search("app")walks a-p-p, reaches a real node but isEnd is false therefalse
startsWith("app")walks a-p-p, node exists regardless of isEndtrue
insert("app")walks existing a-p-p node, now marks its isEnd=true—
search("app")walks a-p-p, isEnd is now truetrue

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Each trie node is a map from character to child node plus an isEnd flag; words sharing a prefix share the same chain of nodes for that prefix. Insert/search/startsWith all just walk one character at a time from the root, so the cost depends only on the query's length, never on how many other words are stored.

When to reach for it. Repeated prefix queries over a set of strings — autocomplete, prefix counting, word-search boards, longest common prefix at scale — anywhere you'd otherwise repeatedly rescan prefixes of many strings.

Complexity. O(L) time per insert/search/startsWith, where L is the query length, independent of how many words are stored; O(total characters inserted) space in the worst case of no shared prefixes.

Pitfalls & variations. Conflating startsWith (path must exist) with search (path must exist AND the end node's isEnd must be true) is the classic bug. Forgetting to set isEnd makes every inserted word also satisfy prefix-only search. Using a fixed 26-slot array per node wastes memory for large or sparse alphabets versus a hashmap.

Trace input: insert("cat"); insert("car"); search("car"); search("ca"); startsWith("ca"); search("cats") — build the trie and answer each query.

StepOperationPath walkedNode changesReturn
0insert("cat")root→c→a→t (all new)create c, a, t; mark t.isEnd=true—
1insert("car")root→c→a (reused)→r (new)create r branching off shared "ca"; mark r.isEnd=true—
2search("car")root→c→a→rr exists and r.isEnd=truetrue
3search("ca")root→c→aa exists but a.isEnd=falsefalse
4startsWith("ca")root→c→aa exists (isEnd irrelevant)true
5search("cats")root→c→a→t, then need child "s"t has no child "s"false

Result: "cat" and "car" share the "ca" branch. The trace shows search("ca") failing (isEnd false) while startsWith("ca") succeeds on the identical path — exactly the distinction that motivates a separate end-of-word flag.

9 · Heap / Priority Queue

Tests efficient access to running min/max/median over a stream or collection — the tell is "kth largest/smallest", "top k", "closest k", "streaming median", or scheduling by priority; reach for a min-heap or max-heap sized to k, or twin heaps for medians.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Kth Largest Element in a Stream Easy AmazonGoogle size-k min-heap, top is answer
2 Last Stone Weight Easy AmazonApple max-heap, repeated pairwise smash
3 K Closest Points to Origin ★ Medium AmazonMicrosoftFacebook max-heap of size k by distance
4 Kth Largest Element in an Array ★ Medium AmazonMetaMicrosoftGoogle size-k min-heap or quickselect partition

Worked example — Kth Largest Element in an Array (LC 215, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, Google.

Problem. Given an integer array nums and an integer k, return the kth largest element in the array (counting from the largest, so k=1 is the maximum), without fully sorting the array.

Idea. Maintain a min-heap capped at size k. Push every number; whenever the heap grows past k, pop the smallest. After processing all n numbers, the heap holds exactly the k largest elements seen, and its smallest member (the top of a min-heap) is the kth largest overall.

import java.util.PriorityQueue;

class Solution {
    public int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
        PriorityQueue<Integer> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>();
        for (int n : nums) {
            minHeap.offer(n);
            if (minHeap.size() > k) {
                minHeap.poll();
            }
        }
        return minHeap.peek();
    }
}

#include <queue>

class Solution {
public:
    int findKthLargest(vector<int>& nums, int k) {
        priority_queue<int, vector<int>, greater<int>> minHeap;
        for (int n : nums) {
            minHeap.push(n);
            if (minHeap.size() > k) {
                minHeap.pop();
            }
        }
        return minHeap.top();
    }
};
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findKthLargest(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        minHeap = []
        for n in nums:
            heapq.heappush(minHeap, n)
            if len(minHeap) > k:
                heapq.heappop(minHeap)
        return minHeap[0]
class Solution {
    findKthLargest(nums: number[], k: number): number {
        const minHeap: number[] = [];
        for (const n of nums) {
            minHeap.push(n);
            minHeap.sort((a, b) => a - b);
            if (minHeap.length > k) {
                minHeap.shift();
            }
        }
        return minHeap[0];
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n log k) Space O(k) — each of n offers/polls costs O(log k) on a heap bounded to size k; a quickselect partition alternative achieves average O(n) time with O(1) extra space.

Walkthrough. Input: nums = [3,2,1,5,6,4], k = 2.

Number processedHeap contents (min-heap)Action
3[3]size ≤ k, keep
2[2,3]size ≤ k, keep
1[1,2,3] → pop 1 → [2,3]size > k, pop smallest
5[2,3,5] → pop 2 → [3,5]size > k, pop smallest
6[3,5,6] → pop 3 → [5,6]size > k, pop smallest
4[4,5,6] → pop 4 → [5,6]size > k, pop smallest
Resultheap = [5,6], top = 55 is the 2nd largest element

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. A min-heap capped at size k always holds exactly the k largest elements seen so far, with the smallest of those k sitting at the root. Any new element only needs to be compared against that single root: if it's bigger, it displaces the current minimum of the kept set; if not, it can't possibly be in the top k and is discarded. You never re-examine the other k-1 kept elements or anything already discarded.

When to reach for it. "Top-k" / "kth largest-or-smallest" problems, especially streaming or one-pass, where a full sort (O(n log n)) does more work than needed because you only care about k extremal elements, not a total order.

Complexity. O(n log k) time — n pushes/pops, each O(log k) on a heap bounded to size k; O(k) space, which is far better than full sort when k ≪ n.

Pitfalls & variations. Building a max-heap of all n elements instead of a min-heap capped at k works but costs O(n log n) time and O(n) space, missing the whole point. Popping before checking size > k is an off-by-one that leaves k+1 elements. Confusing "kth largest" (1-indexed from the top, e.g. k=1 is the max) with "kth smallest" flips the heap type needed.

Trace input: nums = [3,2,1,5,6,4], k = 2 — find the 2nd largest value.

Stepnumheap before (min-heap)actionheap after
0—[]init[]
13[]push 3 (size 1 ≤ k)[3]
22[3]push 2 (size 2 = k)[2,3]
31[2,3]push 1 → size 3 > k, pop min 1[2,3]
45[2,3]push 5 → size 3 > k, pop min 2[3,5]
56[3,5]push 6 → size 3 > k, pop min 3[5,6]
64[5,6]push 4 → size 3 > k, pop min 4[5,6]

Result: Final heap [5,6]; its root (min) 5 is the 2nd largest element. Notice numbers 1 and 4 get pushed and immediately popped straight back out — they never belonged in the top-k, so the heap never grows past size k.

10 · Backtracking

Tests systematic exploration of a decision tree with pruning; reach for it when the problem asks for "all possible" subsets, permutations, combinations, or valid placements and a brute-force search with undo is feasible.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Subsets ★ Medium AmazonMetaGoogle include/exclude recursion
2 Combination Sum ★ Medium AmazonSnowflakeUber unbounded pick, same-index revisit
3 Permutations Medium MicrosoftAmazonBloomberg swap-in-place / used[] array
4 Subsets II Medium MetaAmazon sort + skip duplicate siblings
5 Combination Sum II Medium AmazonMeta sort + skip duplicate siblings, bounded pick
6 Word Search ★ Medium MicrosoftAmazonBloomberg grid DFS with visited-mark/unmark
7 Palindrome Partitioning Medium AmazonMeta prefix-check + recurse on suffix
8 Letter Combinations of a Phone Number ★ Medium AmazonUberGoogle index-driven cartesian product
9 N-Queens Hard AmazonMicrosoftZenefits row-by-row placement + column/diagonal sets

Worked example — Subsets (LC 78, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Google, Bloomberg.

Problem. Given an integer array nums of unique elements, return all possible subsets (the power set), in any order. Input [1,2,3] produces 8 subsets including [] and [1,2,3].

Idea. Every element is either included or excluded from the current subset. Walk the array index by index, at each index branch into "take it" and "skip it", and record the running subset (a copy) at every recursive call — that alone visits all 2^n leaves of the decision tree. Undo the choice (backtrack) after each branch so the shared list is reused correctly across siblings.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

class Solution {
    public List<List<Integer>> subsets(int[] nums) {
        List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Integer> current = new ArrayList<>();
        backtrack(nums, 0, current, result);
        return result;
    }

    private void backtrack(int[] nums, int index, List<Integer> current,
                            List<List<Integer>> result) {
        if (index == nums.length) {
            result.add(new ArrayList<>(current));
            return;
        }

        // Branch 1: exclude nums[index]
        backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);

        // Branch 2: include nums[index]
        current.add(nums[index]);
        backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);
        current.remove(current.size() - 1); // undo
    }
}

#include <vector>

class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<int>> subsets(vector<int>& nums) {
        vector<vector<int>> result;
        vector<int> current;
        backtrack(nums, 0, current, result);
        return result;
    }

private:
    void backtrack(vector<int>& nums, int index, vector<int>& current,
                    vector<vector<int>>& result) {
        if (index == (int)nums.size()) {
            result.push_back(current);
            return;
        }

        // Branch 1: exclude nums[index]
        backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);

        // Branch 2: include nums[index]
        current.push_back(nums[index]);
        backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);
        current.pop_back(); // undo
    }
};
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def subsets(self, nums: List[int]) -> List[List[int]]:
        result = []
        current = []
        self._backtrack(nums, 0, current, result)
        return result

    def _backtrack(self, nums, index, current, result):
        if index == len(nums):
            result.append(list(current))
            return

        # Branch 1: exclude nums[index]
        self._backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result)

        # Branch 2: include nums[index]
        current.append(nums[index])
        self._backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result)
        current.pop()  # undo
class Solution {
    subsets(nums: number[]): number[][] {
        const result: number[][] = [];
        const current: number[] = [];
        this.backtrack(nums, 0, current, result);
        return result;
    }

    private backtrack(nums: number[], index: number, current: number[],
                       result: number[][]): void {
        if (index === nums.length) {
            result.push([...current]);
            return;
        }

        // Branch 1: exclude nums[index]
        this.backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);

        // Branch 2: include nums[index]
        current.push(nums[index]);
        this.backtrack(nums, index + 1, current, result);
        current.pop(); // undo
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(n * 2^n) Space O(n) — 2^n subsets are produced, each costing O(n) to copy; recursion depth is n.

Walkthrough. nums = [1,2]. index=0: exclude branch recurses with index=1, current=[]; there, exclude branch reaches index=2 → record []; include branch pushes 2 → current=[2] → record [2], then pops 2. Back at index=0's include branch, push 1 → current=[1], recurse to index=1: exclude → record [1]; include 2 → current=[1,2] → record [1,2], pop 2, pop 1. Final result: [[], [2], [1], [1,2]].

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Backtracking explores the "include element i or not" decision tree via DFS over a single shared, mutable path. Every call records the current path as one complete subset (all partial paths are valid here, so nothing is pruned) and popping the last-added element after the recursive call returns restores the path so the next sibling branch starts clean — guaranteeing each of the 2^n combinations is generated exactly once.

When to reach for it. "Generate all combinations/permutations/subsets satisfying X" problems — the signal is needing every valid arrangement (not just one answer), combined with a natural choose/un-choose structure per element or index.

Complexity. O(2^n · n) time — 2^n subsets, each up to length n to build/record; O(n) auxiliary space for the recursion stack and current path (excluding the output storage itself).

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to pop (undo) after the recursive call corrupts every later sibling branch with leftover elements. Appending a reference to the mutable path instead of a copy makes every recorded subset alias the same, later-emptied list. Starting the inner loop at 0 instead of the current index produces permutations/duplicates instead of combinations.

Trace input: nums = [1,2,3] — generate every subset.

StepCall (start, path)ActionResult so far
0backtrack(0, [])record path[ [] ]
1backtrack(1, [1])chose i=0→1; record path[ [], [1] ]
2backtrack(2, [1,2])chose i=1→2; record path[ …, [1,2] ]
3backtrack(3, [1,2,3])chose i=2→3; record path; start=3=n, no choices; return; pop 3[ …, [1,2,3] ]
4back in backtrack(2,[1,2])loop exhausted; return; pop 2 → path=[1]unchanged
5backtrack(1,[1]) → backtrack(3,[1,3])chose i=2→3; record path[ …, [1,3] ]
6back in backtrack(1,[1])no more choices; return; pop 1 → path=[]unchanged
7backtrack(0,[]) → backtrack(2,[2])chose i=1→2; record path[ …, [2] ]
8backtrack(2,[2]) → backtrack(3,[2,3])chose i=2→3; record path[ …, [2,3] ]
9back in backtrack(2,[2])return; pop 3, pop 2 → path=[]unchanged
10backtrack(0,[]) → backtrack(3,[3])chose i=2→3; record path; return; pop 3; outer loop exhausted, done[ …, [3] ]

Result: Final result: [[],[1],[1,2],[1,2,3],[1,3],[2],[2,3],[3]] — all 8 = 23 subsets, each recorded exactly once at the instant its call begins, before any further choices are layered on.

Worked example — Course Schedule (LC 207, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Meta, Google, Bloomberg.

Problem. There are numCourses courses labeled 0..numCourses-1. prerequisites[i] = [a, b] means you must take course b before course a. Return true if it is possible to finish all courses (i.e. the prerequisite graph has no cycle).

Idea. Model prerequisites as a directed graph and compute a topological order with Kahn's algorithm: track in-degree (number of unmet prerequisites) per course, seed a queue with all zero-in-degree courses, and repeatedly remove a course, decrementing its neighbors' in-degrees, pushing any that hit zero. If every course gets processed, there is no cycle and the schedule is feasible; if some remain stuck with positive in-degree, a cycle blocks them.

import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Queue;

class Solution {
    public boolean canFinish(int numCourses, int[][] prerequisites) {
        List<List<Integer>> graph = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) {
            graph.add(new ArrayList<>());
        }

        int[] inDegree = new int[numCourses];
        for (int[] pre : prerequisites) {
            int course = pre[0];
            int prereq = pre[1];
            graph.get(prereq).add(course); // prereq -> course
            inDegree[course]++;
        }

        Queue<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) {
            if (inDegree[i] == 0) {
                queue.offer(i);
            }
        }

        int processed = 0;
        while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
            int course = queue.poll();
            processed++;
            for (int next : graph.get(course)) {
                inDegree[next]--;
                if (inDegree[next] == 0) {
                    queue.offer(next);
                }
            }
        }

        return processed == numCourses;
    }
}

#include <vector>
#include <queue>

class Solution {
public:
    bool canFinish(int numCourses, vector<vector<int>>& prerequisites) {
        vector<vector<int>> graph(numCourses);

        vector<int> inDegree(numCourses, 0);
        for (auto& pre : prerequisites) {
            int course = pre[0];
            int prereq = pre[1];
            graph[prereq].push_back(course); // prereq -> course
            inDegree[course]++;
        }

        queue<int> q;
        for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) {
            if (inDegree[i] == 0) {
                q.push(i);
            }
        }

        int processed = 0;
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int course = q.front();
            q.pop();
            processed++;
            for (int next : graph[course]) {
                inDegree[next]--;
                if (inDegree[next] == 0) {
                    q.push(next);
                }
            }
        }

        return processed == numCourses;
    }
};
from collections import deque
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def canFinish(self, numCourses: int, prerequisites: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        graph = [[] for _ in range(numCourses)]

        inDegree = [0] * numCourses
        for pre in prerequisites:
            course = pre[0]
            prereq = pre[1]
            graph[prereq].append(course)  # prereq -> course
            inDegree[course] += 1

        queue = deque()
        for i in range(numCourses):
            if inDegree[i] == 0:
                queue.append(i)

        processed = 0
        while queue:
            course = queue.popleft()
            processed += 1
            for next in graph[course]:
                inDegree[next] -= 1
                if inDegree[next] == 0:
                    queue.append(next)

        return processed == numCourses
class Solution {
    canFinish(numCourses: number, prerequisites: number[][]): boolean {
        const graph: number[][] = Array.from({ length: numCourses }, () => []);

        const inDegree = new Array(numCourses).fill(0);
        for (const pre of prerequisites) {
            const course = pre[0];
            const prereq = pre[1];
            graph[prereq].push(course); // prereq -> course
            inDegree[course]++;
        }

        const queue: number[] = [];
        for (let i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) {
            if (inDegree[i] === 0) {
                queue.push(i);
            }
        }

        let processed = 0;
        while (queue.length > 0) {
            const course = queue.shift()!;
            processed++;
            for (const next of graph[course]) {
                inDegree[next]--;
                if (inDegree[next] === 0) {
                    queue.push(next);
                }
            }
        }

        return processed === numCourses;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(V + E) Space O(V + E) — each course and each edge is visited exactly once during graph build and BFS.

Walkthrough. numCourses=4, prerequisites=[[1,0],[2,0],[3,1],[3,2]]. Graph: 0->{1,2}, 1->{3}, 2->{3}. inDegree=[0,1,1,2]. Queue starts with [0]. Pop 0 (processed=1): decrement 1→0 (enqueue), decrement 2→0 (enqueue). Pop 1 (processed=2): decrement 3→1. Pop 2 (processed=3): decrement 3→0 (enqueue). Pop 3 (processed=4). processed==4==numCourses → return true.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Kahn's algorithm repeatedly removes nodes whose in-degree has dropped to 0 — courses with no remaining unmet prerequisites. If every node can eventually be removed this way, a full topological order exists and the graph is acyclic, because any node stuck in a cycle would never have all its incoming edges consumed and would permanently sit at in-degree ≥ 1.

When to reach for it. Prerequisite / dependency / task-ordering problems — the signal is a directed graph plus the question "can everything finish" or "give a valid order," which is exactly cycle detection plus topological sort.

Complexity. O(V + E) time — each vertex is enqueued/dequeued once and each edge is relaxed once; O(V + E) space for the adjacency list, in-degree array, and queue.

Pitfalls & variations. DFS-based cycle detection with only a single "visited" set fails to distinguish a back edge (real cycle) from a cross edge (fine) — a 3-color unvisited/in-progress/done scheme is required instead. Disconnected components must each independently bottom out at in-degree 0. The real correctness check is comparing the total processed count to numCourses at the end, not just that the queue eventually emptied.

Trace input: numCourses = 5, prerequisites = [[1,0],[2,0],[3,1],[3,2],[4,3]] (edges 0→1, 0→2, 1→3, 2→3, 3→4) — can all 5 courses be finished?

StepPopped from queueProcessed so farIn-degree updatesQueue after
0init[]indegree = [0,1,1,2,1][0]
10[0]course1: 1→0 (push); course2: 1→0 (push)[1,2]
21[0,1]course3: 2→1 (not pushed)[2]
32[0,1,2]course3: 1→0 (push)[3]
43[0,1,2,3]course4: 1→0 (push)[4]
54[0,1,2,3,4]no outgoing edges[]

Result: processed.length == 5 == numCourses → true. The diamond dependency (0→{1,2}→3→4) resolves cleanly because every node reaches in-degree 0; a cycle anywhere would leave the queue empty early with processed.length < numCourses.

12 · Advanced Graphs

Tests weighted shortest-path and minimum-spanning-tree reasoning plus graph construction from implicit constraints; reach for it when edges carry weights or costs and you need shortest distance, minimum connecting cost, or a valid total order.

#ProblemDiffCompaniesSub-pattern
1 Reconstruct Itinerary Hard GoogleAmazon Eulerian path via Hierholzer's / lexicographic DFS
2 Min Cost to Connect All Points ★ Medium AmazonGoogleMeta MST via Prim's with min-heap
3 Network Delay Time ★ Medium GoogleAmazonUber Dijkstra's shortest path from a source
4 Swim in Rising Water Hard GoogleAmazon binary search + BFS, or Dijkstra on min-max path
5 Alien Dictionary ★ Hard MetaAirbnbGoogle build order graph from adjacent word pairs, topo sort
6 Cheapest Flights Within K Stops Medium AmazonGoogle Bellman-Ford relaxed for at most K edges

Worked example — Network Delay Time (LC 743, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Google, Amazon, Uber, Bloomberg.

Problem. Given n nodes labeled 1..n and directed weighted edges times[i] = [u, v, w] (signal travels u→v in w time), plus a source node k, return the time for all n nodes to receive a signal sent from k, or -1 if some node is unreachable.

Idea. This is exactly single-source shortest paths on a non-negative weighted directed graph, so run Dijkstra's algorithm: maintain a min-heap of (distance, node) pairs, always expand the currently-closest unvisited node, and relax its outgoing edges. The answer is the maximum finite distance across all nodes (the last node to receive the signal); if any node's distance is never finalized, return -1.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;

class Solution {
    public int networkDelayTime(int[][] times, int n, int k) {
        List<List<int[]>> graph = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
            graph.add(new ArrayList<>());
        }
        for (int[] t : times) {
            int u = t[0], v = t[1], w = t[2];
            graph.get(u).add(new int[]{v, w});
        }

        int[] dist = new int[n + 1];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        dist[k] = 0;

        // {distance, node}
        PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] - b[0]);
        pq.offer(new int[]{0, k});
        boolean[] visited = new boolean[n + 1];

        while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
            int[] curr = pq.poll();
            int d = curr[0], node = curr[1];
            if (visited[node]) continue;
            visited[node] = true;

            for (int[] edge : graph.get(node)) {
                int next = edge[0], weight = edge[1];
                if (!visited[next] && d + weight < dist[next]) {
                    dist[next] = d + weight;
                    pq.offer(new int[]{dist[next], next});
                }
            }
        }

        int maxDist = 0;
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
            if (dist[i] == Integer.MAX_VALUE) {
                return -1;
            }
            maxDist = Math.max(maxDist, dist[i]);
        }
        return maxDist;
    }
}

#include <vector>
#include <queue>
#include <climits>

class Solution {
public:
    int networkDelayTime(vector<vector<int>>& times, int n, int k) {
        vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> graph(n + 1);
        for (auto& t : times) {
            int u = t[0], v = t[1], w = t[2];
            graph[u].push_back({v, w});
        }

        vector<int> dist(n + 1, INT_MAX);
        dist[k] = 0;

        // {distance, node}
        priority_queue<pair<int,int>, vector<pair<int,int>>, greater<pair<int,int>>> pq;
        pq.push({0, k});
        vector<bool> visited(n + 1, false);

        while (!pq.empty()) {
            auto curr = pq.top(); pq.pop();
            int d = curr.first, node = curr.second;
            if (visited[node]) continue;
            visited[node] = true;

            for (auto& edge : graph[node]) {
                int next = edge.first, weight = edge.second;
                if (!visited[next] && d + weight < dist[next]) {
                    dist[next] = d + weight;
                    pq.push({dist[next], next});
                }
            }
        }

        int maxDist = 0;
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
            if (dist[i] == INT_MAX) {
                return -1;
            }
            maxDist = max(maxDist, dist[i]);
        }
        return maxDist;
    }
};
import heapq
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def networkDelayTime(self, times: List[List[int]], n: int, k: int) -> int:
        graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
        for t in times:
            u, v, w = t[0], t[1], t[2]
            graph[u].append((v, w))

        dist = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
        dist[k] = 0

        # (distance, node)
        pq = [(0, k)]
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)

        while pq:
            d, node = heapq.heappop(pq)
            if visited[node]:
                continue
            visited[node] = True

            for next, weight in graph[node]:
                if not visited[next] and d + weight < dist[next]:
                    dist[next] = d + weight
                    heapq.heappush(pq, (dist[next], next))

        maxDist = 0
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if dist[i] == float('inf'):
                return -1
            maxDist = max(maxDist, dist[i])
        return maxDist
class Solution {
    networkDelayTime(times: number[][], n: number, k: number): number {
        const graph: [number, number][][] = Array.from({ length: n + 1 }, () => []);
        for (const t of times) {
            const [u, v, w] = t;
            graph[u].push([v, w]);
        }

        const dist = new Array(n + 1).fill(Infinity);
        dist[k] = 0;

        // [distance, node]
        let pq: [number, number][] = [[0, k]];
        const visited = new Array(n + 1).fill(false);

        while (pq.length > 0) {
            pq.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0]);
            const [d, node] = pq.shift()!;
            if (visited[node]) continue;
            visited[node] = true;

            for (const [next, weight] of graph[node]) {
                if (!visited[next] && d + weight < dist[next]) {
                    dist[next] = d + weight;
                    pq.push([dist[next], next]);
                }
            }
        }

        let maxDist = 0;
        for (let i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
            if (dist[i] === Infinity) {
                return -1;
            }
            maxDist = Math.max(maxDist, dist[i]);
        }
        return maxDist;
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(E log V) Space O(V + E) — each edge triggers at most one heap insertion, and the heap holds up to E entries.

Walkthrough. times=[[2,1,1],[2,3,1],[3,4,1]], n=4, k=2. dist=[_,MAX,0,MAX,MAX]. Pop (0,2): relax 1→dist[1]=1, push (1,1); relax 3→dist[3]=1, push (1,3). Pop (1,1): node 1 has no outgoing edges. Pop (1,3): relax 4→dist[4]=2, push (2,4). Pop (2,4): no outgoing edges. Final dist=[_,1,0,1,2]; max = 2 → return 2.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. Dijkstra's greedily finalizes the closest remaining node first: once a node is popped from the min-heap holding the smallest tentative distance, no path discovered later — through any longer, still-unprocessed route — can ever produce something smaller, because all edge weights are non-negative. That guarantee is exactly what lets each popped distance be treated as final.

When to reach for it. Shortest path in a weighted graph with non-negative edges from a single (or few) source(s) — the signal is non-uniform weights (uniform weights ⇒ plain BFS suffices) and no negative edges (negative ⇒ need Bellman-Ford instead).

Complexity. O(E log V) time with a binary-heap priority queue (each relaxation may push once, heap operations are O(log V)); O(V + E) space for the adjacency list, distance array, and heap.

Pitfalls & variations. Not skipping stale heap entries for already-finalized nodes wastes work (harmless if guarded, wrong if it overwrites a final distance). Using BFS when weights aren't uniform gives incorrect shortest paths. Forgetting that some node may stay at distance ∞ (unreachable) — the correct answer is then -1, not the max of a still-infinite array.

Trace input: times = [[2,1,1],[2,3,1],[3,4,1]], n = 4 nodes, source k = 2 — time for a signal sent from node 2 to reach every node.

StepActionNode finalizeddist[1,2,3,4]Heap after
0init—[∞, 0, ∞, ∞][(0,2)]
1pop (0,2)node 2, dist=0[∞, 0, ∞, ∞][]
2relax 2→1 (w=1): 0+1=1 < ∞—[1, 0, ∞, ∞][(1,1)]
3relax 2→3 (w=1): 0+1=1 < ∞—[1, 0, 1, ∞][(1,1),(1,3)]
4pop (1,1)node 1, dist=1 (no outgoing edges)[1, 0, 1, ∞][(1,3)]
5pop (1,3)node 3, dist=1[1, 0, 1, ∞][]
6relax 3→4 (w=1): 1+1=2 < ∞—[1, 0, 1, 2][(2,4)]
7pop (2,4)node 4, dist=2 (no outgoing edges)[1, 0, 1, 2][]

Result: All 4 nodes finalized: dist = {1:1, 2:0, 3:1, 4:2}. Answer = max(dist) = 2, the time to reach the farthest node (4), via the chain 2→3→4.

13 · 1-D Dynamic Programming

Tests whether you can define a state that depends on a fixed number of previous states along a single index — the tell is "count the ways" or "min/max cost" over a sequence where brute force is exponential.

Worked example — Coin Change (LC 322, Medium)

Frequently asked at: Amazon, Google, Uber, Bloomberg.

Problem. Given an array coins of distinct denominations and an integer amount, return the fewest number of coins needed to make up amount. If it cannot be made, return -1. Each coin can be used unlimited times.

Idea. Let dp[a] = minimum coins to form amount a, with dp[0] = 0. For every amount a from 1 up to the target, try every coin c ≤ a and take dp[a] = min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1). This is unbounded knapsack: each coin denomination can be reused, so the inner loop simply scans all coins for every amount rather than tracking how many times a coin was used.

class Solution {
    public int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount) {
        int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];
        Arrays.fill(dp, amount + 1); // sentinel > any real answer
        dp[0] = 0;
        for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++) {
            for (int c : coins) {
                if (c <= a) {
                    dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
                }
            }
        }
        return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
    }
}

class Solution {
public:
    int coinChange(vector<int>& coins, int amount) {
        vector<int> dp(amount + 1, amount + 1); // sentinel > any real answer
        dp[0] = 0;
        for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++) {
            for (int c : coins) {
                if (c <= a) {
                    dp[a] = min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
                }
            }
        }
        return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
    }
};
class Solution:
    def coinChange(self, coins: List[int], amount: int) -> int:
        dp = [amount + 1] * (amount + 1)  # sentinel > any real answer
        dp[0] = 0
        for a in range(1, amount + 1):
            for c in coins:
                if c <= a:
                    dp[a] = min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1)
        return -1 if dp[amount] > amount else dp[amount]
class Solution {
    coinChange(coins: number[], amount: number): number {
        const dp = new Array(amount + 1).fill(amount + 1); // sentinel > any real answer
        dp[0] = 0;
        for (let a = 1; a <= amount; a++) {
            for (const c of coins) {
                if (c <= a) {
                    dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
                }
            }
        }
        return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
    }
}

Complexity. Time O(amount × coins.length) Space O(amount) — one dp array, nested loop over every amount and every coin.

Walkthrough. coins = [1, 2, 5], amount = 11. Filling dp[0..11] left to right:

a01234567891011
dp[a]011221223323

Each cell picks the best of "add a 1", "add a 2", or "add a 5" to a smaller already-solved amount — e.g. dp[10] = dp[5] + 1 = 2 (two 5s), and dp[11] = dp[10] + 1 = 3 (5 + 5 + 1). Final answer dp[11] = 3.

🔬 Deeper dive & dry run

Why it works. dp[i] = fewest coins to make amount i. The recurrence dp[i] = 1 + min(dp[i-c]) over every coin c ≤ i works because an optimal solution for i must end by placing some last coin c, and whatever remains (i-c) must itself be solved optimally — this is the DP optimal-substructure argument. Building bottom-up from dp[0]=0 guarantees every sub-answer is final before it's reused.

When to reach for it. Unbounded/unlimited supply of items, you need a min/max count or count-of-ways over a single integer axis (amount, length, steps), and brute force recursion on that axis has overlapping subproblems (visible as an exponential recursion tree with repeated arguments).

Complexity. Time O(amount × coins) — one inner loop over coins for each of the amount outer states. Space O(amount) for the 1-D dp array (coins array is O(1) extra).

Pitfalls & variations. Forgetting to initialize unreachable states to infinity (not 0) causes silently wrong mins; iterating amount in the outer loop and coins in the inner loop counts combinations correctly for min-coins, but if you instead want number of ways (LC 518) swapping loop order changes permutations vs combinations; off-by-one on whether dp[amount] stays infinite (no solution) must be checked and mapped to -1.

Trace input: coins = [1,3,4], amount = 6 — find the minimum number of coins that sum to 6.

ivia coin 1 → dp[i-1]+1via coin 3 → dp[i-3]+1via coin 4 → dp[i-4]+1dp[i]Note
0———0base case, 0 coins for amount 0
1dp[0]+1=1n/a (1<3)n/a (1<4)1only coin 1 fits
2dp[1]+1=2n/an/a2two 1's
3dp[2]+1=3dp[0]+1=1n/a1coin 3 alone beats three 1's
4dp[3]+1=2dp[1]+1=2dp[0]+1=11coin 4 alone wins
5dp[4]+1=2dp[2]+1=3dp[1]+1=221+4 or 1+1+3, tie at 2 coins
6dp[5]+1=3dp[3]+1=2dp[2]+1=32coin 3 path wins: 3+3

Result: dp[6] = 2 (using 3+3). The trace shows the minimum is recomputed fresh at each amount from three already-finalized smaller answers — no amount is ever revisited once computed.
